<span id="page-218" class="sec-page-anchor" aria-hidden="true"></span><h2 class="sec-h1">ROBERT YOUNG AND JUSTINIAN OFFLEY AT BROACH TO THE SURAT FACTORY, JANUARY 1, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 229).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Press for a further supply of money, and some gunny for packing; also for a list of goods to be provided and an invoice of those sent to them. Have heard nothing from Garrett. Eight days ago they forwarded him the Governor’s parwāna for clearing his goods and advised him to come away. The Governor of this town is asking for the gauntlet which was promised him at Surat. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received January 2.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1">THOMAS MILLS AND JOHN MILWARD AT PULICAT TO THE MASULIPATAM FACTORY, JANUARY 3, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Masulipatam</em>, vol. ix. p. 15).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Refer to their last, requesting the payment of 250 [<em>sic</em>] pagodas to one of their merchants. Hope the packing materials are being sent in the <em>Tartowle</em> [<em>see</em> p. 130], which is expected here every day. The Dutch have 100 bales ready to embark in her. The English have allowed them to take all the cloth ‘of the first makinge’, as they themselves have no means of packing goods, and moreover they think those now in hand will be better than the first lot. Would be glad to hear any news from Surat and England. (<em>Copy. ½ p.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1">ROBERT YOUNG AND JUSTINIAN OFFLEY AT BROACH TO THE SURAT FACTORY, JANUARY 5, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 235).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Money received. Note their instructions as to cotton yarn. Garrett arrived with his goods from Baroda on January 2, and was dispatched towards Surat two days later. He has been stopped at Ankleswar by a demand for a toll, but they have sent ‘Scander’ [<em>see</em> p. 8] to clear the goods, and Garrett will probably reach Jahāngīrpur to-morrow. The Governor set out yesterday for Māndū. It is understood that the Governor of Baroda is also <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-219" data-page="219" data-folio="180" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n218_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 180">[p. 180]</button> going to the Prince; and as he owes the Company over 1,000 mahmūdīs, they have sent Glanvill and Hawkridge to Baroda to get either the money or an order for its payment. Progress of their business. (<em>Copy.  <sup>3</sup>/<sub>4</sub> p. Received January</em> 6.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Henry Darrell, Thomas Barker, John Benthall, and Christopher Rosons, aboard the</span> <em>Whale</em> <span class="sc">in Gombroon Road, to the Surat Factory, January</span> 8, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 262).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Received their letter of December 2 by the <em>Blessing</em>, which arrived with the rest of that fleet on the 30th. Will reply in detail by Captain Hall’s squadron. They note the injunction not to land more goods than will pay their debts and meet needful charges, and they perceive from the Company’s letters overland ‘there resolucionn fully bentt to withdrawe this trade from this place, induced thereunto by the small benifitt thereby accurringe [accruing?], with the extreame charges, difficulties and inconvenyences in the procecucion thereof.’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-001" href="#sec-05-fn-001" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-001" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> But the commanders of the last fleet misled them by announcing their intention of sending them part of the spoils of Ormus, and then changing their minds. For the silk ordered in anticipation of this supply they are indebted to the King’s merchant at Ispahān about 4,000 tūmāns. To meet this liability they have landed the eleven chests of ‘larrees’ [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 227], equivalent to about 3,550 tūmāns [<em>see</em> p. 35 <em>n.</em>]; and for the balance, as rials could only be passed at a loss, have taken on shore such Indian commodities (sugar, sugar candy, ‘caffa-seede’ [coffee], and conserves) as they found in the fleet. These are all in great demand, the wars having much interfered with trade with India both by sea and land. Value of sample goods sent. Have ready for lading 820 bales of silk, forty-two bags of ‘ruhannaes’ [<em>see</em> p. 23], and twenty-one and a half pieces of camlets. Have sent the second of these commodities in the <em>Whale</em>, in order that it may have the benefit of the first market, ‘there beinge many merchaunts here expectinge passage in our shippes with great store.’ Cannot spare any factors, as they are but few and all more or less sick ‘through <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-220" data-page="220" data-folio="181" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n219_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 181">[p. 181]</button> the unholsomnes of this clymeatt’; but hope to send some back by the next ships. ‘The Persian this winter hath sentt downe greate forces to these marine partts under comand of Emaucly Beg [Imām Qulī Beg], generall of the last yeares warrs, to the nomber of 15 or 20,000 souldiers, and fortified the castell of Ormus in more stronge manner than ever itt was undere the Portingall, and redefied the castell of Kishme. Whatt there projectts are wee cannott yett conjecture; with them [whether?] they are determyned to goe uppon Muskatt, which soe longe as itt is in the Portingall tuition they esteeme themselves in noe small securitye [insecuritye?]. To the ef[fec]ting thereof they by there one reportte have in prepreacion fifteen frigatts and 400 boatts nott far distance from this place, thirty whereof came heather the same daye our shippes aryved; the rest to accompany the Chann of Shiraz, whoe uppon intelligence of our shippes cominge is reparinge heather and dayly expected.’ As the Persians will not dare to attempt such an enterprise without the aid of the English, they fear they will be involved, and are therefore hurrying as many goods as possible aboard the fleet. If forced to assist, they will take care to have better assurance of performance than they found in the case of Ormus. Will not omit to dispatch the ships by the time appointed. Send transcripts of letters, including one to the Company. (<em>Copy. 3½ pp. Received January 31.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Edward Heynes, John Parker, and William Hill at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory, January</span> 9, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 236).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have bought about 7,000 maunds of indigo at Sarkhej and 1,000 more at Ahmadābād. Hope to dispatch some of their goods within ten days, and the rest shortly after. Request an early remittance, ‘this place affordinge nott mony to supply us, by reasonn the Prince hath commanded his cassanna [Hind. <em>khazāna</em>, ‘treasure’] to bee transported to Mando by the shraffes of this place, which hath this many dayes caused a greatte scarsitye of mony’. If bills can be cashed at a reasonable rate, they will draw on Surat for at least Rs. 10,000; but they are hindered by the fact that previous bills were not punctually paid. Have sold the gold chains. (<em>Copy. 1½ pp. Received January 14.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-221"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="221" data-folio="182" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n220_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 182">[p. 182]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Thomas Mills at Pulicat to the Masulipatam Factory</span>, <span class="sc">January</span> 9, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Masulipatam</em>, vol. ix. p. 16).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Packing materials received per the <em>Tortola</em> on the 5th, with their letter of December 28. Perceives that the new Dutch Governor has demanded from the Masulipatam factors the payment of half the value of gifts presented to certain great men on their visiting the Fort here; this is unreasonable and should be resisted. Is surprised to hear that an agreement has been made concerning the English house at Pulicat, as the matter had been referred to Batavia. Has found a friend who will assist them to two months’ payment. As yet the Hollanders have received all the cloth and the English none; but the latter are confident of being provided as soon as is necessary. Wonders that nothing has been heard from Surat. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Thomas Rastell, Giles James, and Nicholas Bangham at Surat to the Masulipatam Factory</span>, <span class="sc">January</span> 9, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 18).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Acknowledge receipt of their letters of October 26 and November 7 and 18, and explain why they did not reply earlier. Have sent to Batavia goods to the amount of 97,615 mahmūdīs 23⅝, besides six chests of rials and 83,525 mahmūdīs 27¼ in other goods fitting for Sumatra. The ship sailed on December 12, and goes first to Batavia, and possibly afterwards to Achin. Wonder the Dutch do not buy pepper at Surat. Recently procured 9,000 maunds at 16½ mahmūdīs and under, and are now bargaining for 3,000 maunds more. May possibly send them some coral as desired, but as their present stock ‘will shortlie be all the estate we shall have restinge in India’, to export any after having paid the customs ‘will but ad more jealousie to our allreadie suspected intentions’. Cannot supply them with money, owing to the smallness of the amount received from England this year. Had it not been for some 45,000 rials returned from Mokha, and some 17,000<em>l.</em> in spoils from Ormus, they could have done little more than pay the debts in Persia. They have now to provide a venture of 10,000<em>l.</em> to Mokha, discharge their debts, revictual the fleets, and prepare returns for England. Cannot therefore <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-222" data-page="222" data-folio="183" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n221_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 183">[p. 183]</button> send any money or accept any bills drawn on them from Masulipatam. Van den Broeck has received at least 30,000<em>l.</em> in money and goods from Batavia; and had they any cash to spare, he is not likely to be willing to give bills on Masulipatam as suggested. Indignantly deny the assertions of the Dutch regarding the cowardly behaviour of the English at Mozambique; the want of complete success was largely due to the action of the former in sending away two of their ships to the Red Sea, and they themselves applaud the ‘verie valiant stout performance’ of Mr. Goodall in the <em>Anne</em>. The Fleet of Defence sailed from hence on November 23, and is ordered to remain before Goa till March or April and then to be reinforced and to proceed again to Mozambique, this time under the command of an Englishman, to be appointed by the President and Council of Surat. Success of the English at Ormus; but the Persians ‘not onelie defeted them of there shares in the spoiles but of residence allsoe in the castell; yet soe as with hopes dependinge on answeare and approbation from the Kinge’, whose decision they expect to learn on the return of the fleet. The greatest part of the wealth was conveyed away in frigates before the town was surprised, and part was reserved by the Persians after the surrender of the castle, though the latter success was mainly due to the English. Only twenty English lives lost, but a thousand Persians. ‘The Company have in a manner dissolved that Persian trade, forbidinge all further supplie thereunto till better conditions be grannted.’ The enclosed abstract [<em>not extant</em>] will show what returns are intended for England this year. (<em>Copy.</em> 2 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Robert Young and Justinian Offley at Broach to the Surat Factory, January 11, 1623</span> (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 235).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Samples of cotton yarn sent. The merchants will not bring the yarn to their house, and so they are forced to purchase in the bāzār. Beg that the coach belonging to this factory may be returned. Glanvill and Hawkridge have returned from Baroda, bringing the Governor’s ‘barratt’ [<em>see</em> p. 67] for his debt. Are promised early payment of the money due from Himmat Khān. Will buy no more narrow baftas until further order. (<em>Copy. ¾ p. Received January 13.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-223"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="223" data-folio="184" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n222_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 184">[p. 184]</button></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Joseph Salbank and Nicholas Crispe at Cambay to the Surat Factory, January 13, 1623</span> (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 238).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have received their instructions to lose no more time in waiting for Jī Rām Shāh’s performance of his promises, but to make up the required quantities by purchase in the market, and to dispatch the goods in time to be at Surat by the end of February. Will do their best, but fear they will not be able to get all they want. If there is any chance of procuring these later, Crispe will stay behind for that purpose. They see no likelihood of the Governor detaining them, as he appears to be very friendly. The total value of the piece-goods delivered by Jī Rām Shāh is under Rs. 20,000. ‘Herra’ [Hīrā] has supplied part of his contract, and they hope to receive the rest shortly. Bills received and cashed, except one, for which speedy payment is promised. ‘Accordinge to your order wee will continewe possession of our house for the nextt yeare to preventt the Dutch the posse[ss]inge ytt’. (<em>Copy. 1¼ pp. Received January 15.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same, January 13, 1623</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 239).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Explain an apparent excess in the amount of money spent. Will have little left when everything is paid for, but if desired will remit the remainder by exchange to Surat. (<em>Copy. ¾ pp. Received January 15.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Matthew Duke, Francis Futter, and John Dod at Masulipatam to the Pulicat Factory, January 15, 1623</span> (<em>Factory Records, Masulipatam</em>, vol. ix. p. 15).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Think the 250 pagodas mentioned in theirs of the 3rd must be a mistake for 150; if not, and the Pulicat factors have drawn upon them a second time, they will refuse to pay. They have already disbursed the 150 pagodas, and have promised to satisfy the Fort and garrison charges; more than this they will not undertake. Dispatched the packing materials by the <em>Tartoll</em>, which sailed December 31. No further news from Surat. Wrote thither on January 1. Are bare of money and means, but trust a supply will arrive soon. (<em>Copy. ½ p.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-224"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="224" data-folio="185" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n223_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 185">[p. 185]</button></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Robert Young and Justinian Offley at Broach to the Surat Factory, January</span> 19, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 253).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Gunny received. Piece-goods are coming in fast. Have about twelve packs of cotton yarn already, but the price tends to rise, and they think it would be well to buy more in Surat and neighbourhood. ‘This place affourds us ten or twelve maunds a daye, which quantety wee thincke you maye have all the yeare longe.’ Purchase of broad and narrow baftas. Hope by the end of this month to have all the goods for the Red Sea ready, if not everything packed, to accompany the Cambay goods when they come down. Recovery of money due from the Governors of Baroda and Broach. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p. Received January</em> 21.)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Edward Heynes and John Parker at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory, January</span> 22, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 259).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Bills received and accepted. Explain an omission in their former letter. Have closed their investments at Dholka. Yesterday dispatched their first caravan, consisting of 671 bales of indigo and piece-goods. The rest will follow in fifteen days. The indigo sent weighs 4,784 maunds, and they hope to make this up to 8,000. Will need at least Rs. 35,500 more. ‘Mamootte Tuckey’ [Mahmūd Taqī] has importuned them to buy the Dholka indigo, and on their refusal has begged them to refer the question to Surat. The Dutch have bought 500 churls and are now bargaining for 1,000 more. Mr. Heynes will return to Surat after the dispatch of the second caravan. (<em>Copy.</em> 1¾ <em>pp. Received January</em> 25.)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Matthew Duke, Francis Futter, and John Dod at Masulipatam to the Surat Factory, January</span> 24, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 286).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Enclose a transcript of their last letter. A ship from Pegu brought certain merchants belonging to Surat, to whom they have given bills of exchange on that factory for 350 rials of eight, in satisfaction of 210 pagodas paid here. Beg that the bills may be paid and charged to the Batavia factory. The Dutch ship <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-225" data-page="225" data-folio="186" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n224_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 186">[p. 186]</button> expected from Bengal has arrived and is now to be laden for Holland. Hope for a reply by the bearer. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received February 20.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Thomas Barker and John Benthall at Gombroon to the Surat Factory, January 24, 1623</span> (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 298).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have now laden on the <em>Blessing</em> and <em>Reformation</em> the remainder of their silk (570 bales), and received from them broadcloth, powdered sugar and sugar candy, besides ‘larrees and abashees’ [the Persian <em>abbāsī</em>, worth about 16<em>d.</em>] to the value of 320,703½ lārīs. The silk has been packed with greater care than the last consignment. Will also in future be diligent in checking the money received. Request a supply of dutties, cotton, &amp;c., for packing purposes. Repudiate charges of disorderliness. The freight on passengers’ goods from Surat came to 3,321 lārīs and 868 rials of eight. Some merchants have embarked on these ships with rūnās and similar commodities, from whom they have received 5,217 lārīs for freight. Will answer the Company’s letter after their arrival at Ispahān or after the delivery to the Shāh of the royal letter received by Captain Hall’s fleet. Request that they will forward to the Company the Agent’s letter, the note of articles desired by the Shāh, and ‘the previledges and grantts wee have procured’, copies of which are sent herewith, together with the Shāh’s reply<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-002" href="#sec-05-fn-002" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-002" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> to the previous letter from King James, and certain other papers. Mr. Suffield, purser of the <em>Blessing</em>, has received 2,300 lārīs for ships’ provisions. ‘Emancoly Cane [Imām Qulī Khān] the 11th of this month made his entrance into Comroone with a greatte lasker.’ He and the other Persian leaders have treated the English very well, though they expressed surprise at the sudden departure of the <em>Whale</em> and <em>Dolphin</em>. They have urged that the ships now here should accompany the Khān to Muskat; but the factors have excused themselves on the ground of want of sufficient authority. The Persians seem discontented at this. The English on their part have not been sparing in pointing out the bad faith shown to them last year and the small benefit they received for <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-226" data-page="226" data-folio="187" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n225_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 187">[p. 187]</button> the services then rendered, and have hinted that the Persians have only themselves to blame if overtures for further enterprises are coldly received. The two Englishmen, by name Thomas Ely and Peter Tyler, left by Captain Blyth in Kishm Castle, are still there; request instructions as to their withdrawal. Henry Darrell has died since the departure of the last ships. Have not had time to examine his accounts. Request that any property of his in the ships or at Surat may be looked after. (<em>Copy.</em> 3 <em>pp. Received March 23.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">John Wing</span> [aboard the <em>Rose</em> at Swally] to the President at Surat [<span class="sc">January 25</span>?] 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 261).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Received no letters from the fleet other than Mr. Slade’s and that of the Dutch. Capt. Green delivered his to his purser. Has nearly thirty tuns of empty cask to be newly trimmed. <em>P.S.</em>—Thanks him for the refreshments sent. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p. Received January 26.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Robert Young and Justinian Offley at Broach to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">January</span> 25, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 261).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Money urgently needed. Could now buy two packs of cotton yarn a day. Have forwarded the ‘barratt’ to their broker at Baroda, but have not heard from him since. Request that Garrett or some other be sent to assist them, as they are very busy. (<em>Copy. </em> ½ <em>p. Received January 27.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Edward Heynes, John Parker, and William Hill at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">January</span> 26, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 266).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Forward an invoice for the goods sent in their first caravan. Have duly advised the factors at Broach. Would be glad to have instructions about the Dholka indigo. The price is reasonable, and they think the bargain would be a good one, especially if they made the Governor take coral in part payment. The Dutch have finished their investment (having bought 450 churls), and he is not likely to find any other customer. Their second caravan is nearly ready, ‘butt the adowias detracteth tyme with us untill the Princes goodes for Mocha bee speeded.’ ‘The newes is heere <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-227" data-page="227" data-folio="188" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n226_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 188">[p. 188]</button> thatt the Princes cassanna is returned againe to Amadavaz: thatt order is given for the speedey sendinge of warlicke munition to the Princes court: thatt Mamoott Hussen [Mahmūd Husain], in behalfe of the Prynce, is sentt Governor to Cambaya: a generall insurrection is to bee [be]gonne against the Kinge in dyvers places: and thatt the Prince will nott bee longe hence.’ Request an early remittance. The exchange is at present a little under Rs. 41 per hundred mahmūdīs. (<em>Copy.</em> 1½ <em>pp. Received January</em> 31.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">JOHN WING [ABOARD THE <em>ROSE</em> AT SWALLY] TO THE PRESIDENT AT SURAT, JANUARY 27, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 240).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Requests instructions for taking in provisions, &amp;c. One of his men hurt by another in a brawl ashore. Thinks the purser should not let the sailors have money without his consent. Is taking in water, but the casks are in very bad condition. Dutties wanted for topsails and bonnets. (<em>Copy.</em> ¾ <em>p. Received January 28.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">THE SAME TO THE SAME, JANUARY 30, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 262).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The ship will be ready in two or three days. Has punished some of the men for straggling, and the rest are behaving better. The man who was hurt is recovering, and has been reconciled to his antagonist. As to the supply of water, and of dutties for sails. Has sent up his gunner; and when he returns will let his mate, Luke Browne, repair to Surat. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">NICHOLAS WOODCOCK [ABOARD THE <em>WHALE</em>] IN SURAT ROAD TO THE PRESIDENT AT SURAT, JANUARY 30, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 262).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Arrived this morning, in company with the <em>Dolphin</em>. They reached Gombroon on December 30, and remained there till January 9. Took on board the <em>Whale</em> 150 bales of silk; the <em>Dolphin</em> has 100, and another 100 were put into the <em>Blessing</em>. The factors expect to dispatch 800 bales in all this year. Refers to their letters for news. Urges that his Red Sea lading may be sent down as soon as possible. The Persians are ready with an <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-228" data-page="228" data-folio="189" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n227_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 189">[p. 189]</button> army and an ‘armado of frygatts’ to attempt Muskat. Thinks they will expect English aid; but the Persian General said nothing of this to him. Both the General and the merchants are disappointed with the small quantity brought by the English. Would be glad to know whether he is to take in goods at Swally or the Bar of Surat. Has a present for him from Mr. Darrell. (<em>Copy. ¾ p. Received January 31.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Robert Young and Justinian Offley at Broach to the Surat Factory, February</span> 3, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 269).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The authorities at Ankleswar will take no notice of the parwāna of the Governor of Surat, but demand a toll of five mahmūdīs on each cart. Return it herewith and request ‘one of more force, or order to give them there demands.’ Complain that they should be thus abused, ‘where the Dutch passeth withoutt payinge any dutye.’ Salbank arrived on the first with the goods from Cambay, and the caravan from Ahmadābād came next day, in company with the Prince’s goods. The great quantity of the latter retards the clearing of their own commodities, for ‘the Prince will bee fyrst sarved’. Will send their invoice shortly, and hope to dispeed the piece-goods provided by them within five or six days; in the meantime they have forwarded 78 packs by Thomas Aldworth, who is now at Ankleswar, awaiting their second parwāna. Have received from Ahmadābād nine fardles of ‘serryaes’ and dutties to be bleached here. (<em>Copy. ¾ p. Received February 4.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Edward Heynes at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory, February</span> 3, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 278).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Bills cashed, and others accepted. Their second caravan will start in two days, carrying 450 bales of indigo, guarded by fifty-five peons. Mr. Parker’s ‘dangerous sicknes’ prevents him from accompanying it; and Heynes thinks it inadvisable to leave while his colleague is in such a state. He will, however, start for Surat as soon as possible, leaving Mr. Parker chief, and Mr. Hill second. Invoices, &amp;c., will be sent shortly. Requests a reply regarding Mahmūd Taqī’s indigo. (<em>Copy. ¾ p. Received February 7.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-229"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="229" data-folio="190" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n228_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 190">[p. 190]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Nicholas Woodcock at Swally to the President at Surat, February</span> 4, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 271).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has sent up the wine, and landed the ‘ruenas’ [<em>see</em> p. 23], but nobody has yet come to receive it or to rebale the silk. Requests instructions as to his lading. Suggests the transfer of some tar to the <em>Rose</em> for the Fleet of Defence. (<em>Copy. ¼ p. Received February 5.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same, February</span> 5, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 270).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Forwards some books from Persia. Reiterates his request for instructions. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Edward Heynes, John Parker, and William Hill at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory, February</span> 5, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 270).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Refer to previous letters. Will delay the business about the Dholka indigo, as desired. ‘Yf Mellicke Ambers performance with you depend one the freindshipp and coule [<em>see</em> p. 139 <em>n.</em>] of the Dutch, then may wee thincke the finishing of thatt busines is assuredly doubtfull, for soe farr as wee may collect by conference with them they have noe resolucionn to bee at peace with any, yf voylence and theevinge may produce them profitt.’ The Dutch indigo was stopped at the moment of departure, because they had failed to pay ready money for it; but after much wrangling, and on their giving better security, it was allowed to proceed with the English caravan. There is no chance of selling the jewels at this place, ‘the tymes beinge daingerous, and most men att there witts end howe to preserve themselves in favor with the Kinge and Prince in there presentt differences, which makes there estattes howerlye uncertaine.’ Propose therefore that Mr. Heynes shall bring them down to Surat in time for them to be sent to court (if this be determined upon). He will await their decision; but they should ‘consider of the daunger one the wayes att present, which increaseth dayly; difficultt to passe withoutt violence by theeves and rebbells.’ Will send an invoice of the second caravan, which has been dispatched to ‘Battuwaye’ [<em>see</em> p. 10]. <em>P.S.</em>—Return a letter intended for Mr. Salbank, who quitted Cambay some time ago. (<em>Copy. 1¾ pp. Received February 9.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-230"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="230" data-folio="191" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n229_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 191">[p. 191]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Robert Young and Justinian Offley at Broach to the Surat Factory, February</span> 5, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 277).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Prince’s goods have passed the river, but theirs are still waiting for the dispute as to the customs to be settled. Send their invoice, and request instructions for the dispatch of their cotton yarn. Will keep the broad baftas for the present. A further remittance should be sent at once. The customs on their goods and those from Ahmadābād, &amp;c., will exceed 2,000 mahmūdīs, and the Customer refuses to deduct that amount from the Governor’s debt. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received February 7.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Robert Hutchinson, aboard the</span> <em>WHALE</em>, <span class="sc">to the President at Surat, February</span> 7, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 277).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The silk has been got out of the hold, ready for the packers, and the ‘roannas’ has been landed. Neither the packers nor the gunny yet arrived. Requests a tent; only an old sail at present available. The master also desires a couple of country boats to assist him. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">John Wing [at Swally] to the Same, February</span> 7, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 279).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has taken in the provisions of the Dutch, as ordered, but cannot find room for the three oxen. Mr. Cartwright has a large quantity of provisions to come on board. Denies the accusations of waste. The potatoes were very small and poor. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Nicholas Woodcock at Swally to the Same, February</span> 7, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 279).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Will be ready to take in goods for England as soon as they are sent down. Requires mats for the pepper. Mr. Hutchinson has arrived, but not the packers. Requests orders as to the goods on board intended for Mokha, &amp;c. Does his best to prevent the men from straggling. Requests one or two country boats to help in lading, as the surf grows dangerous with a westerly wind, and their own boats are ‘very pretious’. Also needs a tent and a horse. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-231"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="231" data-folio="192" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n230_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 192">[p. 192]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Ralph Cartwright at Swally to the Same, February</span> 8, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 279).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">According to order has procured the necessary provisions for the Fleet of Defence. Suggests that a country boat be obtained to help to transport them to Goa. Imagines the Dutch would pay half the expense. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p. Received February</em> 9.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Edward Heynes, John Parker, and William Hill at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory, February</span> 8, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 281).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Their second caravan was dispatched on the 5th. An invoice of the goods is enclosed, and a statement of charges will appear in their accounts. A letter to the Broach factors was delivered to the ‘adowiaes’ in charge of the caravan. Will draw upon Surat shortly for Rs. 10,000, but would be glad of a further remittance in the meantime, as their creditors at Sarkhej are importunate for payment. Have already realized the bills for Rs. 10,000 last sent. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p. Received February</em> 12.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Thomas Mills and John Milward at Pulicat to the Masulipatam Factory, February</span> 8, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Masulipatam</em>, vol. ix. p. 19).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Received theirs of January 27 on February 1. Regret to learn that they have yielded to the Dutch demand for the payment of a moiety of their gifts to natives. Think this should not have been done without referring to Pulicat. The articles of agreement refer plainly to gifts made with the consent of both nations, which was not the case in this instance; and it was on that ground that they previously refused to pay. Van Uffelen’s threats should have been disregarded. The rent for the house is not unreasonable, but they had referred the question to Batavia, and moreover have spent nearly a year’s rent on it.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-003" href="#sec-05-fn-003" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-003" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Wish Van Uffelen would carry out his threat to seize their cloth, as this would bring matters to a head. They could do much better if they removed from Pulicat.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-232"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="232" data-folio="193" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n231_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 193">[p. 193]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">‘Wee have had sundry letters to those purposes from the greateste man in this quarter, who so kindlie invittes us to seat our selves within his authorytie, with his firman that wee shall live uppon what conditions wee shall desire and to our harts contente, and wee weell knowe the place will afford all manner of our desired investment and divers othere sorts which wee ar here furnished.’ Have written to the President on the subject. Nothing should be said about it to the Dutch. Progress of the investment. Send as requested a copy of the articles of agreement. Inquire for news from Surat. (<em>Copy.</em> 1¼ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">President Fursland and Council at Batavia to the Company, February</span> 9, 1623 (<em>O.C.</em> 1099).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Troubles with the Dutch. Accounts of the subordinate factories. A chest of silver sent to Pulicat in the <em>Unity</em> was found to be short by 104 rials; these must have been stolen, but they have failed to discover the thief. Two doubtful items in Methwold’s accounts from Masulipatam. The one—a deficiency in some brassware— appears to be due to an error in bookkeeping. The other relates to 200 pagodas left by Methwold in the hands of a native at the time of his visit to the diamond mines. This money was stolen from the native as he was returning, and was never recovered. Duke now avers that the amount was really Methwold’s private venture, and that the Company is wrongly charged with its loss. They give no credence to the story, which they set down to Duke’s malice, but think it right to inform the Company and leave them to decide the matter. ‘All other particulars in his accompts wee find perfect, and find himself to be a man of such sufficiencie for imploiement in your affaires that wee hartily wish your displeasure were not the cause of his not returne; whose absence from the charge that hee was imploied in will cause your prejudice in thatt affaire, besides other inconveniences which may happen for want of such an understanding and experienced marchant.’ Goods laden for England in the <em>Palsgrave</em>, and passengers sent in her. ‘Mr. William Methwold having been an earnest suiter unto us that hee maie go for England to answeare unto the informations which have been given Your Worshipps against him, and to cleere himself of all imputations, his request being reasonable, and our perswasions <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-233" data-page="233" data-folio="194" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n232_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 194">[p. 194]</button> not prevailable to stay him, wee have granted his returne in this shipp.’ Account of others sent home at the same time. Affairs in Japan. Proceedings of the Fleet of Defence sent to the Malabar Coast. Death of Fitzherbert and 150 of his men. Destruction of a Portuguese squadron at Mozambique. They recovered 68,553 rials and some provisions from the Portuguese vice-admiral. Had the Dutch kept their fleet intact, all the enemy’s ships would have been taken. The fleet is now before Goa, but will return to this place after the end of March. The <em>Discovery</em> has brought from Surat a good quantity of piece-goods for Achin and Batavia, with six chests of rials from Surat and 30,000 rials from the Goa fleet. Regret that she did not go to Achin on her way hither. The <em>Discovery</em> is very badly built and cannot last long. The capture and sacking of Ormus proves less profitable than was expected. When the <em>Discovery</em> left Surat (December 10), the <em>London</em>, <em>Jonas</em>, and <em>Lion</em> were ready to sail for England. Affairs in Siam, Patani, and other factories. ‘At Pallicatt the factors complaine (like as in all other places where wee are under the Dutches authority) how in what they can they exact there also uppon us, but there they can nott in anie great matter worke uppon us as they doe in other places. The last yeeres charge for your part amounts to 4,321½ rials, which raises the cloth at least 30 per 100 in that small capitall which wee have imploied; and much more it wilbe this yeare, not having above one third part of the capitall wee had last yeere. If before the sending of a shipp for that place there come not further order from Your Worshipps for the continuance of that busines, wee intend to ease you of that charge by dissolving that factory; for so long as wee discontinue the trade in Molluccos, etc., we shall have little need of those sorts of clothing which that place affords.’ Need of provisions and stores. Present employment of various ships. Further conferences with the Dutch about Bantam. Factors wanted. The seamen disorderly. Orders required as to victualling. (<em>Copy. 15¾ pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">James Slade, Master of the</span> <em>Discovery</em>, <span class="sc">at Batavia to the Company, February</span> 10, 1623 (<span class="sc">O.C.</span> 1100 i).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wrote from Surat by the <em>London</em> and <em>Jonas</em>. Sailed from that port on December 14, with goods and treasure for Batavia. Arrived <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-234" data-page="234" data-folio="195" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n233_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 195">[p. 195]</button> off Goa on December 22, and received from Captain Green 30,000 rials of eight, 22 slaves, and some sandalwood. With these he sailed again on December 28, passed Cape ‘Commeryne’ on January 9 and Ceylon four days later, and reached his destination on February 4. News from Batavia. Unsatisfactory condition of his ship. (<em>Holograph.</em> 2 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Robert Hutchinson at Swally to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">February</span> 10, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 280).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The packers arrived on the 8th; also the carts, with the gunny and ropes. They are now busy at work. The tent is very useful. The ‘Decan horse’ appointed for Captain Woodcock has not yet come. Will observe the instructions to prevent the shipping of private men’s goods, but fears there will be some squabbling. Bales taken out of the <em>Rose</em>. Advises that the pursers be ordered to keep aboard while the ships are lading and send others to buy their provisions. (<em>Copy.</em> ¾ <em>p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">JOSEPH SALBANK AT JAHĀNGĪRPUR TO THE PRESIDENT AT SURAT, FEBRUARY 10, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 281).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has reached this place with the goods from Cambay, and must now wait till they arrange for their clearance; then he will take them down to Swally. Begs that Mr. Crispe may be sent with money to pay the carters and peons. Has a bad foot, and would be glad if the surgeon would send some salve. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received February 11.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">JOHN WILLOUGHBY [AT AGRA] TO THE SURAT FACTORY [ABOUT FEBRUARY 10, 1623<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-004" href="#sec-05-fn-004" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-004" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>] (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 290).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has received their letter, by which he finds that they intend to put on him the sole responsibility for the bill for Rs. 4,300 he gave to ‘Mamood Mafuse’ [Mahmūd Mahfūz]. ‘Yf I had knowne you to bee in this cuntry the dogge should have as soone had my lyfe as my bill.’ However, he will give them reason for <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-235" data-page="235" data-folio="196" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n234_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 196">[p. 196]</button> his proceedings. He understands from the messenger that they sent a bill of exchange for Rs. 1,500 to Jādū<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-005" href="#sec-05-fn-005" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-005" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, though they do not mention this in their letter. Jādū has made none of it over to him, though he was forced to come back from Samāna to Agra for want of money to proceed another way. Doubtless Jādū thought he had gone overland, and that, Mr. Hughes being dead, the money would remain in his own hands. ‘This falce dogge’ has got hold of ‘Coja Ameas’ [Khwāja Amīr’s] bill, and has also extracted money from the ‘Bloches’ [Balūchīs] on behalf of the English. As soon as proper authority comes from Surat, Willoughby will call him to account for the goods and papers committed to his charge by Mr. Hughes. He at present avoids Willoughby, fearing that the latter will make him prisoner for the remainder due from ‘Greewe’ [Guru]. Notes their order that he is to come to Surat; but since they are so straitlaced about the bill, he intends to remain here until he receives an answer to the present letter. As the debt ‘belongeth wholy to Jadowe’, Willoughby hopes to receive an order under their hand and seal ‘as well in English as Mosselemana<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-006" href="#sec-05-fn-006" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-006" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>’, and then he will ‘teach him howe to goe to plowe with dogges’. Requests that full particulars of debts, &amp;c., should be forwarded for this purpose; also the agreement for their house. If they send any Englishmen hither (as he hears they intend) to deal with this business, Willoughby protests he will have nothing to do with them or with Jādū. Has only Rs. 30 at present, and begs therefore that they will remit him money for his expenses and for the bill of Mahmūd Mahfūz. ‘The busines of Jadowe’ may be worth Rs. 10,000 or Rs. 12,000, and besides ‘there resteth in the hands of Calfa [the Khalīfa: <em>see</em> p. 11] 2,200 rup[ees] of the busines of Sendeman’ [the Sindi-men: <em>ibid.</em>]. Further, as his period of service has now expired, he purposes to go home this year. In spite of the pains he has taken, he has never obtained promotion. ‘Alwayes boyes have bine preferred before mee, homarea comboch [Hind. <em>hamārā kambakht</em>, ‘our ill-luck’]. You for your partts loose nothinge, butt our masters have payd soundly for there learninge. Att present the newes heere is badd. The Prince is come to Fittapoore [Fatehpur Sīkri], and have sentt his <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-236" data-page="236" data-folio="197" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n235_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 197">[p. 197]</button> forces to Agra, 16,000 horse, and heere they have made a greatte spoyle; soe thatt daye and nightt here is wattch and ward, and nott a man dare stur fourth of his house.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-007" href="#sec-05-fn-007" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-007" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> The Kinge sertainly will come for Agra, and Asuffchan is committed prisnor into the handes of Lollo Bronssinge<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-008" href="#sec-05-fn-008" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-008" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>.’ As the ways are consequently dangerous, no goods should yet be ventured to this place. The bearers have agreed to go and return within twenty-eight days; so they should not be delayed at Surat. Would be glad of news from England and of any letters that may have come for him. Two Dutchmen were brought prisoners from Cambay to this place ‘for the debt of Asuffchan for 13,000 rup[ees], butt the Captaine heere is bound for them; soe thatt att presentt [they are] att liberty.’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-009" href="#sec-05-fn-009" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-009" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> Jādū has bought a house for Rs. 1,200. Awaits their orders. (<em>Copy. 2 pp. Received March</em> 1.)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Nicholas Woodcock at Swally to the President at Surat, February</span> 11, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 281).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Will take care that no private man’s goods be taken in to the injury of the Company. Has sent a cooper, but cannot find an instrument-mender in either of the ships. <em>P.S.</em>—Would be glad of some cotton. (<em>Copy. ½ p.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Matthew Duke at Masulipatam to President Rastell at Surat, February</span> 12, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 293).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has received his letter of January 9, and was glad to hear of the prosperous state of affairs at Surat. Repeats the advice of bills drawn on that factory for 350 rials of eight, and trusts they will be duly honoured. (<em>Copy. ¼ p. Received March 6.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-237"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="237" data-folio="198" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n236_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 198">[p. 198]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Captain John Hall, aboard the</span> <em>Blessing</em> [<span class="sc">at Swally</span>], <span class="sc">to the President at Surat, February</span> 12, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 282).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Announces his arrival with the <em>Blessing</em> and <em>Reformation</em>. The <em>Primrose</em> was sold in Persia for 400 tūmāns.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-010" href="#sec-05-fn-010" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-010" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Set sail from Gombroon on January 26. Mr. Darrell is dead. Requests orders for fitting his ship. <em>P.S.</em>—All their silk is in the <em>Blessing</em>. They have made 40,000 ‘larrees’ by freight, besides 600 more paid at Gombroon. (<em>Copy. ¼ p. Received February</em> 13.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Captain Matthew Wills at Swally to the President at Surat, February</span> 12, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 283).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">All the goods at Swally have now been taken on board. Some were in bad condition, ‘as did appeare where the surplis was toren.’ Has also received the goods from the <em>Whale</em>, and now awaits the rest from Jahāngīrpur. Sent him a ‘barecoe’ of beer by Mr. Hutchinson. The bearer is the instrument-mender he requires. <em>P.S.</em>— The <em>Blessing</em> and <em>Reformation</em> arrived this last night. (<em>Copy. ¼ p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Robert Young and Justinian Offley at Broach to the Surat Factory, February</span> 13, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 283).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">As Surat cannot supply more money, they have been obliged to cease buying cotton yarn; are now baling what they have already bought, and are getting in their broad baftas. The Customer is daily pressing them for payment of their debt for customs, but they are putting him off until they receive what is due from the Governor. To-day they will dispeed eight bales of dutties to Jahāngīrpur, being the last of their goods for Mokha. Their broker at Baroda writes that he has small hope of recovering the money due there without the aid of an Englishman; but they have now sent him the Mīrzā’s<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-011" href="#sec-05-fn-011" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-011" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> letter to the Deputy-Governor urging the payment of the debt. Their goods will be ready within six days. Shall they send them to Jahāngīrpur? The Dutch arrived yesterday with money [to purchase goods]. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received February</em> 14.)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-238"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="238" data-folio="199" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n237_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 199">[p. 199]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">John Leachland at Rānder to the President at Surat, February 16, 1623</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 284).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">All the goods for Mokha have been dispatched, and only those for England remain here. Details of the packages. <em>P.S.</em>—Has written to Mr. Hutchinson. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Edward Heynes and William Hill at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory, February 16, 1623</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 287).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Received their bills for Rs. 12,000. Have drawn on them for Rs. 10,000 at Rs. 41¼ per hundred mahmūdīs. Will not need any further remittance, as they can draw for any small amount now likely to be wanted. ‘Antony<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-012" href="#sec-05-fn-012" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-012" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>’ the Dutchman got slightly better terms for his bills, but had to pay Rs. 80 to a broker to insure the payment at Surat before he could clear his goods. Mahmūd Taqī was much disappointed at the decision not to buy his indigo, and desired them to give him the preference in any future dealings. As to the coral, he did not absolutely refuse, but was evidently not very favourably inclined towards the proposal. Probably he will make inquiries at Surat as to its quality, &amp;c., before doing anything more. Mr. Parker, who has long lain ill of a languishing disease for which he can find no remedy here, has importuned to be allowed to repair to Surat, and they have consented. Trust that this will be approved. He started this day ‘with a pallinkeene and attendance’, defraying his own expenses. Mr. Heynes awaits their order as to the jewels and his departure for Surat. <em>P.S. by Heynes.</em>— Suggests that a surgeon be sent to meet Mr. Parker. ‘Yf God mend him nott in his jurney, you shall finde him a nothemaie [i. e. an ‘anatomy’ or skeleton] of a man, butt harte whole.’ Consideration should be had of the need of supplying some assistance here, for Mr. Hill is sickly. (<em>Copy. 1¾ pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Captain John Hall, aboard the <em>Blessing</em>, to the President at Surat, February 17, 1623</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 285).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Perceives his resolution for their early dispatch. Cannot possibly sail at the date mentioned, but will do his utmost to be ready within <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-239" data-page="239" data-folio="200" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n238_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 200">[p. 200]</button> seven days. As to the transfer of the silk. Begs to know to what place they are to be employed. Suggests the four ships should go together to the Red Sea for the better protection of their valuable cargoes. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">JOHN LEACHLAND AT JAHĀNGĪRPUR TO THE SAME, FEBRUARY 17, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 285).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has dispatched the goods intended for England. Will see to the cotton yarn and piece-goods. (<em>Copy. ¼ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">NICHOLAS WOODCOCK AT SWALLY TO THE PRESIDENT AT SURAT, FEBRUARY 18, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 286).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has prepared his ship for taking in more goods, and would be glad to have some soon to stiffen her. <em>P.S.</em>—Requests that he will take some measures for clearing the ship of ‘the Romans<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-013" href="#sec-05-fn-013" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-013" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> goods, for they lye and pester us much’. (<em>Copy. ¼ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">CONSULTATION HELD IN SURAT BY PRESIDENT RASTELL AND MESSRS. JAMES, BANGHAM, YOUNG, AND HOARE, FEBRUARY 18, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. i. p. 36).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">It is resolved to send the <em>Blessing</em> and <em>Reformation</em> to Chaul and Dābhol to seize any junks there preparing for the Red Sea, and to bring them to Surat, ‘for satisfaction for the lost caphila.’ Robert Hutchinson and Thomas Thomson to go on these ships as merchants. Robert Young to be stationed at Swally to hasten the lading of the <em>Whale</em> and <em>Dolphin</em>, and to prevent private goods being put into them. (<em>Copy. 1 p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">THOMAS RASTELL, GILES JAMES, AND NICHOLAS BANGHAM AT SURAT TO THE MASULIPATAM FACTORY, FEBRUARY 18, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Masulipatam</em>, vol. ix. p. 20).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Received theirs of December 31 on February 5. The <em>Rose</em> brought back letters from the Fleet of Defence before Goa, dated December 28; nothing of importance had occurred, but they had <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-240" data-page="240" data-folio="201" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n239_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 201">[p. 201]</button> burnt a small vessel from Cochin. One carrack could be seen in the harbour, but according to report there were five galleons further in. They intend to remain until the end of the monsoon, and then go to Batavia, as the season is too late to carry out the proposed voyage to Mozambique. The <em>Discovery</em> was to receive 30,000 rials from the <em>Exchange</em> to take to Batavia, besides the six chests (and goods) already on board; and this supply should enable the President and Council at that place to relieve the needs of Masulipatam and Pulicat. In any case Surat cannot at present spare money for this purpose, though possibly they may be able to do so if they carry out their project of seizing ‘this countrey joncke, wich as yett standes doubtfull, in respect of a daylie expected restitution of the Companies losse caphila, accordinge to conditions contracted here with the agent of Mellike Amber, whose performance therein limitted to the time of our shippes departure for Mocho is the cause that houlds our one resolusion till then in suspence.’ Request that this answer may be communicated to Pulicat; will write to that factory shortly. ‘Our fleet from Persia are (blessed be God) returned in saftie with this yeares full cavidall of silke, consistinge of 804 far[dles], great and small, wich by the lattnesse of the seasonne and our want of goods to make it up two shippes ladinge (the silke in one bottom beinge too great an adventure) we are inforced to despeed upon the <em>Whale</em> and <em>Doulphine</em>, nowe bound for Mocho, from whence ere they returne we shall be able to furnish out the rest of there ladinge, havinge procured allreadie towards its accomplishinge above 100 bales of indicoe (now parte att shippes and parte on the way) to winter with the silke att Mocho. In Persia noe newe thinge hath hapned by any assistance of the English, though importuned much by the Persians for prosecucation [<em>sic</em>] of there conquest on Muscatt, where the Portingalles with 7,000 men, a gallion, and many friggottes are awaytinge there assault, wich they intend neverthelesse to attempt by the helpe of many friggattes and boates of there owne, noe doubt to a bade successe. They seeme to make good there promise by proffer of the one halfe customes to the factours there residinge, and by the Shawe his letters to His Majestie do sollicitt somewhat cover[t]lie the performance on our parte concerninge Ormoz, wich is nowe fare better fortefied then before.’ (<em>Copy. 1½ pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-241"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="241" data-folio="202" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n240_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 202">[p. 202]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Captain John Hall, aboard the</span> <em>Blessing</em>, <span class="sc">to the President at Surat, February</span> 19, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 286).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They are now ready to set sail on a 15–20 days’ voyage. They have still some silk to discharge, but can get rid of it in three hours. Proposes to leave one of his purser’s mates behind to look after certain business. Requests his acceptance of a case of rosewater. <em>P.S.</em>—Begs him to send down any of the crew that may be at Surat; also to save them some bread. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">President Fursland at Batavia to Gabriel Towerson [at Amboyna], February</span> 20, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Miscellaneous</em>, vol. viii. p. 569).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The <em>Discovery</em> brought news that the taking of Ormus had not proved so profitable as was hoped. The town was easily captured, but the castle held out for eight weeks, and cost the lives of a thousand Persians and twenty Englishmen. The booty obtained by the latter amounted only to about 18,000<em>l.</em> sterling and ten brass guns, with which the fleet has returned to Surat, leaving the Persians in possession. (<em>Extract only.</em> 2 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Robert Hutchinson, aboard the</span> <em>Whale</em>, <span class="sc">to the Surat Factory, February</span> 21, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 289).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Sent a message by Mr. Salbank yesterday that the <em>Dolphin</em> was fully laden. The Ahmadābād, &amp;c., goods remain on shore until further orders. As to the <em>Whale</em>, although his instructions were not to put any indigo into her until the pepper arrived, he has ventured to ship some of the former at the master’s request. Will make over charge to Mr. Young, and await their commission and dispeed with the <em>Blessing</em> and <em>Reformation</em>. <em>P.S.</em>—Captain Woodcock is anxious to know what he is to do with ‘the Roman gentleman his goods’. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Instructions from the President and Council at Surat to Captain John Hall for his Voyage to Dābhol, February</span> 22, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. i. p. 37).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">As it is understood that certain junks are being prepared for the Red Sea in the ports of Dābhol and Chaul, and it is deemed <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-242" data-page="242" data-folio="203" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n241_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 203">[p. 203]</button> advisable to seize them in satisfaction of the losses sustained from ‘the Decan nation’, he is to sail in the <em>Blessing</em>, with Richard Wedmore in the <em>Reformation</em> (and two merchants to assist them), to Chaul, where the latter vessel is to be left to search the port, while the former goes on to Dābhol. All junks seized should be brought to Swally, and handed over intact to the Council. The crews may be set on shore. Care is to be taken to put a sufficient crew into each prize. He is to return before the time of the <em>Whale’s</em> departure for Mokha. Any Indian junks met with that are not provided with an English pass should also be seized. (<em>Copy.</em> 2 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Christopher Rosons at Swally to the President at Surat, February</span> 28, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 289).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has sent all the pepper bags available; the rest have been used to lay under the pepper on the <em>Whale</em>, for want of mats, of which a supply should be forwarded. Gunny and ropes received. (<em>Copy.</em> ¼ <em>p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Christopher Rosons, aboard the</span> <em>WHALE</em>, <span class="sc">to the President at Surat, March</span> 2, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 292).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">As to the shipment of goods. The <em>Dolphin</em> is already full and can take no more. They are now lading the <em>Whale</em>. (<em>Copy.</em> ¼ <em>p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Nicholas Woodcock in Swally Road to the President at Surat, March</span> 3, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 293).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Is sorry to hear of his friend’s illness. Has sent up their surgeon with a bottle of their best wine. Is lading goods with all possible speed, and hopes to sail at the same time as the <em>Dolphin</em>. <em>P.S.</em>— Requests that some mats may be forwarded. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">William Hill at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory, March</span> 3, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 293).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Hopes Mr. Heynes has reached Surat in safety with his charge. Last night Safī Khān and Mahmūd Taqī sent for him and the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-243" data-page="243" data-folio="204" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n242_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 204">[p. 204]</button> broker, ‘where when wee came, they tould us thatt the Kinges shipp was toe goe for Mocha, and thatt the shipp and goods onely belonged to the Kinge, the Normall [Nūrmahal], Assafcon, Suffichan, and other greatte men, and thatt they had received intelligence, both from Surratt and Cambaya, thatt the English had given outt speeches thatt they would sease one her; wherefore they demaunded a screet for her peaseable passage, and forced mee to promyse them to dispeede presantly an expresse, thatt soe they might receave answer there with all speede possible; usinge many good woords, with fayre promyses of frindshipp.’ Begs an early reply to their demand. Sends copies of his accounts. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p. Received March 7.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Consultation held in Surat by President Rastell and Messrs. James, Salbank, Bangham, Heynes, Young, Hopkinson, and Hoare, March 3, 1623</span> (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. i. p. 39).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Reference is made to previous consultations of February 13 and October 23, 1622, at both of which the idea of seizing the junks of this country was approved; and, as the Dutch continue their resolution of surprising ships from the Deccan ports, it is propounded whether the project shall be put in execution this year at the return of the ships now bound for the Red Sea. It is urged that they have no other means of obtaining redress for their losses and wrongs, and that the present opportunity is a favourable one, as the Company’s estate in India is very small, while that of the natives now abroad or about to be sent is unusually large. The chief objections are that there would be a risk of the trade being totally lost, and that the merchants on shore at the time would be liable to rigorous treatment; to which it is answered that they have the authority of the Company for risking the trade, and that they are one and all prepared to endure any hardships that may result from their action. It is therefore decided unanimously that the project shall be ‘put in action by the most convenient and certaynest meanes they could devise’. ‘The manner how and the place where the said seazure might with most conveniencie and securitie bee effected’ is left to the decision of the council appointed <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-244" data-page="244" data-folio="205" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n243_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 205">[p. 205]</button> for the voyage, with the limitations that no vessel shall be seized outward bound for Mokha, ‘nor homward to the norward of Aden;’ that the fleet shall not return with its captures earlier than the end of October; and that no prize crews shall be put on board the junks. In lieu thereof the principal persons of each vessel are to be taken on board the English ships, and well treated. To assist in guarding the prizes, ‘as also to secure the greate estate alreadie laden uppon the <em>Whale</em> and <em>Doulphin</em> for England,’ it is decided that on his return from Dābhol Captain Hall shall be ordered to accompany them with the two ships under his command. For the decision of all matters of importance, a council of six is appointed, consisting of Captain Hall, the four merchants, and Master Woodcock. The masters of the other ships are to be admitted ‘for advice’, but are not to have votes; while all purely mercantile questions are to be settled by the four merchants only. In the case of the death of a member, the survivors will elect to the vacancy. The merchants appointed for the voyage are Messrs.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Salbank, Heynes, Hoare, and Crispe, with Richard Garrett as assistant. The <em>Whale</em> and <em>Dolphin</em> are to sail in company, and the former may leave behind her the goods not yet shipped, which she is to take to England. The <em>Blessing</em> will bring on these. No goods provided for England are to be sold at Mokha, as there will be no chance of investing the proceeds there. After the seizures have been made, the <em>Reformation</em> (if present) should be sent in advance to apprise the factors at Surat, arriving, if possible, by the middle of September. If, however, Captain Hall’s ships should return from Dābhol too late to get to Mokha this monsoon, they will be instructed to winter at ‘Cape Russalgate’ [Ras-al-hadd] or some other suitable place, and await the junks and the other two ships at Diu Head. Messrs. Bangham, Leachland, and Lancaster are appointed to proceed to Ahmadābād to procure indigo for the return cargo of the <em>Dolphin</em>, and three chests of money are to be landed from the <em>Blessing</em> for that purpose. Every effort is to be made to get this indigo down before the rains, with a view to its being ready for lading before the news of the seizure of the junks arrives. The Prince’s officers have proposed to buy the whole of the coral, paying one lakh of mahmūdīs down, one lakh in a month’s time, and the rest in September and October; it <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-245" data-page="245" data-folio="206" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n244_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 206">[p. 206]</button> is decided to accept this offer, as the quantity is sufficient for three years’ consumption, and it is better to run the risk of the last payments being withheld rather than to leave the coral to be embezzled or spoiled. Nicholas Crispe is to be paid 20<em>l.</em> on account of wages, ‘in regard of his paines taken in the Companies service,’ and subject to allowance at home. Rs. 300 to be remitted to John Willoughby to defray his expenses in coming down to Surat, but the Council for the present accepts no liability in regard to the Rs. 4,300 for which he has been forced to give a bill. (<em>Copy. 4½ pp.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Christopher Rosons at Swally to the President at Surat, March</span> 4, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 292).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Shipping details. Robert Tottle has arrived to help in getting the goods aboard. (<em>Copy. ¾ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Nicholas Woodcock in Swally Road to the President at Surat, March</span> 7, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 294).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Acknowledges receipt of certain goods, which shall be carefully stowed. Is hastening the dispatch of the ship as much as possible. (<em>Copy. ¼ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Christopher Rosons at Swally Sands to John Bangham at Surat, March</span> 8, 1623 (<em>O.C.</em> 1104).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Requests him to make the investment mentioned in a previous letter sent by Mr. Lancaster, and to dispatch the goods as soon as possible. (<em>Holograph. ½ p. Seal.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">William Hoare at Surat Custom House to Christopher Rosons at Swally, March</span> 9, 1623 (<em>O.C.</em> 1105).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Asks him to notify Mr. Woodcock of the arrival of ‘the six bales sent by this bearer, which belong to him and me’. Some trunks of his, brought down by John George, if not already aboard the <em>Dolphin</em>, should be put into the <em>Whale</em>, in which ship Hoare is to go with Mr. Heynes. Rosons is to remain at Surat. (<em>Holograph. ½ p.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-246"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="246" data-folio="207" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n245_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 207">[p. 207]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Joseph Hopkinson at Surat to Christopher Rosons or Thomas Hawkeridge at Swally, March 10, 1623</span> (<em>O.C.</em> 1106).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Entreats him to see the accompanying five bales put on board the <em>Whale</em>. (<em>Holograph. ½ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Instructions from the President and Council at Surat to Captain John Hall and his Council for the Voyage to Mokha, March 15, 1623</span> (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. i. p. 43).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Should the <em>Blessing</em> and <em>Reformation</em> return at the expected time from Dābhol, all four ships are to set sail in company, but if not, then the <em>Whale</em> and <em>Dolphin</em> are to proceed alone and the others will follow. Captain Hall is to have supreme command; until his arrival Nicholas Woodcock will take charge. No junks are to be interfered with on the voyage outward. On arrival at Mokha only the <em>Blessing</em> is to remain there, the other three going over to Asab, both because the air is more wholesome and because by this means no trouble will be caused by the disorderliness of the sailors. On the return voyage, after clearing the Straits and making sure that there is no risk of their action being prematurely discovered at Surat, they are to seize all Indian junks returning from Jiddah and Mokha. No Englishmen should be allowed to go on board, but the junks are to be kept under command ‘only by the awfull power of your ordinance and houldinge in custodie the principall offecers, merchants, and cheefe passengers aboard of your shipp’; there will then be no risk of pilfering and no opening for accusations on that score. Any pillager should be punished with death. After the capture of the junks, the <em>Reformation</em> should be sent ahead to notify the Surat factors, in order that they may get down as many of their goods as possible and repair aboard ‘to avoide the furey and insolency of these people’. If the <em>Blessing</em> and <em>Reformation</em> be unable to get to Mokha, they will await the junks and the other two ships at Diu Head (‘the ordinare landfall of these India traders’); and on the arrival of the latter the <em>Reformation</em> should come over to Surat, while the others and their prizes cruise about in that latitude so as not to arrive before October 20 at the earliest. A special stock of water should be <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-247" data-page="247" data-folio="208" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n246_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 208">[p. 208]</button> provided in order that some may be given to the junks if necessary, in addition to a stock for the homeward voyage of the <em>Whale</em> and <em>Dolphin</em>, should they be unable to procure a further supply here. Besides the junks of Gujarāt, any belonging to Chaul, Dābhol, the Portuguese or Malabar ports should be taken. In these cases, ‘wee will not abridge you your shares,’ though a strict account must be kept of the goods, jewels, treasure, &amp;c., captured; but nothing of the kind must be attempted with the Gujarātī junks, as they are to be restored intact should the factors’ demands be granted. A council established, as laid down in the consultation of March 3. <em>P.S. (March 17).</em>—‘Gods aflictinge hand havinge prevented us in a contrary disposure of som the above nominated persons of counsell by the <em>Whales</em> unhapie shippwrack,’ Matthew Wills, Richard Lancaster, and Thomas Pory are to take the places of Salbank, Woodcock, and Crispe.
