In the absence of Captain Hall, Heynes is to command, and is to have a casting vote. In the event of his death he is to be succeeded by Hoare. (<em>Copy.</em> 6 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1">INSTRUCTIONS TO THE MERCHANTS FOR THE VOYAGE TO MOKHA, MARCH 15, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. i. p. 50).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Refer to the general instructions already given. The goods on board destined for England are not to be meddled with, as it is not likely that they can be replaced on the ships’ return from Mokha, when all trade will probably be stopped, owing to ‘our expected differance with these people’. On arriving at Mokha, before landing their goods regard must be had to the intentions of the Dutch, who in revenge for the bad usage of their people last year may be planning some assault upon the town. The narrow baftas should not be sold except at a better price than last year, for more profit can be made of them in England. The rest of their commodities may be got rid of at any price. In the complimentary letters to the Governor herewith delivered, he has been asked to obtain the release of the <em>Rose’s</em> thirteen men detained at Aden; they are to do their best to effect this. In consideration of the former services of ‘Shavan the Jew’ at Mokha, they have licensed the transport of certain bales of goods for him in the <em>Whale</em>; for the freight of these he is to pay at the current rates. Their returns for Surat should be in gold or rials (or both), as they find most convenient. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-248" data-page="248" data-folio="209" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n247_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 209">[p. 209]</button> Any drugs fit for England may, however, be bought; and three or four tons of aloes may be procured if they touch at Socotra. They should do their best to get exact information as to the trade of the Dutch at Mokha. (<em>Copy. 2¾ pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Edward Heynes and William Hoare, aboard the</span> <em>Dolphin</em>, <span class="sc">to the Surat Factory, March</span> 16, 1623<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-014" href="#sec-05-fn-014" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-014" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 295).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">‘I must bee unto you as one of Jobes messengers, to wryte you of the misserablest disaster thatt ever beefall our masters or ourselves in these parts. . . . The <em>Whalle</em>, in the mide waye betwixt Surratt Barr and Damon is cast awaye, herselfe and goodes lost (as farr as att present wee may conceave) unrecoverable; most of her men saved, yett whome, or whome lost, wee cannot yett wryte you.’ They suggest the dispatch of boats from Surat to endeavour to save her goods; also request copies of the invoices, &amp;c., delivered to Mr. Salbank, for they fear ‘the ould man’ is lost with all his papers. They are detaining the <em>Dolphin</em> until further instructions. Cannot write details, for ‘greefe abondeth in us soe exceedingly’, but refer them to the bearer. <em>P.S.</em>—‘Mr. Salbancke, Mr. Chrispe, Richard Garratt, and many others lost, butt Mr. Woodcocke saved.’ (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received same night.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Matthew Wills, William Hoare, Richard Lancaster, and Thomas Pory, aboard the</span> <em>Dolphin</em>, <span class="sc">to the Surat Factory, March</span> 19, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 295).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Most of the country boats sent down have arrived. Note also the measures taken to watch the coast for any goods that may float ashore. ‘The conditionn of the wracke is such thatt it is the generall oppinion little or none of her goods wilbee saved, and thatt the attendance of your boatts will be more chargeable then benefitiall to our masters.’ Will, however, retain them until they hear further. According to order they have examined the master and principal officers concerning the disaster, and send their depositions herewith. ‘Wee that weare spectatours of soe sudaine an accidentt in the tyme of darkenes and trouble to free ourselves from the daunger of fallinge one her gave cause thatt nott any <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-249" data-page="249" data-folio="210" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n248_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 210">[p. 210]</button> observacion by us could be apprehended.’ Are sending up the survivors, except twenty-eight who volunteered to serve in the present voyage. Captain Woodcock and sixty-eight more are proceeding to Surat in his barge and the <em>Dolphin’s</em> long boat. Give this notice in order that steps may be taken to prevent disorder or desertion to the Portuguese. Return an invoice. Are ready to set sail within three hours. (<em>Copy. 1 p. Received March 20.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">PRESIDENT FURLAND AT BATAVIA TO GEORGE ROBINSON [AT ACHIN], MARCH 19, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Miscellaneous</em>, vol. viii. p. 571).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The fight of the Anglo-Dutch squadron with a Portuguese fleet off Mozambique. From the wreck of the vice-admiral they took 680,000 [<em>sic</em>] pieces of eight, but ‘an infinite wealth’ and 400 Portuguese perished in the sea. The squadron is now blockading Goa. Two Dutch ships fought with a carrack near the Cape, and believe that they sunk her.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-015" href="#sec-05-fn-015" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-015" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> These two vessels lost 530<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-016" href="#sec-05-fn-016" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-016" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> men by sickness between the Cape and Bantam. The Dutch repulsed at Macao, with the loss of 300 men. They have now settled at the Pescadores. (<em>Modern extract only. 2 pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">NICHOLAS WOODCOCK AT SWALLY TO THE PRESIDENT AT SURAT, MARCH 22, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 297).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Some of his men have gone to Surat without leave, including one Daniel Peel, who has reported that he intends to serve the Mogul, and has enticed others to go with him. Requests that he may be stopped and punished. Inquires for news from Captain Hall. (<em>Copy. ¼ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">THE SAME TO THE SAME, MARCH 22, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 297).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Thanks him for his ‘loving remembrance’. Repeats his request for the detention of Peel and his companions. The <em>Blessing</em> is now at anchor within sight of Old Swally. (<em>Copy. ¼ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-250"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="250" data-folio="211" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n249_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 211">[p. 211]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Robert Tottle at ‘Dandeboere’</span><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-017" href="#sec-05-fn-017" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-017" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> <span class="sc">to the President at Surat, March</span> 22, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 296).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">On arrival at Noesorre [Nosārī] they went to the Governor and showed him the letter from the Governor of Surat. He sent them to the Kotwāl in order that the latter might accompany them to the waterside, as he had been present at the recovery of certain of the goods already. ‘Att our cominge to his house wee found him a makinge a saddell with a pare of cloth breetches of Mr. Brutons and dryinge of English papers.’ He explained that he found these at the waterside, but it is reported that he has other things in his house and he confesses to have taken two knives. There is nothing of the Company’s here, except a bale of tobacco. Of private men’s chests there are thirty-four, but they have all been opened by the natives and the Kotwāl, who will not admit the receipt of more than a small quantity of piece-goods. As nothing more is to be had here, they are proceeding to Gandevī, having heard that a boat from that place has been here and probably carried off some things. Yesterday news came from Balsār that Stephen French had there borrowed a horse and some peons from ‘Gopall Das’ [Gopāl Dās], saying that the President had sent him to Damān. They have found two pipes of ‘racke’ [arrack] and have intelligence of two more casks, but know not what is in them, and cannot get at them because of the water. ‘Scander’ [<em>see</em> p. 8] has written the enclosed letters to the Governor and ‘John Collebecke’ [Jam Qulī Beg, Captain of Surat Castle], to be delivered if the President thinks fit. Here are twenty-seven bales of tobacco belonging to private men. <em>P.S.</em>—Requests orders as to the tobacco. It is so much damaged by the water that it is not worth the expense of cartage and storage. The chests are all staved in. If they are wanted, a boat should be sent to fetch them to Swally. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Instructions from the President and Council at Surat to the Merchants proceeding to Ahmadābād, March</span> 25, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. i. p. 53).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">To endeavour to procure as much indigo as it is possible, to send down before the rains. It is understood that Mahmūd Taqī, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-251" data-page="251" data-folio="212" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n250_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 212">[p. 212]</button> Diwan of Ahmadābād, has 1,300 or 1,400 churls which he is anxious to sell on favourable terms. They are to bear in mind the Company’s injunctions to buy the better quality only. The best way would be to purchase loose indigo at Sarkhej, but as time is short they will probably find it necessary to take that which is already fardled. Detailed accounts to be kept. At first not more than a thousand small churls should be bought; and if possible, agreement should be made with Mahmūd Taqī to pay him at Surat. Directions for the sale of amber. They are to be frugal in their housekeeping, and not imitate others who ‘by a vaine retenew of superfluous servants putt the Company to a prodigall expence’. They should all return together instead of making up several caravans. Nicholas Bangham is to be ‘prime or cape marchant’ and to take charge of the cash; John Leachland (who is to look after the general accounts) is to be second; William Hill to be third; and Thomas Hawkridge (who is to disburse the house expenses and charges of merchandise) to be fourth. (<em>Copy.</em> 2½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Justinian Offley at Broach to the President at Surat, March</span> 25, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 301).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">This morning Messrs. Bangham, Leachland and Glanvill departed for Ahmadābād, and Mr. Young for Baroda to get in the money due. Glanvill was taken in the place of Hawkridge, who remains here very sick. Mr. Young has left 2,000 mahmūdīs for Rastell; the money awaits his orders for its disposal. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p. Received March</em> 26.)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Robert Tottle at Gandevī to the President at Surat, March</span> 26, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 301).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">On arrival went to ‘Gopallvesy’ [Gopāl Vaisya] and showed him the Governor’s letter. He at once told off a peon to take them to all the waterside villages and to provide them with boats. They failed, however, to find any goods, and could hear of none having come ashore. So they went by boat to a town called ‘Mender’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-018" href="#sec-05-fn-018" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-018" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-252" data-page="252" data-folio="213" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n251_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 213">[p. 213]</button> belonging to Nosārī. Finding some scattered tobacco, they made further search and discovered a chest hidden under a hedge. The ‘pottell’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-019" href="#sec-05-fn-019" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-019" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> of the place denied all knowledge of the matter, alleging that he had been absent of late; so they took him to ‘Gopallvesy’, who was very angry with him and gave him strict orders to have the place watched. In all their journey they have only seen of the Company’s goods two butts of arrack and one bundle of tobacco. They think, therefore, that the hold is not opened, and that more will come in later. If furnished with twenty or thirty peons they could have the coast watched for a distance of three or four <em>kos</em>. They are now proceeding to Balsār. The natives tell them that when a country ship is cast away the goods do not float in before a month or two. (<em>Copy.  ¾ p.  Received March 27.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Captain John Hall [at Swally] to the President at Surat, [March 29?], 1623</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 294).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has received his orders of the 27th to put ashore their lead, but this cannot possibly be done before their return. His leaving the <em>Reformation</em> was generally approved. They have now got all their goods aboard and most of their provisions. Marvels that money is not sent down for the ship’s needs. Hopes to sail on Saturday. <em>P.S.</em>—He is welcome to the lead which was used for wrapping the broadcloth. (<em>Copy.  ½ p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Justinian Offley at Broach to the President at Surat, March 29, 1623</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 302).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">According to instructions he has taken into the Company’s cash the 2,000 mahmūdīs and has remitted them to Ahmadābād by bills of exchange. The shroffs were very unwilling to deal, as the disturbed state of the country makes the conveyance of money difficult. Hawkridge, who is now better, has paid in 400 mahmūdīs and requests that they may be credited to him at Surat. He also desires to know whether he is to follow Mr. Bangham to Ahmadābād on recovery, as the latter directed. (<em>Copy.  ½ p.  Received March 31.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-253"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="253" data-folio="214" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n252_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 214">[p. 214]</button></div><h2 class="sec-h1">INSTRUCTIONS FROM THE PRESIDENT AND COUNCIL AT SURAT TO CAPTAIN HALL, PROCEEDING TO MOKHA, MARCH 31, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. i. p. 49).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In the event of the <em>Reformation</em> losing the monsoon for Mokha and being thus forced to winter at ‘Cape Rasselgatt’ by herself, she is to return direct to Surat, without staying at Diu Head; and, should the <em>Blessing</em> meet her, the master should be instructed accordingly, in case she should be unable to get into Swally. If nothing be seen of her, the <em>Blessing</em> must take her place and come for Swally as arranged. (<em>Copy.</em> <sup>1</sup>/<sub>2</sub> <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1">CAPTAIN JOHN HALL [AT SEA?] TO THE PRESIDENT AT SURAT, MARCH [31?]<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-020" href="#sec-05-fn-020" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-020" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 305).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has met the <em>Reformation</em> and sent her to Swally, where she should arrive to-morrow. Has placed in her Jeremy Sugar, formerly purser of the <em>Primrose</em>. Recommends him to the President’s favour. (<em>Copy.</em> <sup>1</sup>/<sub>4</sub> <em>p. Received April 5.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1">CAPTAIN MICHAEL GREEN [OFF GOA] TO PRESIDENT RASTELL AT SURAT [MARCH, 1623] (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 303).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Nothing has happened since the departure of the <em>Rose</em>. They are all in good health. ‘Since our rydinge heere wee have had divers letters brought from Abraham Abdelaw [Ibrāhīm Ādil Shāh] Kinge of Visepoore [Bījāpur] by a sarvantt of his, wherein hee desyreth with all vehemency a perpetuall amitye to bee concluded betwixtt us and him, to this onely instantt [intent?] that wee joyninge with him may dispose and exclud the Portinggalls from such places (meaninge Goa and the places neere adjoyninge) which they soe longe tyme have unjustly detayned, wherein with much con[s]tancy hee perswages us to persever in the attemptt begun (meaninge the bees[e]ginge of Goa with our forces by sea), and thatt yf itt shall bee thought fitt by us to send a man of discreetion to goe through with the league begun betwixt him and us, hee for his partt will <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-254" data-page="254" data-folio="215" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n253_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 215">[p. 215]</button> undertake to bringe them to such a scarcety of victualls by his forces apposed against them by land thatt in the least tyme then wee cann conseave wee shall be masters of the cittye of Goa; to which his demaunds wee have divers tymes retourned the messenger with faire speeches accordinge as the tyme fitted, to continewe the newe begun amity untill wee shall bee of more possibillitye to performe our desyers and his request, which cannot bee donne att present, the tyme of yeare beinge altogeather spentt. As conscerninge there forces att Goa, they have ten shippes, greate and small in the ryver, besides gallyones and frygatts, and the carricke, which nowe lyeth alltogeather unrigged, havinge (to the greefe of many unmarryed Lisborne wenches) given over there voyadge for this yeare.’ The junk carrying this letter belongs to Safī Khān, Governor of Cambay, and was captured coming out of Goa, to which place she had carried corn, butter, and other provisions. She had a Portuguese pass, and also one from ‘Vanderbroocke’ [Van den Broeck, Dutch chief at Surat]; but the only thing that saved her from confiscation was the fear that the English factors in Gujarāt might suffer in consequence. ‘The principall thinge in her is a tree currall sett in gould by artt, made to distill watter from the topp unseene, rounde aboutt the branches. Itt is contayned in five chestes, and beinge by us vallued itt was thought to bee worth 2,000<em>l.</em>, itt beinge procured by the Governor of Cambaya to bee made in Goa against the beirthdaye of the Greatte Mogull and to him to bee presented, for which intentt this juncke was purposely sentt.’ ‘Rufrero is captain of the castell att the enterance of the ryver of Goa, where the carricke rydeth, with whome the Viceroy recydeth, to attend our further attemptts. Wee have had divers letters brought by the Portingalls aboutt the redemtionn of there prysnors, butt concluded nothinge as yett; only wee attend an aunswer of the last, wherein itt was proffered by the Dutch 24 parsonns (whereof the women [<em>see</em> p. 155 <em>n.</em>] are to bee ttwo) for six of the Dutch which they hould prysnors, which weare belonginge to the <em>Samsonn</em> and surprysed att Surratt.’ The prisoners are suffering from scurvy, and he fears they will infect his men. The fleet is not likely to remain longer than March 10, by which time they hope the <em>Rose</em> will have returned. (<em>Copy. 1¾ pp. Received April 2.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-255"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="255" data-folio="216" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n254_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 216">[p. 216]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Richard Wedmore [Master of the</span> <em>Reformation</em>] <span class="sc">to the President at Surat</span>, [April 1?] 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 304).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Announces his arrival. Is now ready to follow any instructions he may receive. Trusts he will not be limited to any one port, but that if he misses the Red Sea he may winter in the Persian Gulf. Understands that the money in the ship is to be landed; so he will get it ready. Has anchored this night off the river mouth, and hopes to see the President to-morrow. Did his best to bring in the frigate left in his charge by Captain Hall, but she sank on March 20 to the southwards of Chaul. About March 1 [<em>sic</em>] he met with a junk of Goghā, coming from Goa, but did not Interfere with her, as she had passes both from the Dutch and from Captain Green’s fleet. Received two letters from her, addressed to Van den Broeck and the President [<em>see</em> p. 214], and has sent them herewith. Requests the delivery of money for the purchase of fresh provisions, as he has been ill ever since he quitted Dābhol, and the crew have had a hard time. (<em>Copy. 1 p. Received April 2.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Robert Tottle at ‘Dandeboore’ to the President at Surat, April 2, 1623</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 302).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has received his letter ordering the chests to be sent to Surat. Suggests that a boat or two be dispatched to fetch these, and some other lumber which has floated ashore here at ‘Dandebora, a towne belonginge to Nōnsoree [Nosārī] which is right with the place where the shipp was cast awaye’. A chest full of things came ashore while they were at Gandevī. In their absence the natives stole a hogshead of arrack, but their peons followed them and took them to the Governor of Nosārī. On examination they confessed to have taken some chests and other things, and were therefore committed to prison. Last night a boat came in and carried away eleven pipes, two of which were full of arrack. Have been at Balsār, but could hear of nothing but nine bundles of tobacco. Left four peons to watch there. Complains of the small support received from the Nosārī officials. Requests 25 more <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-256" data-page="256" data-folio="217" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n255_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 217">[p. 217]</button> peons. A boat sent to the wreck reports that her mainmast has come out; so they think she will break up shortly. (<em>Copy. ¾ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham, John Leachland, William Hill, and John Glanvill at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory</span>, April 3, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 306).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Bills drawn on them by the Broach Factory for Rs. 835. Are awaiting a remittance from Surat, without which they can do little. ‘Heere is newes thatt Assufchan and Normall are both in disgrace, and there jaggres [<em>see</em> p. 90] taken from them. As conscerninge the Prynce, heere goeth soe many severall reportts of his proceedinges that wee cannott tell whatt to wrytte you.’ Hear that Captain Hall has arrived, having lost twenty men in an attack upon Dābhol; request details. The indigo of Mahmũd Taqī has been brought here and all dealers have been warned not to sell till it has been disposed of. He has not spoken to them on the subject. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received April 9.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Directions from the President and Council at Surat to Richard Wedmore, Master of the</span> <em>REFORMATION</em>, <span class="sc">for his Voyage to Mokha</span>, April 4, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. i. p. 57).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">He is referred for general instructions to the commission given to Captain Hall, whom he is to join as speedily as possible. Should he fail in doing this, he is to proceed to Cape ‘Rassellgatte’ and winter there or in some port adjacent. However, if he gets as far as Aden, and finds it safe to stop there instead, he may do so. In that case he is to communicate with the Mokha factors, and inquire how the thirteen English captives have been used; this example will guide him in his dealings with the natives. On the outward voyage, or while at Aden, no prizes are to be taken, for fear of imperilling the English at Mokha; but when homeward bound he is to seize all the Indian junks he can find, provided that he does nothing to hinder his return to Surat by September 20 at the latest. Any pepper, gum-lac, cotton wool or turmeric seized is to be reserved for shipment to England; the remaining goods may be sold. (<em>Copy. 1¾ pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-257"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="257" data-folio="216" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n256_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 216">[p. 216]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Richard Wedm</span><span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">ore</span> <span class="sc">the President a</span><span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">nd</span> <em>Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 3<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">7</span>…</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Announces his ar<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">rival</span> he may receive. but that if he mi<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">ght</span> Gulf. Understa<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">nds</span> so he will get <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">a</span> mouth, and hop<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">es</span> to bring in the <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">first</span> sank on March <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">28</span> he met with <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">Mr.</span> interfere with <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">the</span> Captain Gree<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">n</span> to Van den <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">Broeck</span> them herewi<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">th</span> of fresh prov<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">isions</span> and the cr<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">ew</span> <em>April</em> 2.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Rober</span><span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">t</span> <span class="sc">Surat,</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">A</span></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has r<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">eceived</span> Suggest<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">s</span> other l<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">etters</span> towne <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">to</span> place w<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">hich</span> came a<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">t</span> natives and to confes<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">sion</span> theref<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">ore</span> carrie<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">d</span> been tobac<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">co</span> suppo<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">rt</span> <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-258" data-page="258" data-folio="219" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n257_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 219">[p. 219]</button> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">progress of the Broach investment. Will look after the debts.</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">Must shortly draw on them for a supply of money. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p.</em></span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan"><em>Received April 7.</em>)</span></p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham, John Leachland, William Hill, and John Glanvill at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory, April</span> 7, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 308).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The news in their previous letter has been confirmed. They received the letter from Surat with instructions, and at once opened negotiations with Mahmūd Taqī. He is willing to sell his own indigo (371 bales) and promises to do the same with the rest (which belongs to the Prince) ‘when tymes are more quiett’; but he requires payment here and not in Surat. He asks Rs. 40 per bale, which they consider too dear, and now refer the question to Surat for decision and for the remittance of the necessary money. There is plenty of old indigo available, but the merchants are loth to deal because of the prohibition. If they buy any from the latter, they will endeavour to agree for payment at Surat. This must be done at the rate of exchange, which is at present Rs. 41 for 100 mahmū- dis paid there. New indigo may be had at Rs. 7½ and Rs. 8 per maund, but much of it is coarse and not well made. There is little hope of selling their ambergris, for trade is at a standstill. Request a speedy answer. (<em>Copy.</em> 1½ <em>pp. Received April 11.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Robert Young and Justinian Offley at Broach to the Surat Factory, April</span> 8, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 306).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Forward letters received from Broach. George Turner has arrived. <em>P.S.</em>—Will draw on them for 1,000 mahmūdīs in a day or two. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Robert Tottle at Nosārī to the President at Surat, April</span> 11, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 309).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">According to instructions they are sending up such things as have already been recovered, and for this purpose have hired a boat at one mahmūdī per candy. The Governor of this town has punished the natives who stole the butts of arrack. Under pressure they admitted having received two quilts, but denied all knowledge of the chests. Thereupon ‘wee had them whiped tell <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-259" data-page="259" data-folio="218" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n258_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 218">[p. 218]</button> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">Ni</span> <span class="sc"><span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">John</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">Apri</span></span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">Le</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">pers</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">toge</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">side</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">his</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">wa</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">per</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">hin</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">lyv</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">hi</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">so</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">g</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">tl</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">r</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">t</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">!</span> <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-260" data-page="260" data-folio="221" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n259_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 221">[p. 221]</button> have requested leave to return to England, their covenanted period of service having expired, it is decided to permit them to do so, and to order Thomas Mills to take the place of the former as chief factor or agent at Masulipatam, with John Dod for second, and John Milward for third. A supply of 30,000 rials in Japan silver, with other suitable goods, is to be sent to that place, to be invested in certain specified kinds of cloth. (<em>Copy.</em> 1½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Matthew Duke, Francis Futter, and John Dod at Masulipatam to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">April</span> 14, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 316).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wrote on March 20, but having now an opportunity, ‘by the returne of the Dutches pattamar,’ send a few further lines. Are daily expecting a ship from Batavia. The Dutch <em>Schiedam</em> is on the point of departure for Holland. The frigates formerly mentioned have gone again to sea, except one which is refitting at Narsapur. ‘Itt is reported thatt the Dutch will take upp att intrest 10,000 pa[godas], with another tanto alreadey in cash, [on?] purpose to imploye in dymonds, and for better accomodacion have framed a mill in there house att Bander<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-021" href="#sec-05-fn-021" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-021" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, and builded a house att the dymond mines, besides one other house builtt by Adulphe Thomas<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-022" href="#sec-05-fn-022" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-022" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> at ditto mines for his one turne. Soe thatt nowe the trade of dymonds is mainely and principallest followed by them. Whatt pryce dyamonds are att the minds [<em>sic</em>] wee cannott informe you; butt itt is lickley good cheape, by the proceedinges as afforesayd; butt wee see nor heare of any com heather to Mussulapatam; yett itt is most certaine thatt the mindes are open and stoore of stoones gotten.’ (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p. Received May</em> 16.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">John Willoughby at Agra to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">April</span> 15, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 313).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has received their letter directing him to proceed to Surat, but sending him not a penny for his expenses. Has, however, found a friend here to lend him money, and has this day quitted Agra <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-261" data-page="261" data-folio="222" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n260_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 222">[p. 222]</button> on his way towards them. The roads are very dangerous, ‘for heere is nothinge butt killinge every daye in these parttes.’ Has been forced to hire six Balūchīs at Rs. 5 per month, besides four or five other servants; also two camels, one for himself, and the other for the two mariners, William Norton and Roger Browne. For these he must pay Rs. 13 for the journey to Burhānpur. If he dies, his papers will be found in a little English box. ‘I have left in Agra two poorters… and have putt all such loggadge thatt is the Companies goods in one roome and locktt all the doores and putt my seale and gave the kees to the two portters in reguarde of the repayre, for thatt the goodes would bee spoyled. I have paid them two months beforehand, after the ratte of four rup[ees] per month. The present newes heere is: by reportt the Kinge will goe to Agemeere to see a peare<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-023" href="#sec-05-fn-023" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-023" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> thatt [there?], and whatt the peere shall dyrectt him against his sonne Corom, thatt hee will doe. Att presentt noe fight; butt the father and the sonne are somethinge neare one to the other. The Kinge is nowe att Fittapoore [Fatehpur Sīkrī] and Shawguñ [Shāh Jahān] is att Hendon [Hindaun], 25 course of. Sufficann<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-024" href="#sec-05-fn-024" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-024" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> is in prysonne, butt in the custodey of the Nurmall.’ Has received of ‘Walter Hutton’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-025" href="#sec-05-fn-025" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-025" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> Rs. 600, half of which he has remitted by exchange to Burhānpur. (<em>Copy. 1 p. Received May 5.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Robert Tottle at ‘Daudebotte’</span><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-026" href="#sec-05-fn-026" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-026" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> <span class="sc">to the President at Surat</span>, <span class="sc">April</span> 16, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 283).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They have sent away the boat with such things as were here. The peons are on the point of desertion, since there is no money to pay them, and ‘this is such a place that heere is nothinge to bee gott’. Money should be sent at once; at present they are forced to borrow of the ‘pottell’ [<em>see</em> p. 213]. At their departure for ‘Bulsar’ they left four peons to watch for anything that might come ashore. The Governor approved of this, and ‘tooke a screete from the townesmen thatt yf they keptt anythinge thatt belonged to the English they should bee punished’. During Tottle’s absence <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-262" data-page="262" data-folio="223" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n261_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 223">[p. 223]</button> a bale of tobacco and two chests (one of them empty) drifted in; whereupon the Governor whipped the peons on the supposition that they had emptied one of the chests; and on their denying his authority he committed them to prison. He has appropriated the chests and three bales of tobacco; and it is reported that he has sold the latter ‘and sayeth whatt soe ever comes there is his’. Intends to see him to-morrow, and demand a reason for his action. (<em>Copy. ¾ p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">President Fursland and Council at Batavia to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">April</span> 17, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 347).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Received their letter of December 10, 1622 [<em>see</em> p. 164] on February 4 by the <em>Discovery</em>. The <em>Unity</em> arrived here with Mr. Methwold on December 4. They wish that Mr. Slade had been allowed to use his discretion as to the <em>Discovery</em> calling at Achin, for he declares that he could easily have touched there. Forward a copy of his journal. On March 20 they dispatched the <em>Elizabeth</em> to Achin and the <em>Unity</em> to the coast of Sumatra. Explain their previous remarks as to the sending of the <em>Lion</em> to the Red Sea. ‘Wee knowe of noe shipps sente this yeare by the Dutch Company from hence for the Redd Sea, but certaine freemen of this place [it] is supposed are gone theather with three small phinises, the comander whereof is on Brustons, sometimes Bailive of this place. These wee make account will make noe scruple to take all they meate and can master, be they Guzeratts or others.’ Wish them all success in their projects for taming ‘the pride and injustice of those Guzeratts’. The barbers and washers duly arrived. Thank them for the slaves and provisions supplied to the Fleet of Defence. Regret Fitzherbert’s death, and approve his protest against the Dutch admiral. Share their apprehensions regarding the effect at home of the taking of Ormus, ‘for if the Company have ever beine so wary in medlinge with the Portingalls that they would never give any absolute commission for their surprisall, but still with caveats and doubtfull reservations, wee may justly feare this busines, so nerely conserninge the honor and estate of the Kinge of Spaine in all these Indies, will cause much troble at home. The Hollenders smile att it, and well they may, for howsoever it bee taken att <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-263" data-page="263" data-folio="224" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n262_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 224">[p. 224]</button> home, they are sure their mortall enimies is much weakned by these proceedings of our people.’ Dispatched for England the <em>Lesser James</em> on August 28, and the <em>Palsgrave</em> on February 11, with cargoes amounting to 200,000 rials of eight. Hope to lade the <em>Moon</em> and the <em>Elisabeth</em> this year. Are surprised to hear so little from England; their letters of three years back are not yet answered. The money, &amp;c., brought from Surat and that received by the Manilla fleet have quickened their declining affairs. Errors found in the invoice of the <em>Discovery</em>. As regards supplies, one-third of the quantity of wheat last sent will suffice in future. They would be glad of half a dozen mills for grinding it. The meal was much heated in the ship, but they could have wished for more, both of that and of the butter. ‘Gravances’ [<em>see</em> p. 166] are not required, as they are at once better and cheaper at Batavia. The defects in the <em>Discovery</em> are being remedied, and she will then be quite serviceable. Mr. Slade sends his commendations. At the instigation of the Dutch, the Fleet of Defence has abandoned its intention of proceeding to Mozambique. ‘Wee are glad it hapneth so well for us as to come of with creditt.’ They quite agree that any ships withdrawn from the Fleet of Defence and replaced by others from Surat should be at the disposal of the latter presidency, but request that the ships supplied in their place may be valued by the Council of the Fleet of Defence. As regards the further question, they think that any commander of the said Fleet nominated from Surat ought not to be appointed to another employment without his first repairing to Batavia to answer any charges that may be made against him. Moreover, under the agreement with the Dutch, no such commander can be appointed without the approval of the Council of Defence at Batavia. Note the receipt of the box of camphor sent on the <em>Advice</em>; can furnish a large quantity if desired. Present state of their affairs. Have been obliged to order the dissolution of all factories in the Moluccas, Amboyna, and Banda, owing to want of supply from England. ‘And wheras our masters for these two years have paid a great charge att Pallacatt and have received no benniffitt at all from that place, by reason the clothing made there vends cheefly in the Mollucas at those factores, as wee find experince, for all the goods wee have received from Pallacatt lies upon our hands, wee <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-264" data-page="264" data-folio="225" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n263_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 225">[p. 225]</button> have therfore allso resoulved to disolve that factory untill further order from our imployers; and for that purpose wee have now sent the shippe <em>Ruby</em> thether to take in all the Companies goods and factors in that place, and after to proceed for Musalapatnam.’ Cargo sent in her. The situation at Jambi and Achin. In consequence of the abandonment of the eastern factories, they will not need all the goods they have asked for from Surat, and they therefore forward an amended list. Have hopes of re-establishing relations with Bantam, but are at present awaiting orders from England regarding that business. The <em>Bull</em> is to be sent to Japan to withdraw the factory there. The Manilla fleet has returned with a booty amounting to 159,000 ‘tayes’ [taels] (‘every taye be 5<em>s</em>. sterling’) for each of the two nations, Dutch and English. The Dutch attacked Macao last June, but were repulsed with the loss of half their men. They thereupon seized the ‘Pecadores’ [the Pescadores Islands, between Formosa and China], and there remain, hoping to force the Chinese to trade with them. The <em>Mauritius</em> and <em>Rotterdam</em>, which arrived from Holland last September, reported that at the Cape they attacked a Portuguese carrack returning from Goa. While she was parleying for surrender, a storm separated them, and from some wreckage afterwards seen it was concluded that the carrack had foundered [<em>see</em> p. 210]. These two ships lost 350 men between the Cape and Batavia—an unprecedented mortality. They saw at the Cape four ships in chase of a fifth; these are thought to have been the English <em>Star</em> and <em>Eagle</em>, with two Dutch ships, pursuing a consort of the carrack. The losses sustained by the Portuguese seem likely to ‘shake their India trade, so as it may make them wearey, espetially if the Dutch and we should follow the defence as we have begune’. (<em>Copy. 8½ pp. Received August 5.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">President Fursland and Council at Batavia to Thomas Mills, &amp;c. [at Pulicat], April 17, 1623</span> (<em>Factory Records, Miscellaneous</em>, vol. viii. p. 577).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">All their new Eastern factories dissolved for want of money. Have also decided to close the establishment at Pulicat, on account of the heavy charges and want of profit. The Dutch have lost 300 <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-265" data-page="265" data-folio="226" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n264_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 226">[p. 226]</button> men at Macao, and have now fortified at the Pescadores. The Chinese deluded them, and they have little prospect of establishing a trade there. (<em>Extracts only. 1½ pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham, John Leachland, William Hill, and John Glanvill at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory, April</span> 17, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 310).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have now bought all Mahmūd Taqī’s indigo at Rs. 36 per bale, but must delay matters till they can receive the Rs. 15,000 from Surat to pay for it. For what else they buy they will arrange to have payment made in Surat. Find their credit here impaired, partly by the prevalent uncertainty, which renders men unwilling to part with money, and partly owing to some difficulties made at Surat over the payment of Mr. Heynes’s last bills. Have now received theirs of the 12th, and were disappointed that no arrangements had been made to supply them with money. Urge a speedy remittance. Are bargaining for a further parcel of indigo. Many conflicting rumours. There is ‘dayly expectacionn of the downefall of thatt villonous Prynce, whoe is gott into the montains and there resteth himselfe with a small company of soulders and accompaned onely with Conne Canna [the Khānkhānān] and Mozoffe Cann, Abdala Cann [and] Droabe Cann [<em>see</em> p. 112] beinge both slayne in a combatt with sertaine Rasboots [Rājpūts]<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-027" href="#sec-05-fn-027" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-027" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, wherein were dyvers men lost. Abdela Cann had revoulted to the Prynce, whereuppon hee had given him the govermentt of Guzarratt, whoe forthwith dispeeded heather a sarvantt of his<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-028" href="#sec-05-fn-028" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-028" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> to take possetionn, whoe is heere aryved with 200 horse and nott suffered to come into the cittye, because newes of his masters deth is come heather since his departure from him. This Raja<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-029" href="#sec-05-fn-029" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-029" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> is alsoe a-preparinge to bee gonn, and will resigne his govermentt to Merza Mena and Mamood Tuckey.’ Find they cannot get their piece-goods bleached here, and so must send them as they are. Mahmūd Taqī has just sent for his money; fear they may have to borrow it at interest, but will avoid doing so if possible. (<em>Copy. 2 pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-266"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="266" data-folio="227" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n265_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 227">[p. 227]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Robert Young and Justinian Offley at Broach to the Surat Factory, April</span> 29, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 312).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Inquire whether they shall buy some cotton yarn which has been offered them. The broad baftas come in but slowly. ‘Jaccobbeage’ [Yāqūb Beg, Deputy Governor of Broach] has not yet returned, but when he does they will press him to pay what he owes. The broker at Baroda writes that he has small hope of recovering the money due there without the help of an Englishman. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received April 30.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Thomas Rastell, Giles James, William Martin, and Joseph Hopkinson at Surat to the Pulicat Factory, April</span> 29, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Masulipatam</em>, vol. ix, p. 25).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Excuse this tardy reply to the Pulicat letter of November 14, 1622, which was not received till February 6. It is true that the Dutch have accused the English of not backing them up in the fight with the carracks; this probably proceeds from jealousy, for Captain Green and his company appear to have got more pillage than the Hollanders. As to the Ormus affair, it must be confessed that the action was ‘noe les vainly undertooke at first then frutlesslie succeed [<em>sic</em>] at last, besides the dishonour of supplanting a Christian to establesh a heathen, and God knowes what infinite domage by imbargoes in the Spanish dominions, with other inconveniences unthought on if the mach with Spaine take effecte’. They expect to hear soon how it is viewed in England. If the Company approve, the Persian factors have hopes that the natives will be obliged by lack of trade to perform the conditions, especially as the castle is not worth maintaining without English or Dutch support. As regards the troubles with the Hollanders at Pulicat, patience is the only remedy. Regret their inability to send a supply of money, for although their ‘projects on the junckes frome Mocha is confirmed’, they will want themselves all they can get, and besides have no ship to spare for conveyance. Have already explained to Mr. Methwold the delay in supplying Achin. Although the <em>Unity</em> missed Pulicat and could not therefore carry to Batavia the slaves provided, they are glad to say they have been able to furnish <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-267" data-page="267" data-folio="228" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n266_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 228">[p. 228]</button> some from Surat, having dispatched twenty-two on the <em>Discovery</em>, besides some sent on the Fleet of Defence. Repudiate indignantly the imputation of the Dutch ‘touching any private mens improvement by bootie’ [<em>sic</em>]. Now come to ‘the saddest messadg that ever of [<em>sic</em>] our handes put pen to. The <em>Whale</em> having, with hir ladinge of 476 bales of silke with 1,087 indicoe and other goods for England, receaved her dispatch on the 16th last month to accomepanie the <em>Doulphin</em> for Mocha, betwen 7 and 8 in the eveninge, when as yet she had not fullie bine five howers at most [?] under saile, at an instant become most miraculously shipwracke[d] in about 10 fadame water, to the utter losse both of shippe and goods, with 37 of hir people; which heavie accident, by examinacion of the cheefest that servive, they impute to some soddaine leake by the springing of a butt head.’ Two days later the <em>Dolphin</em> proceeded on her voyage alone, and she has been followed by the <em>Blessing</em> and <em>Reformation</em>. These two ships had previously been sent to Chaul and Dābhol. At the latter place they fetched away two frigates of value about 500<em>l.</em> or 600<em>l.</em>, but were rash enough to land, ‘with a vaine expectation of sacking the house of Aga Raza [Āghā Razā],’ and were beaten back with a loss of twenty of their number. (<em>Copy.</em> 1¾ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Francis Futter at Masulipatam to President Rastell at Surat, April 29, 1623</span> (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 449).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Thanks him for his letters, and sends such news as this place affords. The two junks intended for Mokha and Achin have lost their voyage, being found too leaky to start. ‘In the begininge of March here arived three frigatts under the comand of Signor Brusell [<em>cf.</em> p. 223], a freeman, who tooke near Tannasaree [Tenasserim] a Portingall frigatt of St. Thomay, worth by their owne reporte 50,000 ryalls of eight, and sente the goods for Battavia.’ Their commander has been forced to trim his vessels here, and, having taken no prize since, is obliged to sell his gold chain to buy provisions, &amp;c. Their voyage is declared to be for the Red Sea and the coast of Malabar, but the truth is not known. It is eight months since they left Batavia. ‘They have done ther country butt little creditt in cominge heather.’ Only one small junk has <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-268" data-page="268" data-folio="229" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n267_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 229">[p. 229]</button> arrived from Tenasserim this year, and she was fifty days on her passage. She reports that two others started before her, but nothing has been heard of them. ‘Cojah Nassann’ [Khwāja Nizām] is expected this year for [from?] Pegu, but no ships have yet arrived from thence and the monsoon is nearly over. Two days ago Mr. Mills advised ‘the arivall of Adames<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-030" href="#sec-05-fn-030" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-030" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> shipp at Tringambar’, and that he had sent a ‘pattamar’ express for news. The <em>Schiedam</em> left this place for Holland twelve days ago, laden chiefly with saltpetre, pepper, indigo, and longcloth. The Europe news to the disadvantage of the Dutch is declared by them to be false. Their warehouse at Sambas<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-031" href="#sec-05-fn-031" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-031" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> was burnt in February, 1622, and it is reported that goods to the value of 80,000 rials were destroyed. They have taken away all their people from that place, and it is expected that they will make war on any junks from thence. ‘The diamond mines is open, but as yett little seane, for in this place there cometh fewe, and them very small and deare and also fowle. The Hollanders here gives out great woards for great somes of mony that they mean to invest in them; and sente ten dayes past two Dutchmen, one a marchante, the other a lappadariea [i. e. a lapidary], to the mine to by what they cann meete with. Tis thought that makes them deare, as is the reason that so fewe comes heather.’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-032" href="#sec-05-fn-032" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-032" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> The <em>Schiedam</em> landed at Pulicat 60 bahars of pepper, which they sold to little profit. ‘Our people there adviseth us that 2,000 ryalls is the most they have in Pallicatt, and here not above a chest of money. This last yeare they [i. e. the Dutch] sente in three of their shipps for Battavia 2,000 slaves at the least. Wee looke out daly for a shipp for [from?] Battavia to relive our wants. Wee are so poore that wee shame to thinke of yt; this yeare no comodities in howse vendable. Our means is most in purslain, and in regard of the warrs betwene the Mogoll and the Prince is cause of such bade sale. It is reported for certaine that our people hath burnt Dabull and taken in the harbour two juncks, on bound for Damon, the other [for] Ormus; the occasion of abuse proferred by our people which came theather to buy <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-269" data-page="269" data-folio="230" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n268_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 230">[p. 230]</button> provitions and spicked upp their ordinance, for which they killed them [some?] of our people. This hapned about the 18 of February. There is a juncke of this place bond for Ormus.’ <em>P.S.</em>—The bearer desired a letter of recommendation. He should be used as Rastell sees fit. (<em>Copy. 1½ pp. Received November 18.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham, John Leachland, William Hill, and John Glanvill at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory, May 2, 1623</span> (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 314).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have received theirs of April 24. ‘Your needlese rebukes therein wee omitt to annser, being more fitter for boyes then men.’ Have also received the bill for Rs. 4,200 and paid the money to Mahmūd Taqī on account of his indigo; for the remainder due one of them must remain as hostage until a further remittance arrives. After much trouble they arranged that their caravan should start on the 5th; but now ‘all the adwayes [<em>see</em> p. 63] are taken and are in prysonn, where they are by the power of this newe governor keptt untill they furnish a certaine quantety of carttes for the Princes lasker or paye a some of mony towards the accomplyshinge of the same.’ When the matter is settled, all the merchants except Bangham will accompany the caravan. Await a further remittance from Surat. Cannot get any acceptable offer for the amber. The indigo merchants are still prohibited from dealing, and Mahmūd Taqī will not permit the sale of any or dispose of the Prince’s at present. <em>P.S.</em>—‘Heere is newes of the Kinges nere approch to Agmeere, and the Prynce beeinge in Mando.’ (<em>Copy. 1¼ pp. Received May 6.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Robert Young and Justinian Offley at Broach to the Surat Factory, May 8, 1623</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 315).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Request a reply to theirs of April 29. Mr. Martin should either send his accounts or come himself to clear them. Would be glad also of a priced invoice of the goods supplied to them. The debts are still unpaid. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received May 10.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-270"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="270" data-folio="231" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n269_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 231">[p. 231]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Thomas Mills and John Hunter at Pulicat to the Masulipatam Factory</span>, <span class="sc">May</span> 8, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Masulipatam</em>, vol. ix. p. 22).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">With grief of heart they announce the death of Mr. Milward, which occurred on the 6th after a brief illness. A Brahman undertook to cure him and commenced by giving him ‘Pau de China<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-033" href="#sec-05-fn-033" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-033" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>’, which made him feel so much better that he was very angry when Mr. Mills suggested he should make his will. By the next morning, however, he had lost the power of speech, and in about an hour and a half he passed away amidst the prayers of his colleagues. They imagine that his disease was ‘the malfrances<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-034" href="#sec-05-fn-034" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-034" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>’, but he was very reticent on the subject. They send an inventory of his effects and a note of his debts; his accounts they find to be in a very backward state. As soon as the ship comes his clothes, &amp;c., shall be sold aboard, according to custom. He was interred with some pomp, ‘for he was caried by 8 gentlemen of the campa, all in blacke, with a band of souldiers befor, marching with ther coulers trayled after and pickes, and at his putting into the grave 3 volles of smale shotte and betwene each volley a pees of ordinance.’ This loss has made the two remaining factors very busy. <em>Appended:</em> The aforesaid inventory of clothes, books, and other articles, with a list of debts. (<em>Copy. In all 3 pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham, John Leachland, William Hill, and John Glanvill at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">May</span> 13, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 317).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Bills received and accepted. Were obliged to borrow in order to pay Mahmūd Taqī. Recount their troubles in regard to the cartage of their goods. Ten of their carts were forcibly taken from them and their bales thrown on the highway; but they have since procured others and have got the caravan outside the city, where it awaits the carts from Sarkhej. The carters will go no further than Baroda, and others must be procured there, ‘all our adowiaes save <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-271" data-page="271" data-folio="232" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n270_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 232">[p. 232]</button> one beeinge still in prysonn and have received noe little punishmentt to furnish the Prynces sercar with cartts for the transpoortte of the tauckett [<em>see</em> p. 218] and Rajae to the Prynce wheresoever his unconstantt aboade shalbee, which is nowe readey to departt for Mando or Brampoortt; the newe Governor of Abdala Canns displaced, and the Raja in his former govermentt againe, and every greatte man heere is a governor, Suffecann beinge departed hence, noe man cann tell dyrectly wheather, whoe hath voyolently carryed with him our adowia Serjenacke [<em>see</em> p. 137] for nott speedely findinge him carrts to serve his sudaine wantt.’ Trust the caravan will be finally dispatched to-morrow, and that they will all meet at Surat before the rains. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p. Received May</em> 17.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Robert Young and Justinian Offley at Broach to the Surat Factory, May</span> 14, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 316).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have now completed the bargain for cotton yarn. The bulk of their goods have been dispatched this day under the charge of Thomas Aldworth. The Governor has partly discharged his debt. As regards that at Baroda, the Deputy-Governor promises payment ‘uppon the retourne of his fowsdar [Pers. <em>faujdār</em>, ‘a military commander’; here, the Governor] from his townes’. Martin’s accounts. <em>P.S.</em>—Have returned the green velvet. The prices of the goods have not been received. (<em>Copy.</em> ¾ <em>p. Received May</em> 15.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same, May</span> 15, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 317).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Their goods were stopped at Ankleswar for customs, the officers demanding 1,700 mahmūdīs for those previously passed. Request either an order from the Governor of Surat to pass the goods or directions to pay the amount demanded. They applied to the Customer of Surat, but he upheld the local officials. In the end they got these goods through on payment of 30 mahmūdīs to be left in the hands of the Customer pending a decision. Trust that an order will be obtained for the repayment of this sum. <em>P.S.</em>—An answer must be sent within six days or the money will be forfeited. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p. Received May</em> 17.)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-272"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="272" data-folio="233" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n271_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 233">[p. 233]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Matthew Duke at Masulipatam to President Rastell at Surat</span>, M<span class="sc">ay</span> 22, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 435).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">‘The Shabander [<em>Shāhbandar</em>, ‘ruler of the port’] of this place, named Coge Mahamad Cashim [Khwāja Muhammad Kāsim], who hath continued in this office (by reporte) 32 yeares, and now in the vacance of the Governor rules as Governor, and hath att all times bene a very good freind to the English’, has asked Duke to write in favour of a friend of his, named ‘Molla Mahamad Alee’ [Mullā Muhammad Alī], who is now in Surat and desires to proceed to Ormus. Expresses surprise at not hearing from Surat. They expect daily a ship from Batavia. The Dutch report that Mr. Milward is dead at Pulicat. ‘Wee remaine poore and idle for wante of imployment.’ (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received November 6.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory</span>, M<span class="sc">ay</span> 23, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 323).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Since his last letter, ‘Seffeecaun hath impresned all the Princes servants, espetially Mohamot Tuckee, and robbed his house of all that was in it, the indico of the Princes sould by armefulls, and happie hee that could carrey away most; Cambaia, Pettelan [Patan?], Doulke, and all such places beinge also searched [seized?] on for the Kinge; and when all at litle more quiett here, Surratt and Barroch wilbe next; all men here seeminge well content that the Kinge is ther kinge; the Raja fl[y]inge and leaveth his goods behinde him; the cajana [<em>see</em> p. 181] beinge sente for backe.’ Advises having drawn bills on Surat. Hopes soon to receive a remittance from them. <em>P.S.</em>—Trusts that their caravan is near Broach by this time, having left ‘Battaway’ [<em>see</em> p. 10] on the 16th. Mr. Leachland will deliver the invoice. (<em>Copy. ¾ p. Received May 27.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">John Leachland at Baroda to Robert Young at Broach</span>, M<span class="sc">ay</span> 24, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 321).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They arrived here with their ‘caffilo’ [caravan] on the 21st, and were refused leave to pass on, because the Prince has instituted a toll at this place. Leachland, however, went to the Rājā (who happened to be in the town) and obtained permission to depart <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-273" data-page="273" data-folio="234" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n272_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 234">[p. 234]</button> without payment. Next he applied to the Governor for the money owing to the English; whereupon ‘first hee put mee into the hands of horsemen who robbd mee of all that I had about mee, to say, my shash [turban], my girdle, my rings, my sword, tore my coate from my backe, drue their daggrs at mee, drue blood of mee (but how great the wound is, as yett I know not). After they delivered mee, with Choute [Chhotā] and Mutterdas [Mathura Dās] into the hands of the Topagee [Hind. <em>topchī</em>, ‘an artillery officer’], [who?] th[reat]earned to throw me to his eliphants, brought roppes to hange mee up by the heels, with often shewing us the chawbucke [Hind. <em>chābuk</em>, ‘a whip’], demandinge on lacke of rupp[ee]s for the paiment of his soalders with many reprochfull words, beate mee with their fists and hilts of their swords that at present I am not myselfe, thertned to robb our caphilo: all which I indured with paticence [<em>sic</em>], and (by means used) had now and then answere from him conser[n]inge agreement and taken up of this busines. So fallinge from one lacke to 50,000, and so lower and lower, which I still refused to pay a pice, till such time the people of the towne tould mee it was paid by all that pased that way, being latly ordered by the Prince, and hath not beine but within these 20 dayes; soe att last I was faine to agree with him for 500 m[ahmūdīs], which hee seemed as yett discontent withall. Wee are now alooking after money, which is here hard to be gotten, having charged Amadavaz with 200 rupp[ee]s to cleare our goods one the way, as wassett att chouckees<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-035" href="#sec-05-fn-035" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-035" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>.’ Spent all their money on the road and have been forced to borrow of ‘bannyan weomen that comes alonst with us’. Hope to make a fresh start to-day. ‘Here is like to arise great troubles, the Kings lasker beinge within 20 course of this place with foure thousand horse, and Bobcaun hath incompassed Fully beagree<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-036" href="#sec-05-fn-036" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-036" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>, who hath shutt himselfe up in Deboys [Dabhoi] castle; soe this cannott hould out longe ere all comes into the Kings hands, and then I hope after a great [deal?] of trouble wee shall injoye some ease.’ As the ways are so dangerous they have engaged some more peons. <em>P.S.</em>—Requests that Mr. Rastell <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-274" data-page="274" data-folio="235" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n273_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 235">[p. 235]</button> may be informed, or this letter forwarded to him. Will write to him presently, as they are forced to draw on Surat for money. (<em>Copy. 1½ pp. Received May 25.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Giles James at Surat to [President Rastell at Swally]</span>, <span class="sc">May 24, 1623</span> (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 322).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Forwards two letters, one from Mr. Duke and the other from the Persian ambassador at Golconda. These were brought by a German who has arrived from Masulipatam. ‘The currant newes in this towne is that 6,000 horse (parte of the great army spoaken of before your departure) is come into Amadavaz and that both the Governors there placed by Abdilacaun and the Governor of Cambaia, &amp;c., are in irons. The Governor of this towne is so bused that wee cannot have admitance to speake with him, and itts gennerall[y] thought that within 3 or 4 dayes hee will take his journy towards Croad<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-037" href="#sec-05-fn-037" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-037" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>.’ Will endeavour to obtain the money due from him before his departure. ‘Vanmallidas [Vanmālī Dās] is come to towne and hath brought withim on of the English youths longe since taken by the Grassceeas [Grāsiās: <em>see previous volume</em>, p. 299] of Raj people [Rājpipla]. I have spocken with on of the Turkes which is come from Share [Shehr, on the coast of Arabia], who telleth mee that before his cominge thence hee received a letter from Adenn which advised him of sertine English shipps which had surprised juncks, wherof on belonging to Dabull in the porte of Mocha, the merchants all beinge gone on shore, but where the other two were taken or to what place they Helonge hee saith hee was not advised. It was two months since the Dabull juncke was taken, which sheweth directlie that they could not be but the English, unlesse some of the Fleete of Defence. Hee saith that the pretence wherefore the foresaid juncke was taken was conser[n]inge certaine English which were the yeare before kept prisonours in Adenn, and that they offered to surrender her againe conditionally that the people might bee delivered unto them. Hee likewise affermith that the Dutch in Mocha are againe in prison, and that 12,000 were come within Mocha to defend the towne.’ <em>P.S.</em>—Has just received the enclosed from Mr. Bangham. (<em>Copy. 1 p. Received May 25.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-275"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="275" data-folio="236" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n274_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 236">[p. 236]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2">NICHOLAS BANGHAM AT AHMADĀBĀD TO THE SURAT FACTORY, <span class="sc">May</span> 25, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 329).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Refers to previous letters received and sent. Returns a bill of exchange which has not been accepted. Hopes the caravan has reached Broach by this time. Will have the contents of the house appraised, and will bring a list with him. Can get no one to take charge of the premises; so will lock everything in one room and leave two peons in charge, at the same time asking the shroffs to help the peons in case of any troubles. ‘Yesternight came to Suffecann two fermaunds, one from the Kinge, the other from Sultann Blockee, Cossoras sonne, who is upon the way heather with a greatt leskar, accompanied with Cann Assame, Mocrobockcanne [Muqarrab Khān] and 22 omrowst [<em>see</em> p. 76 <em>n.</em>] of the best rancke, which are to atende the younge Prince; which newes is sertaine, for the preperations here are such as if the Kinge himselfe were cominge, and lookinge out allredy for houses to entertaine that laskar. Here wilbe a great courte and this cuntry in flurishinge estate againe, all people much rejoysinge at this newes. Sultan Perwes with 80,000 horse followeth Corome so fast that hee is within 20 course of Mando; the King in Ajemer.’ <em>P.S.</em>—Will draw upon them at his departure for any money necessary for the payment of debts here. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p. Received June</em> 1.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">JOHN LEACHLAND, WILLIAM HILL, AND JOHN GLANVILL AT ‘SALLADE’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-038" href="#sec-05-fn-038" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-038" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> TO THE SURAT FACTORY, <span class="sc">May</span> 27, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 323).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Recount the ill-treatment they suffered at Baroda. After the payment of the 500 mahmūdīs, the Governor forced from them 570 more. Next day ‘Beleleconn<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-039" href="#sec-05-fn-039" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-039" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>’ arrived with 500 horse, and took possession of the town. Upon their complaint he promised to see their money restored in a few days. They have visited him twice since without getting it, but he has promised to have it paid to their broker. He has also given them his parwāna and a guard to Broach.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-276"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="276" data-folio="237" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n275_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 237">[p. 237]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have drawn on Surat for 1,286 mahmūdīs. ‘The formerly Governor with all his cheifest men are in irons, and kept with a great watch over them, now tasting of the same sauce they gave us. Here is newse of Lalabazines <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-040" href="#sec-05-fn-040" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-040" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> sonns approch nere unto Amadavaz (if not arived) with 27 thousand horse to furnish all places of Guzeratt withall. The Governor of Brothera which now is hath sente for 4,000 horse from Amadavaz, which Saphe Cann hath promised to send forthwith, for the takinge in of Barroch and Surrat, which cannott well yett arive this six dayes.’ Suggest that the factors should get all their goods away from Surat and ‘lye without’. Have just reached this place. (<em>Copy.</em> 3 <em>pp. Received May</em> 28.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Giles James at Surat to President Rastell [at Broach]</span>, <span class="sc">May</span> 28, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 327).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Regrets to learn that the caravan has been detained at Baroda, but hopes it has since been released. ‘These fellowes are growne ripe; I hope wee shall have the shakinge of them.
