On Tusday last wee had newes here of the Rajaes coming to Barroch and of the Merzas resolution to maintaine the towne against the Kinges offecers; which doubtlesse will prove the ruine both of him and his brother. Itt seemeth hee doth thinke they are so farr allredy ingaged that now over shooes, over boots, as the ould proverb is.’ Fears the money due from them will be lost. Two bills of exchange from Mr. Bangham have been presented and accepted. Requests that Mr. Hopkinson may be sent back to Surat to help in arranging for their payment. Forwards second bills for the last remittance to Ahmadābād. Two bills to be recovered from a native banker now at Broach. Since writing last he has not seen the German who brought the letters [from Masulipatam], and probably he is lodging with the Dutch; but when he comes he shall have the best entertainment the house affords. Yesterday the Governor of Surat took his journey towards Ankleswar to meet the Rājā, and to-day Mr. Hutchinson has gone after him to get ‘his choppe [<em>see</em> p. 91] to the burratt [<em>see</em> p. 67] for Mr. Darrill his mony’. Is doing his <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-277" data-page="277" data-folio="238" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n276_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 238">[p. 238]</button> best to recover what is due from the Governor to the Company. Will endeavour to obtain further news about the English ships at Mokha. (<em>Copy. 1¼ pp. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same</span>, <span class="sc">May</span> 29, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 326).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Is sorry to hear of their friends’ trouble with the caravan. As the rains seem imminent, he suggests that the goods be housed at Broach. The Governor of Surat is expected back to-day, and James will then apply to him for warehouse room at ‘Rannell’; if this be denied, they must manage at Surat. Sends some wine, arrack, and ‘ananasses’ (Guj. <em>annanās</em>, ‘a pine-apple’). (<em>Copy. 1 p. Received May 30.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">[<span class="sc">Thomas Mills</span>] <span class="sc">at Pulicat to Matthew Duke at Masulipatam</span>, [<span class="sc">Early in June</span>], 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Masulipatam</em>, vol. ix. p. 27).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Acknowledges the receipt of his letter of May 9. Had doubted the accuracy of the report of the loss of the <em>Whale</em>, but a few days since ‘Signor Martine’ [<em>see</em> p. 120] received letters from Masulipatam with a full account of the disaster. As regards the investment, up to the present they have received 907 bales of goods from the native merchants. As, however, the latter owed them 3,000 pagodas (besides at least 7,000 to the Dutch) they recently seized their persons and kept them prisoners for sixty hours, until their friends guaranteed the satisfaction of the debts within twenty days. Mills had expected to better the quality of the goods this year, but finds it just the opposite; wherefore his hopes of Pulicat are declining, and he heartily wishes the Company were freed from ‘theis unspeakable charges’. Has been obliged to ask the forbearance of the Dutch for this month’s charges, amounting to 403 rials, as he had no funds and was unable to borrow in the town. ‘Mallayo’ has already lent 1,200 pagodas. Requests therefore an early supply of money. Disposal of Milward’s effects. Yesterday the Dutch executed two native Christians, the one a soldier’s wife, and the other ‘a Gentishe youth’, accused of ‘coupelinge’. The authorities were urged to defer punishment, but refused. This was the effect of Coen’s new law. (<em>Copy. Imperfect. 2 pp. Received June 14.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-278"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="278" data-folio="239" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n277_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 239">[p. 239]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory</span>, JUNE 12, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 332).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">His departure has been prevented by ‘the unexpected aproch of Abdela Caun with 4,000 horse, accompanied with Hemote and some fewe others, who are now incamped at Mamadabaud [Mehmadābād, 17 miles south of Ahmadābād]; and Suffe Caun with all his company salled out of this cittye some five dayes since, and have incamped themselves some four course of from this place; who have joyned togeather 20,000 horse and many footemen, purposing to give Abdela Caun battell when he doth intend to march towards this cittye; who as yett lyeth still, beinge somewhat dismayed at the great army these people have gott togeather. The arivall of the lasker of Abdela Cauns hath strock such a feare amonst these people that everyone seeketh how to shift for himselfe, the citty gates are now shutt upp, and no man suffred to passe in or out one horsebacke; and the wayes are so full of robbers that no man can passe; the one halfe of Cambaia beinge come heather for refuge, and all the country nere ad[j]oyninge. What the event wilbe, God only knoweth, Suffe Caun fermly protesting that if the Prince Corome himselfe do come, hee shalbe resisted untill the cominge of Caun Asame and that lasker, which is daly expected. Both the lesker of Suffe Caun [and] Abdala Caun are within five course of each other; and those of Abdela Cauns parte seekes meanes how to secure themselves, and would faine fall into the King’s service if fairly the[y] could.’ Confusion in Ahmadābād. The King’s army is said to number 30,000 horse, and reinforcements are expected from Māndū shortly. ‘Corome wanteth ayde to helpe himselfe, Sultan Perwes with 50,000 horse foll[ow]inge him one that side; so that now his time, I hope, will not bee longe.’ Scarcity of money here. Their shroff entreats that Rs. 1,000 may be made over from Surat in payment of what is due to him. <em>P.S.</em>—Hears that a caravel has arrived at Goa from Portugal with news ‘that the marridge of Spayne and England is efected, and many good conditions of peace concluded, with liberty for our shipps to goe for Goa or any other porte of [the] Kinge of Spaines in India. This and more here is certanly here reported to me by a merchante of Goa, who <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-279" data-page="279" data-folio="238" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n278_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 238">[p. 238]</button> best to recover wh<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">…</span> Will endeavour to <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">…</span> Mokha. (<em>Copy.</em> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">…</span></p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to</span></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Surat</em>, vol. cii. p.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Is sorry to he the rains seem Broach. The James will th if this be don arrack, and . <em>Received Ma</em></p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">[THOMAS LIPATAM, [ vol. ix. p.</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Acknow the accur since ‘Si with a f to the [ mercha (beside perso guara had . it ju: and spe the ha ha of e c ` <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-280" data-page="280" data-folio="241" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n279_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 241">[p. 241]</button> one his face; of which the whole citty rejoiced. So that all the captains that Abdela Caun brought with him ar slane and taken alive, himselfe sore wonded and fleed with the fragment of his company no man knoweth wheather; he having lost in this bloudy conflict nere upon 3,000 of his best soulders. This lasker hath lost 7 or 800 men and divers hurte, but no man of note slaine. Both the cheife captains, Suffe Caun and Nahar Caun, received some hurtte in their faces. The heads of Himott Caune and Sale Beage, with some 200 heads of the cheifest, are brought into the cittye, and a pillar shalbe erected and filled full of them in the cheife markett place. This battell beinge ended and the enimy fleed, this lasker hath retired into the citty, entringe in with great pompe, bringinge all their prisoners with them, all the cheefe streetes being hanged with velvett and other good things to entertayne these victours, and the bannyans everyone presenting them with somewhat, giving them many applawses and shouts, as if they had delivered them from bondage; and to say the truth myselfe in hart rejoyced with them for the delivery from soe tiranicall a crue; which is thought as yett nott to bee ended, for report saith that Jado Raja [Jādū Rājā] with 4,000 horse of supply is come from Mando and arived in Brothera, whether Abdela Caun is supposed to bee fleed, either to retourne with those forces heather or to march towards Broch and there to fortife themselves, or elce to spoyle and robbe all the country that way, seeinge little hopes hee hath here, and so to retourne to their distresed master . . . These two captains will salley out againe, who entred now to refresh themselves and people, having layne this five dayes abroad in the rayns, not beinge prepared for such wether. The spoyle of this overthrown armey laye open to all men, for horses, elephant, camells, chists, tents or whatsoever was given to them that could first lay hould therof, which was not allitle. Since the writing of this it is reported that Abdela Caun is dead in his flight, beinge sore wounded with a lance. Upon the 19 presente will enter the cittye 7,000 horse of the Kings lasker, it beinge the daye of the new moone, conducted by Caun Asomes sonne and Merza Sadalee [<em>see</em> p. 244], the young Prince coming after so fast as the weather will give him leave. Then I hope all will be quiett, for as yett no man travelleth that way.’ (<em>Copy. 1½ pp. Received June 28.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-281"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="281" data-folio="242" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n280_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 242">[p. 242]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">William Martin at Broach to President Rastell at Surat</span>, June 17, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 330).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wrote last on the 13th [<em>not extant</em>], ‘since which time here hath bene and is great alteration of that which was before. Abdela Caun, with such smale force as he had of the Princes, not fearinge the great power of the Kings at Amadavaz, approched neer to it about Serques, where Abdela babb [Caun?]<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-041" href="#sec-05-fn-041" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-041" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> with some 15,000 horse incountred him and soone gave him the overthrough. In this fight Hamett Caun, Surge Caun [Sharzeh Khān?] and Salley Beage were slayne. And Mussiffe Caun intendinge to goe to searve the Kinge, as reporte was given to Abdela Caun, hee sente 500 of his soulders to his tente in the night [and?] there made away with him. Aucob Beage [Yāqūb Beg] was hurte, and Merga Mahamoodiery [Mīrzā Muhammad Yār] lost his horse under him and wente to save himselfe in the feilds, where beinge seene by some of his company lente him a horse; who came into the towne yesterday with Abdela Caun, and with them about 2,000 horse. He lieth at Cojanasans, [Khwāja Nizām’s] and his soulders and horses where they cann gett place. He intendeth to goe to Hamett Cauns house, so soone as their mourning is a littel overpast. These peopel here are in great feare least they should bee robbed of what they have by him to pay his soulders, who are come very poore, having lost their cassanna [<em>see</em> p. 181], and came away in hast to save their lives. It should seeme they weare not longe in cominge, for that I received a letter this instant from Mr. Banggam, dated the 12th, who adviseth nothing of this fight; but Caun Assane was expected to come with 30,000 horse. What will follow, a little more time will shewe it; but wee thinke that the greates[t] heate is past for ought the Prince cann doe now, for wante of meanes, having had great losses by the taking of his cassanna. If the Kings people should not follow after Abdela Caun and that hee should remaine here these raynes it would not bee amisse to give him some presente, that wee might not be troubled with him nor his. The Dutch, as wee lerne, hath bene with him and given him a presente. Wee intend to vissitt him and the Meza [Mīrzā].’ Suggests that <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-282" data-page="282" data-folio="243" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n281_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 243">[p. 243]</button> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">one his inc: r......</span> to pay what he <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">captains for......</span> <span class="sc">p</span>. <em>Received</em> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">alive, himself......</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">company......</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">conflict:......</span> <em><span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">bid</span>., p.</em> 330).</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">; or for......heard</span> that ‘Mossoff <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">the rhefe......</span>ul is now at Ahmad<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">hurne in......ris</span> still ‘full of greeife <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">with......</span>e unto Suffy Caun that <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">and a......</span> receve whom hee would <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">markett......</span> towne, and that hee and <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">this laste......</span>uld have done this sooner. <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">bringing......</span>ell. Wee here the Kings <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">hanged......</span>towne and some for another, <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">victorie......</span>d but that the raynes letteth, <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">what, gi......dera</span> [Baroda] and this towne. <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">deliverd......</span> [Ankleswar] and the Merga <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">hart enjoy......</span> not.’ No business can be done <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">which it thoug......</span> (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p. Received June</em> 23.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Hill at Broach to the Company</span>, <em>June</em> 20, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 334).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">Jado Ray......</span>ondence. ‘Mierga Mahamood’ quitted <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">March ast......fter</span> reclaiming the silver plate he had <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">to be fot......og</span>; and has gone to Surat, where he is <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">toward Bro......ing</span> for the money owing. ‘Luke Beage’ <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">and rotie......mpanies</span> him, promises also to discharge his <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">feet, ad......</span> has been received on account of what is <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">two capt......Caun’</span>; so application should be made to the <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">themelve......uld</span> at Surat. ‘Wheras wee advised you of <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">rayn or d......usuf</span> Beg] coming to keepe this towne, he came <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">mertheum......nge</span> to the watter side from Uncleseire with his <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">Chou......horses</span>; but the inhabitance would not suffer him <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">head that......aused</span> all the boattes to staye on this side, and they <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">It is report......cept</span> the towne for the Kinge; who caused good <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">now at Bro......ward</span> to be keept till they here from Amadavaz, where <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">of it pr......written</span> to Suffe Caun &amp;c. Abdela Caun wee here is <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-283" data-page="283" data-folio="244" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n282_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 244">[p. 244]</button> come from thence with 4,000 horse. Part hee hath sente to Brothera; himselfe with the rest is gone to his country aboute Dabois. When hee hath settled his busines there, it is thought hee will come to this towne. Reporte goeth that Caun Assum and Sultan Curserods sonne is come to Amadavaz with 25,000 horse… Here is no busines att all done this troubelsome time.’ <em>P.S.</em>—Offley and Glanvill arrived here last evening. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p. Received June</em> 27.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">July</span> 3, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 340).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">‘This presente morninge entred this cittie Sultan Blockee, of 15 years of age, accompanied with his father in lawe, Cann Asame and his owne sonne<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-042" href="#sec-05-fn-042" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-042" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, with Merza Abedala<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-043" href="#sec-05-fn-043" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-043" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>, newly adopted of 5,000 horse for his vallare in slayinge the Rajae in a combatt neare Agra, which you have formerly heard of. These be all the men of noate that are com as yett; who brought with them a poore lasker, at the most five thousand horse, meanly accootromented and in very small pompe, and so farr as I perceave but a smalle cajana [<em>see</em> p. 181] with them to sustaine any present warrs. These men are to have their jageares in this province, but how they are disposed of as yett I cannott learne. Some say that Surratt and Barroch are both Caun Asomes when conqured. After a fewe daies of settlement all wilbe knowne, and forces disperced to all parts, wherby the wayes may be fitt to travell, for as yett no man goeth out of this cittye that way; which will cause mee to stay a little longer, wherby I may with safety take my passage towards you.’ Begs a few lines by the bearer to say how matters stand at Surat. Thinks the jewels should not be sent to this place, as there is no one here to buy such things. ‘The sending of the young Prince and ould gentlleman so slightly from the Kinge is by some conjectered to bee a ploott of that vealinous strompitt Nahar Malle [Nūr Mahal], whose continuall present in the Kings view, togeather with the ould mans inestigatinges the King to persewe that Corome, did often cause the Kinge to call to mind the death of his murthered sonne; which gave him much distast, and shee receaving a touch therof <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-284" data-page="284" data-folio="245" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n283_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 245">[p. 245]</button> Mr. Young should come <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">away</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">and</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">rest</span> content owes. Mr. Hill has recovered some money, as here <em>June</em> 18.) at Ajemer the Lord</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same, June</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">23, 1623</span> <span class="sc">(Factory</span></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Refers to his previous letter. The Prince will be no Caun’ was not killed in <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">the fight</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">…</span> them shortly ābād. Articles remain unsold <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">…</span> Caun is in his and sorrow’. Two horses presented <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">…</span> Asaf <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">Khān</span> came hath sett the gates of this town against the Prince. ‘The broker send in the name of the Company. <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">…</span> been very ill but his are ready at Mandu. (<em>Copy. Extract only. 1 p.</em>) (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 342).</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">if hee would have allowed provision</span> of ‘Sultan Blockee, <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">lasker are dispersinge, one</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">…</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">Baidmān</span>] his sonne, and <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">to place new Governors in the</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">…</span> lefte the greatest parte <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">they would not be longe for them</span> charge of Rustom Caun <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">Abdela Cam is yet att Tremmucke</span> and Caun Izane [Khān <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">is here, but for what time God knoweth</span> 9,000 horse. What these <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">until some settlement is settled</span> Surratt is not yett certainly Caun and Nahar Caun shalbee</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">William Martin and Other Factors to the</span> they have begone, and shall <span class="sc">Surat Factory, June</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">28, 1623 (Factory</span> we formerly entertayned. Some</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">Refers to previous letters</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">…</span> the Prince nor Caun Asome as yet <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">the town in the captain’s</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">…</span> To-day they sent for him, but <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">entrusted to their keeping</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">…</span> und of ill-health. Will give them <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">to account with the factors</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">…</span> ary. There is little chance of their <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">[Lālā Beg]</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">…</span> as they need their money for paying <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">debt then. Setting</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">…</span> p. 218] beinge allready puled in peeces <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">due from Nasīr</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">…</span> uld thereof served to pay those soulders <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">Mīrzā to the town</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">…</span> heard from Broach of the Mīrzā’s sudden <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">English bug</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">…</span> hants there will hold out against any move- <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">…</span> dela Cauns destreesed estate stands wavoring, <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">company’s goods</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">…</span> hath little refuge lefte in this country, either <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">to compile their accounts</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">…</span> <em>P.S.</em>—Has just received theirs of June 30, <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">impossible to</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">…</span> the first opportunity of joining them. (<em>Copy. 15.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-285"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="285" data-folio="246" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n284_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 246">[p. 246]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3">WILLIAM MARTIN AT BROACH TO THE SURAT FACTORY, JULY 7, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 339).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Debts of Himmat Khān and his brother. Have forwarded a horse, in obedience to their orders. ‘Merga Mamoodia’ [<em>see</em> p. 242] refused to pay for the goods he had, on the ground that they were a ‘pishcash’ [<em>see</em> p. 10]. Are glad to hear that they are now in cash again. ‘This morning is come into the towne, sent from Suffe Caun, under the charge of on of his cheife soulders, Mellickbackee [Malik Bakhshī], 500 horse and 400 foote men; and 400 horse more are expected to come within these two dayes. Those soulders which were here before of Abdelababbes [<em>cf.</em> p. 242] had their pay of the townes men and are retourned to the place from whence they came. Chan Azam, Sultan Curseroos sonne and two others, noblemen of the Kinges, are come to Amadavaz with 12,000 horse, and more are expected to come there verey shortly. Abdela [<em>sic</em>] Chan and Suffe Caun are to come this way with 10,000 horse. They intend to sett forth of Amadavaz at the newe moune. From hence they intend to goe to Surratt. Att ther coming there wilbee a governor appointed for this towne, where wee hope althinges wilbe settled in peace.’ Send a note of money expended in repairing their house, which should be deducted when paying the landlord his rent. (<em>Copy. 1 p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">THOMAS MILLS AT MASULIPATAM TO THE SURAT FACTORY, JULY 7, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 337).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Received their letter of April 29 on June 6. Condoles with them on the loss of the <em>Whale</em>, and the repulse at Dābhol. ‘The shipp <em>Rubie</em> arrived att Pallacatt the 18th June, bringinge order for the desoulvinge of this factory [i. e. Pulicat], the reasons wherfore you shall understand by the Presidents letter more att large. From the said Pallacatt I speeded this shipp <em>Rubie</em> the prime July and raised that factory, and there imbayled 137 baylls cloth, being woven and paintings, which now suppose, as the case stands, will little advance our masters, in regard of the disoulving of all the factories in the Mollucas, beinge clothes most parte not vendable in other places, except Bantam when open (whereof there is some <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-286" data-page="286" data-folio="247" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n285_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 247">[p. 247]</button> hope). The 4th July wee by Gods providence wee safly arived in this road of Musulapatnam. The cargazon this shipp hath brought, the inclosed copie of our invoyce will make mention; from which I have drawne, to pay debts, 5,000 rials, Mr. Duke for the like occasion att the least 12,000 rials; wherby you may neere calculate the poore capitall wee shall have remayninge. Mr. Duke and Mr. Futter are appointed to retourne upon this shipp, and myselfe yt hath pleased the Presedent and the Counsell to instabulate to succed, contrary to all imagination of myne, but seing they have ben pleased to putt yt upon mee, I will with Gods assistance endeavor and strive to performe my best service. Mr. Dodd appointed my secound, of whom there is a hopfull expectation, being a younge man [of?] farely carridg and of sevile goverment. Pray God blese us both, that wee may merritt our preferment.’</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">For other news refers to the President’s letter. Encloses a note from Mr. Methwold, who has left Batavia for England in the <em>Palsgrave</em>. ‘The Dutch Generall Coen is gone for Holland with five shipps in company. Hee shewed his royalty in partinge by his large donations, presenting our Presedent and the rest merchants, every one accordinge to his degree, with chaines of gould and diamonds, amounting to a great some of mony in that kinde given.’ He also gave presents of less value to the commanders of ships, the captains of the soldiers, and the principal inhabitants of Batavia. The President has censured Mr. Duke for ‘his humerous ussadge of Mr. Methwould, whom by all reports hee hath most intolerrabley injured, verified by those that have had ther shars in the like kinde. He is a man to mee unknowne not above three dayes, butt seeming in this small experience to be of an unconstant and pevish dispossition.’ The Dutch are expecting a ship with money to pay their debts, ‘having takinge upp at the least 12,000 pagothes at intrest to invest in diamonds, for which purpose they have kept a factor about 2½ months at the mines, where imployed not above 5,000 pagothes, and bought att verey deare rattes, themselves being the occasion therof. The mine is yett open, and thought hereafter they wilbe better cheape, in regarde the Dutch have no money. If supply come not for them within fewe dayes they must be faine to use more their creditts for the furnishing of Pallicatt for payment of their garrison, where I know they are worne threed bare. They <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-287" data-page="287" data-folio="248" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n286_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 248">[p. 248]</button> have longe since expected a shipp to arrive from Holland, and remaine yett hopefull therof, but it is to be feared their hopes will prove frutles.’ The <em>Ruby</em> will probably be dispatched for Batavia by the middle of September, and will take any letters that may arrive from Surat for transmission to that factory. (<em>Copy. 2½ pp. Received August 5.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Matthew Duke, Francis Futter, and John Dod at Masulipatam to the Surat Factory, July 8, 1623</span> (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 356).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Received theirs of April 29 on May 29, but have retained the messenger until now, hoping to be able to send news from Batavia. In the meantime they have allowed him three cash<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-044" href="#sec-05-fn-044" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-044" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> [per diem?]. Now forward a letter from the President and Council at Batavia addressed to Surat [<em>see</em> p. 223], which they have opened and copied, with the intention of sending the transcript by another messenger in two days time. Account of the stock received by the <em>Ruby</em>, consisting of benzoin, camphor, and porcelain from Japan, tutenague, nutmegs, sandalwood, and silver plate; total, 34,543 [rials]. Express their thanks for the payment of one of their bills of exchange, and ask that inquiry may be made regarding the other. Regret the loss of the <em>Whale</em>. Pulicat factory withdrawn. Messrs. Duke and Futter return with this ship to Batavia, and thence, they hope, to England by the first opportunity. Mr. Methwold has gone home in the <em>Palsgrave</em>. <em>P.S.</em>—The <em>Ruby</em> will sail not later than September 15. (<em>Copy. 1 p. Received August 15.</em><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-045" href="#sec-05-fn-045" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-045" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">William Martin and Justinian Offley at Broach to the Surat Factory, July 14, 1623</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 341).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have received the 20,000 mahmūdīs. Return a few to be exchanged for better. Will follow their instructions in the investment of this sum, but little can be done until a new Governor is appointed. Wages paid to Messrs. Hill, Offley, Aldworth, and Martin. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received July 20.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-288"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="288" data-folio="249" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n287_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 249">[p. 249]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">July</span> 16, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 344).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has been ill, but is now recovering. ‘Newse here is none, save Agga Nohar [Āghā Nūr] yeasterday arived from courte, accompanyed with 400 horse; who brought with him searpaus [Hind. <em>sarāpā</em>, ‘a dress of honour’] and horses for all these well deserving captains, most espetially for Nahar Caun, whose jager is Baroch, alotted him by the Kinge himselfe, as here reported; who purposeth after the feast of this next new moone either to goe or send, some saye 5,000 horse; [with] which company, [if] God giveth mee abillitye of body, I purpose to take my pasage. This younge Prince and Caun Asame as yett doe nothing, butt referr all matters of corratte <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-046" href="#sec-05-fn-046" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-046" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> to Suffe Caun, who now is not of expeditions as formerly. Cambaya is Nohar Mahalls, and shee hath consigned the government thereof to Suffe Caun, who hath sente theather to take posetion in her behalfe.’ (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p. Received July</em> 28.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">William Martin and Justinian Offley at Broach to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">July</span> 26, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 343).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have given out to their brokers 4,800 mahmūdīs for ‘necanees’ [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 51] and 7,200 for broad baftas. Intend also to provide narrow baftas, but as yet few are available. Request authority to accept an offer of Rs. 15,000, to be repaid in Surat. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p. Received July</em> 27.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">John Willoughby at Gandevī to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">July</span> 28, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 345).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Arrived here last night with his companions. This morning went to see ‘Gopollvosie’ [<em>see</em> p. 212] and gave him the present brought for that purpose. He received it very kindly, and promised any assistance in his power. Thereupon Willoughby inquired what news there was regarding the wrecked ship. His answer was that two masts had come ashore, as he had already informed the English President at Surat. Willoughby then asked whether anything else <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-289" data-page="289" data-folio="250" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n288_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 250">[p. 250]</button> had been recovered. He replied that at a place called ‘Otta’ two chests had come ashore, with some silk and some beef; but these goods, which were all rotten, had been carried off by the ‘Mochadam’ [<em>see</em> p. 125], whose name was ‘Surgenate’ [Surgī Nāth]. Also a pipe of arrack and a bale of tobacco had been found at ‘Bullshar’ [Balsār], and these the Kotwāl of that place had taken. He declared on oath that he knew of no more goods recovered; so Willoughby contented himself with asking for one of his servants to go with him to Otta to assist him in his search. Intends to start to-morrow. Would be glad to know what should be done with the masts. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">THE SAME TO PRESIDENT RASTELL AT SURAT, JULY 28, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 344).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Believes the President will do him no wrong, although he has been informed otherwise. Hopes his accounts will be well examined, for he protests he has not defrauded the Company of a single pice. Never heard before of a consultation being called to go through any one’s accounts, ‘but ytt semeth my account hath bene larger then any man’s elce, or rather the cause of the mans, elce beloved of all, but homarra comboct’ [<em>see</em> p. 196]. Has no doubt that if the President weighs the matter impartially, he will have a charitable opinion of him. Refers to his general letter for news. Requests permission to come to Surat, ‘in regard the shister [ships?] are now neere approching, and I alltogeather unprovided for my voyage; and againe my accounts are not perfect. Robert Tottell hath the langauge, and Mr. Billings is verey sufficiente.’ If he cannot be allowed to return, he begs that a bottle of good wine may be sent him, ‘for now the time requireth to rusncha [<em>sic</em>].’ <em>P.S.</em>—Sends such fruit as he could procure, namely, two ‘annacasses’ [annanasses? <em>see</em> p. 238]. His companions beg to be remembered to the President. (<em>Copy.</em> ¾ <em>p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">JOHN WILLOUGHBY AT ‘OTTA’ TO THE SURAT FACTORY, JULY 30, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 346).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Arrived here yesterday, and went to the ‘Coulla’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-047" href="#sec-05-fn-047" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-047" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, who is accused of having received the goods which came ashore. He, however, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-290" data-page="290" data-folio="251" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n289_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 251">[p. 251]</button> stoutly denied the charge. Has this day been to ‘Boucha’, ‘just against our lost shipp,’ and found planks, yards, and other wreckage on the shore, but all the ironwork had been carried away. One of the masts is at ‘Sumarpore’ and the other at ‘Dandepoore’ [<em>see</em> p. 211], but he could not get at them because the water was so high. Has sent eight men to these and other places on the coast, and intends to-morrow to collect all the wreckage together. Believes this ‘Coulla’ knows all about the goods which have come ashore, but will not confess. ‘Thereof they say to the Guzz[er]ats Pilla latte and pecher botte.’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-048" href="#sec-05-fn-048" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-048" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Suggests that a parwāna be obtained from the Governor of Surat to the ‘Crowery’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-049" href="#sec-05-fn-049" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-049" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> of Nosārī to send the said mukaddam to Surat. The parwāna should be forwarded to him at ‘Boucha’, and he will then see to its delivery. <em>P.S.</em>—Requests that someone may be sent to him who can write Persian, to help in corresponding with the native officials. (<em>Copy. 1 p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Edward Heynes and Richard Lancaster at Mokha to the Surat Factory</span>, August 3, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 375).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Write but briefly, as they hope shortly to follow and overtake the <em>Blessing</em>, which brings this letter. They arrived here in the <em>Dolphin</em> on May 9, and found many alterations. ‘The Dutch still in prison, their goods attached and taken for the Bashawe, and the Principall inforced by feare of death to give his writinge of 40,000 rials more to bee paid att the Bashawes pleasure; the ould Governor, Allia Agar (our ever trustie freind), executed publiquely upon contempt, or rather for freeing the Dutch captives the last yeare without commision by our mediation; a Dutch shipp not long before departed hence, without performance of any matter of note for the redemption of their factors, who live continualy under gard as prisoners, the secound in his owne house here att Mocha, the Cheife with the Kinge at Sinane<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-050" href="#sec-05-fn-050" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-050" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup>; our countrymen the English <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-291" data-page="291" data-folio="252" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n290_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 252">[p. 252]</button> taken the last yeare att Aden carred up in irons to the Basshawe in Sinane, where with cruell and continuall labor they have ever since lived in shaverey.’ On finding matters in this state, they at first hesitated whether to trust themselves or their goods on shore (the <em>Blessing</em> not having then arrived); but after consultation, and the receipt of written assurance from the officials and principal merchants, they disembarked, took a house and landed about half their cargo. ‘In shorte time after wee received the Bashaws firmaund for good wellcome to his porte and confirmation of freedome and former liberties’; also letters from the English captives, imploring their assistance. Thereupon they wrote to the Pāshā, who in reply offered to release his prisoners on receipt of 1,000 rials and ‘a present of vallew’. ‘Their is of 13 persons left living 12; that dead was John Turnour, Mr. Banggams kinseman; two of them the Bashawe himselfe gave libertie; soe only 10 remayneth in daly travell and miserie.’ They decided to comply with the terms demanded, and sent an Englishman to deliver the present. The Pāshā, however, in spite of his undertaking, returned the messenger with a letter requiring the payment of 3,000 rials. After consultation, they paid this sum to the Mokha officials, on their promising the delivery of the captives within sixteen days, failing which the money was to be returned. They hope to receive the men in time to bring them back in the <em>Dolphin</em>, which is to stay fifteen days after the <em>Blessing</em>, for reasons which Captain Hall will explain. They see no reason to apprehend any difficulty in getting permission to depart, ‘notwithstandinge Davis his jellboat<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-051" href="#sec-05-fn-051" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-051" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> is now in question.’ But few of their goods have been sold, and those at little more than will cover their cost and charges. Are now relading the rest, and hope to finish in time to accompany the junks back to India. The latter, if they return <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-292" data-page="292" data-folio="253" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n291_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 253">[p. 253]</button> this year, will return poor; most of their goods will be left here unsold until next year. ‘Such is the beggerlines and bad condition of this place at present. The <em>Shahee</em>, shee onlie retornscfrom Judda rich; to whome Capt. Hall is inordred to give his attendance; which God grante may be prospirous to theffectinge of our resolved designes, on which jonkes only it wholie dependeth.’ Further relation is left to the bearer, Richard Lancaster. <em>P.S. (by Heynes).—</em> The prize goods laden in the <em>Blessing</em> are returned in her, except the ‘termarcke’ [turmeric]; also 37 bales transferred from the <em>Dolphin</em>. (<em>Copy. 2¾ pp. Received September 22.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">John Willoughby at Nosārī to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">August</span> 5, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 358).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The two masts recovered are half spoiled, but he is doing his best to prevent further damage. Cannot discover who has stolen the ironwork and planks. Advises that the masts be towed up to Surat by boats; otherwise the Portuguese may carry them away. On receipt of their letter of August 2, he and Mr. Billings came to this place and delivered the Governor’s parwāna to the ‘Crowry’. His answer was that ‘now was the cheife time of their harvist, and that the Mocadam had not paid in his last years hassell [Hind. <em>hāsil</em>, the government share of the produce]; and for that intreated me to stay seven or eight dayes untill his sowing was something over’. Willoughby replied that he had no power to delay the matter by ‘one gree<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-052" href="#sec-05-fn-052" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-052" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> time’; and thereupon the Krorī promised to summon the Mukaddam and examine him; should he refuse to confess, he should be sent to Surat as ordered. ‘The second, aboute halfe the night, Mr. Billings, walking of his round, found three blaks deginge behind a hedge and had made a great pitt, and saw with them certaine poots [Hind. <em>pāt</em>, a board or plank]. And hee having no Indeston [Hindūstānī] to speake to them, hee returned presenly to me; and before I could get to them two of them were rune away with their loggs, but the other I tooke, which was the Mochadam, whom all the night I kept him close prisonour.’ Next day he took him to the Krorī of Nosārī, who threatened the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-293" data-page="293" data-folio="254" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n292_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 254">[p. 254]</button> Mukaddam very much, but he would confess to nothing but a little latten, which was probably part of the lining of the bread-room. The Krorī is afraid to beat these people, because they owe him money and would probably run away. Nothing has come ashore since Willoughby’s arrival, and he considers it unlikely that any further recovery will be made, ‘because I thinke her ould sides is buried in the sands, so that her goods wilbe rotted with her for company.’ Suggests therefore that they should return to Surat after the dispatch of the masts, as they will only be spending money to no purpose. On the 4th ten or eleven boats left Gandevī River and went close to the site of the wreck. One returned, but on seeing them she put to sea again and made for Damān. Probably they were Portuguese. ‘If ought from oure shipp, they wilbe sure wee shall have the least share.’ <em>P.S.</em> (6th).—The Mukaddam of Otta has been granted a few days grace by the Krorī to make inquiries regarding the missing goods. Probably ‘hee will father yt uppon some poore man that hee may be cleare’. At present the Krorī is detaining him for two or three days about his ‘hassell’. (<em>Copy. 2 pp. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">WILLIAM MARTIN AND JUSTINIAN OFFLEY AT BROACH TO THE SURAT FACTORY, AUGUST 7, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 359).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Progress of their investment. Money due from Mr. Offley. ‘Wee have procured a house for the baker, with all things fitting for him, and have given money for provition of 100 ma[unds] of mealle at 39 pice per ma[und]; and [to the?] workmen which wee have hyred to helpe him wee are to pay 11 pice per ma[und], but they are to provide watter and wood so much as hee shall need.’ According to instructions they have refused to take the money offered them, as previously advised. Have written to the Baroda broker for the amount due from him. ‘Five days since here came from Nere Chan some six or seven hundred horsmen under the charge of his kinsman, Abdela Kerim [Abdullah Karīm], to make provition to take Unclesere, Hansott, and other places on the other side of the river which are given to Near Chan. His sonne is on the waye from Amadavaz with 200 horse. Soe soone as hee cometh hee will set <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-294" data-page="294" data-folio="255" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n293_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 255">[p. 255]</button> that business in action; of which Eesuph Beage hath notice, who will atend ther coming. Soe soone as Near Chans soulders come to the towne, these which were here before of Suffe Caun departed for Amadavaz.’ (<em>Copy. 1 p. Received August 10.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">August</span> 17, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 363).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Being a little better, though still weak, he started on the 12th for Surat in company with the usual caravan for Cambay; but at the gate of the city he was turned back by order of Safī Khān, who then sent him a message charging him in the Prince’s name not to leave the city without permission. Cannot get any reason from him, but he ‘telleth mee of newes that the English have robbed Mocha, and that wee have 50 shipps upon the coast in readnes to asist or transporte Corome; to which hee will here no replye, but keepeth me in daly expectation of my licence to depart, with this caution that I goe no farther then Barroch untill Surrat be fore the Kinge; which I have promised to observe, but as yett cannott gett free; all which is, as most men conjecture, they doubt that we will keepe the Princes joncks for his use, which they would not have, and to that end are settinge out friggatts from Camb[a]ia with firmauns and letters to meete them and perswade them to come from [for?] Goga.’ Fears that if Surat holds out, all the English goods and men will be stopped at Broach until the Prince’s junks have been secured. Also hears that earnest inquiry is being made as to what estate the English have in Broach and Surat. ‘Be sure that this rave[n]ing woulfe Suffee Caun will not sticke to robb us all in the Kings name.’ Has at last obtained permission to go as far as Broach with ‘Nasur Caun’ [Nasīr Khān], who starts to-morrow. ‘It is allso reported that upon the 7th of this new moone the young Prince and Caun Asame, Suffe Caun, and all the lesker here will follow towards Surratt. These men have writt to the Governor and Jancoly Beage [<em>see</em> p. 39] to receive in the towne and castell and repaire heather, and they shall have double the paye that they had of Corome from the King.’ If he is detained further, he will try to slip away secretly. Sends his cash statement. Has given ‘Gourdas’ a bill for Rs. 1,000, and begs that it may be honoured. (<em>Copy. 1½ pp. Received August 22.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-295"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="295" data-folio="256" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n294_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 256">[p. 256]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">John Willoughby at Nosārī to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">August</span> 19, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 360).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Agrees with them ‘that their hath never come anything ashore of the goods of the losst shipp that ere came in good condition’. Thinks, however, that the Krorī and Kotwāl here received what there was, and that with pressure all would come to light. However, those likely to know are keeping out of the way and he cannot discover them. As for the Mukaddam of Otta, he is waiting to see whether the Krorī will carry out his promise. Arranged for 2 boats (at Rs. 16) and 12 men to take the masts, &amp;c., to Surat, but was compelled to hire also about 115 men at three or four pice each to assist in getting them down to the water. Sent them off on the 15th. Considers it useless to expect any further recovery; and so he, with Richard Billings and Robert Tottle, are now about to start for Surat. They have left two peons to watch for twenty days in case anything else should come ashore. Some pieces of timber left as not worth carriage. Will bring a few small things with them. Has again urged the Krorī to send the Mukaddam to Surat, and he has promised to do so in two or three days. His peons have deserted him. (<em>Copy.</em> 2 <em>pp. Received August</em> 21.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Martin and Justinian Offley at Broach to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">August</span> 19, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 362).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Their cotton cloth is coming in daily. Request a supply of gunny for packing. The baker has used up the first 100 maunds of meal, and is now at work on a second batch. Can procure four or five hundred maunds of butter, if required, at 7½ mahmūdīs the maund. Send samples of rice. ‘Gravances’ cannot be got for two months yet. Request speedy instructions; also jars to put the butter into. Have spoken to the Dutch concerning the money, but they deny all knowledge of the matter. ‘Izack Beage tooke a good course to gett away from Uncleseir; for the next daye after his departure Nacec [Nasīr] Chans lasker, consisting of some 3,000 horse and foote, went from hence and tooke the towne without any resistance. The Deputy Governor of this place stayed but two dayes, left heare [theare?] and at Hansott about 400 souldiers, and returned with the rest of his company. Being arived here, had <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-296" data-page="296" data-folio="257" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n295_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 257">[p. 257]</button> presently newes that Isace Beage was to retourne with a greater force then hee had before; wherupon they sent more strenth from hence of men and horse to Unclesire; soe that now they doe not fere of any thinge that either the Governor of Surrat or hee canne doe. The daye before this now Governor goinge to Unclesir, hee sente for ours and the Dutch brocker to come to him; of whome hee demanded that wee should lend him 2,000 m[ahmūdīs] to pay his soulders, which hee would repay againe in five or six dayes. But they made him answere that wee had not any money at this time; soe that, haveinge kept them at his house more then halfe the daye, at night lett them departe. Since wee have not heard any thinge from him. Reporte goeth that Near Chan [Nāhir Khān] is to sett forth of Amadavaz this newe moone to come hether, and Rustram [<em>sic</em>] Chan, Chan Jeans sonne [<em>see</em> p. 245], is to goe to Surratt, and Suffe Caun hath written to the Governor there to yelde up the towne to the Kinge without further trouble and himselfe to come into the Kings service. His answer is not yett knowne; neither doe we know whether this bee of truthe.’ (<em>Copy.</em> 1<span class="sc">¾</span> <em>pp. Received August</em> 21.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same</span>, AUGUST 23, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 365).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Presume that the hundred jars sent to them are intended to be filled with butter. Have therefore contracted for 200 maunds at 7⅛ mahmūdīs, to be delivered in one month. Can get more if necessary, but money must be supplied for the purpose. Three days ago they had a visit from the Deputy-Governor and others, to whom they presented knives, &amp;c. Afterwards the party went to the Dutch house, where they were given spices and a looking-glass. Nāhir Khān is expected shortly. (<em>Copy.</em> ¾ <em>p. Received August</em> 24.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same</span>, AUGUST 31, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 366).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Gunny and bills of exchange received. The baker is ‘still in acction, and doth take gret care for the preserving of the bread, it beinge putt in a close drye house and well fenced with dutty etc. to keepe itt from the wales or ground’. As soon as they get money they will arrange for filling the ‘dubbes’ [<em>see</em> p. 8] with butter. Mr. Bangham arrived yesterday from Ahmadābād. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p. Received September</em> 1.)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-297"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="297" data-folio="258" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n296_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 258">[p. 258]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham at Broach to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">August</span> 31, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 366).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">On the 23rd, having after no little trouble obtained leave from Safī Khān, he set out from Ahmadābād and travelled about three kos on his road. The next morning, however, he was fetched back by order from the Khān-i-Azam. ‘In my retorne mett the young Prince and him with all there lesker, cominge out to ther tents, some halfe a course from the cittye, who are preparing to come this way. So that I was carried to his tente, where himselfe came out and caused mee to sit doune. After I had presented him with my ringe of my finger for want of other, hee begann to aske me some such questions as the people often do, concerninge the differance of religon betwene the Portingalls and us, and some other such like. After some other speeches to other of his people, hee rose to goe into his tente, not once questeinge mee why I went away; butt following him with termes of licence to depart, made a stand and caused his dewan to give me leave to goe wheather I would; which to procure from him cost mee more vexation then from the Nabob himselfe, insomuch that by the force of a bribe in mony of 30 rupes to him and his infferior officers I was discharged and leave to departe, and my garde of horse and foote taken away. All which trouble came by the villany of a bannyan, that at the instante of my departure toulld the Nabob that I was depart[e]d by the licence of Suffe Caun and not had [<em>sic</em>] visseted him; wherupon the Cuttwall was sente to the house to seeke me, but not findinge, hee like a villan revenged himselfe upon the two poore peons that I had left to keepe the house with 100 correes [Hind. <em>kore</em>, pl. of <em>korā</em>, ‘a blow with a whip’] a peece, and sente for the porters of the cittye gattes and likewise whipped them for lettinge mee goe out without his chopp.’ Being thus set free he resumed his journey, and reached this place yesterday. Intends to rest here until they let him know whether he can proceed to Surat with safety. ‘This cominge of Bauder Caun [Bahādur Khān] in such perimtory maner may cause conjecture of some senister underhand delivery of Surratt.’ Heard nothing of it till he reached Baroda, where Bahādur Khān is Governor for the Khān-i-Azam.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-298"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="298" data-folio="259" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n297_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 259">[p. 259]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Nāhir Khān is on his way to this place with 3,000 horse, but probably he will not hurry. (<em>Copy. 1½ pp. Received August</em> 31.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">William Martin and Justinian Offley at Broach to the Surat Factory, September</span> 7, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 368).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Return a bill of exchange which has not been met. Nāhir Khān is detained at Baroda by the state of the roads; and until he comes no one will contract for a further supply of butter. Money urgently needed. Bahādur Khān, Governor of Baroda under the Khān-i-Azam, marched from this place towards Surat on August 26 with 500 horse. (<em>Copy. ¾ p. Received September 8.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Matthew Duke and Thomas Mills at Masulipatam to the Surat Factory, September</span> 8, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 372).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Theirs of August 8 was received on September 4. ‘Aboute a month since, the <em>Memlicke</em>, a Dutch shipp, arrived on this coast out of Hollan,’ but she did not manage to get into port until two days ago. She brought little news, ‘althought shee were the only shipp was arrested in England<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-053" href="#sec-05-fn-053" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-053" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, but cleared upon the arrivall of some of the Staytes and East India Company. They only reporte of a hopefull aggreement betwene our masters and theirs, but not fully effected att ther settinge forth.’ Further information is given in the enclosed letters from Batavia, which were brought by the Dutch <em>Tortel</em> on the 5th. A Dutch frigate, the <em>Masulipatam</em>, has also arrived from Achin. She brought no letters from the English factors, but reports that she left the <em>Elizabeth</em> there, almost fully laden with pepper. ‘Their shipp the <em>Memlick</em> in her passage alongst this coast some fewe leages shorte of St. Thomas [St. Thomé] surprized a Portingall vessell, wherein was the Captain of St. Thomas with his wiffe and children, with divers others in like qualitie, and being for Goa imbarked his whole estate, which togeather with some others of his company valuing goods, moneyes, and jewells, to 35 or 40 thousand rials of eight; such is there overswelling fortuns that follow them. But they take this as parte requitall of ane afronte offered lattly by the Portingalls; who understandinge of our remove from Pallicatte, belevinge our league of frindshipp have bine brocken, begann to take harte, and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-299" data-page="299" data-folio="260" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n298_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 260">[p. 260]</button> some two daies after our departure thence sett forwards in their frigats, beinge only two, and cominge into the roade of Pallicatt as bould enterprise[r]s found only two Mores shipps, which longe before had discharged there goods, which was rice and such like provitions, which they yearly bringe to that place, beinge in most request; which shippes they sett both on fier, and stayed till they had seene an end of them, being performed att hight noone. On of those shipps belonged to the Kinge of Aracan, the other to merchants of Gingellee; which was som disparagment to the Dutch, which now by this shipp they have most fortunatly requited in the surprise of the aforesaid vessell, wher the Captain of St. Thomas lost his life, being burnte with his owne powder; his wife and daughters, with divers others weomen of note and two padrees [Port. <em>padre</em>, ‘a priest’], kepte as prisoners within the forte of Pallicatt, for whose release, especally the Governors wiffe and daughters, greate sute hath bene made, butt all in vain; this Dutch Governor, without any remorse or petty, intends to send them for Jaccatra.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-054" href="#sec-05-fn-054" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-054" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> I have bein solicited by letters from the Bishopp and others of St. Thomas to beg for them, wherein I have perswaded what may be for their freedom, butt cannott prevale.’ Two small ships, the <em>Abigail</em> and <em>Roebuck</em>, have lately arrived at Batavia from England, and the <em>Hart</em> is daily expected, bringing the new agreement with the Dutch. The letters now sent will inform them of ‘the lamentable death of soe many our good freinds in Amboyna, performed on them by the Dutches crultie.’ ‘Wee have received some allowance from our masters of the goodnes of these diamonds, with incoragment to proceed in byinge of more, if the mynes oppen and they continue in goodnes and prices accordinge; but our unity [<em>sic</em> only?] wante is moneyes to buy them.’ The ship from Holland brought store of rials and gold in small bars; while the <em>Tortel</em> had a good cargo of spices, Japanese copper, and silver; ‘wherby you may perceave how farr they goe beyond us in trade in all places where they are seated, Surratt only excepted.’ Their own small stock of nutmegs they have sold at a good price; otherwise they would have been in difficulties, for they cannot get rid of their silver, partly because most of it is coarse, and partly because the monopoly of trade in silver has been farmed out to <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-300" data-page="300" data-folio="261" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n299_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 261">[p. 261]</button> a native. Hope to dispatch the <em>Ruby</em> within ten days. It is understood that the <em>Great James</em>, <em>Charles</em>, and two other ships, under the command of John Bickley, are bound for Surat. Trust they will arrive in safety. (<em>Copy.</em> 2¾ <em>pp. Received November</em> 18.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Martin and Justinian Offley at Broach to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">September</span> 9, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 369).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Non-payment of a bill of exchange. Cannot take up any money here; so request a speedy supply from Surat. For the present it is impossible to get any butter beyond that already ordered. Will inquire whether boats can be had to take their goods to Surat. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p. Received September</em> 10.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Justinian Offley at Broach to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">September</span> 17, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 371).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Two thousand mahmūdīs received. They note that satisfaction for the protested bill is promised at Surat. Account of disbursements. The baker has made about 400 maunds of bread, and is still at work; he recommends that it should be packed in casks rather than in bags. Cannot get more than 160 maunds of butter; but they are making inquiries as to the possibility of buying some at Hānsot. Boats can be procured here to carry a thousand maunds of goods apiece, and the freight to Swally Road would be fifty mahmūdīs per boat; but whether the local authorities will permit any goods to leave the town is more than they can say. Nāhir Khān arrived on the 13th with 2,000 horse. On the next day they visited him and presented some red velvet and a piece of camlet; ‘hee gave us good words and promises us faire usage.’ The Dutch have visited him and given him some spices and a gold ring. He inquired of the English broker the date of the arrival of their ships from England and whether the English brought any slaves from Mokha; and also asked him why Safī Khān had ordered that no Englishman should leave Broach. The broker answered that he had heard nothing of the matter. They surmise that Safī Khān wishes to make the English ships convoy the junks coming from the Red Sea to Goghā. The Governor is not making any preparation to go to Surat before the arrival <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-301" data-page="301" data-folio="262" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n300_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 262">[p. 262]</button> of Khān-i-Azam. It is said that the young Prince has been left at Ahmadābād, for want of a suitable place of entertainment here. ‘Wee are sorrey to here that Jamcolly Beage [<em>see</em> p. 39] and your quondam Governor doth such mischeife from the castell to your townes people. They will feele the smart of it ere it be long, if Bauder Caun may have his desire, which will soon be knowne after Caun Assams arivall ther.’ As requested, they have sent by Robert Tottle 18 seers of enamel. Must leave it to them to fix the amount of money to be supplied to this factory. (<em>Copy. 2 pp. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Consultation held in Surat by President Rastell and Messrs. James, Bangham, Young, and Hopkinson, September</span> 19, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. i. p. 58).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In June last the Ahmadābād goods were housed in Broach (to avoid the rains), with the intention of sending them thence by sea at the time of the arrival of the fleet. Considering, however, the danger from frigates and the good condition of the roads, it is decided to abandon that plan and bring them down by land instead. William Gibson, having been employed for four years without salary and being now about to return to England, is granted 400 mahmūdīs as a reward for his services. (<em>Copy. ¾ p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">William Martin and Justinian Offley at Broach to the Surat Factory, September</span> 22, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 377).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Although the protested bill has not yet been paid at Surat, they have released the shroff on his giving security. Cannot procure any carts to carry their goods, owing to the recent heavy rains, but will forward them as soon as possible. The Customer promises that they shall not be unduly delayed at the river. The new Governor has claimed on behalf of the King all the goods which Himmat Khān and his brother had handed over to their creditors (including the English factors). ‘Wee have given attendance this three dayes to speake with him, but cannott. Hee is every day feasting and drinkinge; so that his busines is done now by his officers. This morninge he is come to Cojanazans house with his weomen to see the Dutch shipp sale uppe and downe the river and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-302" data-page="302" data-folio="263" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n301_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 263">[p. 263]</button> to shoote of gunnes which are said to be lente them and poulder from the towne.’ As directed, they will make no further provision of bread or butter. ‘Some three or four daies past wee hard that Sultan Blockey, Chan Assam, and Suffe Caun were come to Wasshett [<em>see</em> p. 234], but since cannott learne where [i. e. whether] they are come any nearer.’ (<em>Copy. 1½ pp. Received September 24.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">JOSEPH HOPKINSON [AT SURAT TO PRESIDENT RASTELL AT SWALLY], SEPTEMBER 25, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 379).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has sent to Broach copies of Rastell’s letters of the 23rd and 24th, together with those from England for the factors there. Thanks him for English news and letters. ‘Captain Browne<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-055" href="#sec-05-fn-055" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-055" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> his remembrances were thankfully received and quickly empted, his health not forgotten; wherin if he please to make a fresh supply, his courtisie wilbe verey welcome.’ Hutchinson is bringing down the box of emeralds, as desired. ‘I have bine with Bauder Caun and (though att first a little demured on) have procured his order to the Coustomur for licenceing bakers, Parses, &amp;c., to keepe a bazar on Swaly sands.’ The Customer promises to pass the English goods without delay, and ‘the people of the castell have allso, upon the mediation of Harrivesie [Hari Vaisya] and other bannians, licenced boats to passe over att the custom-house. They send [sent?] Bawhadur Caun word [that?] thought [though] they have for some reasons heitherto stood out, yett they are the Kings slaves; and reporte saith this day Aganoor is come to [him?] with an ambassador.’ Hopkinson has released the imprisoned banyan at the instance of the Governor. ‘Also cuttbaie [<em>see</em> p. 143] is taken of those lead theeving Parses, that yf that wee cane find them giulty they are the Kings gumagars [Hind. <em>gunagār</em> a criminal].’ How to prove their guilt is the difficulty, as they stoutly deny it. There is no show of suspicion on the part of the natives. ‘The Governor remembreth his dooa [<em>see</em> p. 245] unto you and intrateth to be remembred for some tofa [<em>see</em> p. 89] (if any) for his master.’ The Customer begs to be furnished with a long lance. (<em>Copy. 1¼ pp. Received September 26.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-303"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="303" data-folio="264" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n302_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 264">[p. 264]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Edward Heynes and William Hoare, aboard the</span> <em>Dolphin</em> <span class="sc">near Damān, to President Rastell, September</span> 27, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 388).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">After many unexpected difficulties and the expenditure of nearly 4,000 rials of eight, they cleared themselves and their ‘captived freinds’, and embarked in the <em>Dolphin</em> on August 23. Although markets were bad they have made some profit, and are bringing back about 34,000 rials in money. Ten leagues south of Aden they surprised a small junk belonging to Cannanore, out of which they took some money, opium, and myrrh. The principal merchants were brought on board the <em>Dolphin</em>, and the junk was ordered to keep company; but on the English giving chase to two other vessels she slipped away in the darkness. They captured the two ships, which proved to belong to Surat, ‘the one called the <em>Toklie</em> [? <em>Tawakkul Alī</em>: ‘Trust in Ali’], nochadah [skipper: Pers. <em>nākhuda</em>] Muchsud Allia [Maqsūd Alī], whom wee retaind with his principall marchants [and?] the vickle [<em>see</em> p. 11] of Isack Beage abord us, and suppose shee may prove welthy. The other juncke is called the <em>Tawrie</em> [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 278], nochadah Surgge Bannian<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-056" href="#sec-05-fn-056" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-056" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, who by information of divers in Mocha shee doth transport a great estate of the marchants of Dabull.’ In accordance with their instructions, they have not touched the cargo of either. On September 24 they met with Captain [Hall], who had under his guns the Prince’s junk from Goghā, a vessel of Diu, and a small junk belonging to ‘Pellwan Saffeet’ [<em>see</em> p. 176]. He had also taken a ship belonging to Chaul, but had allowed her to depart. The <em>Blessing</em> and <em>Dolphin</em> are now at anchor near Damān, ‘and have under our comand five junckes, a world of people to mayntaine with water and victualls.’ They are short of water, and unless a supply can be sent must come to the Bar of Surat or to Swally. Would be glad to put ashore most of the natives, but dare not do so without orders, especially as any discovery of their designs might endanger the liberty of the English at Surat. The <em>Ganjāwar</em>, belonging to Surat, was expected to leave Mokha shortly after the <em>Dolphin</em>, so they hope to add her, and possibly the <em>Shāhī</em>, to their other prizes. They have a number of men of quality among the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-304" data-page="304" data-folio="265" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n303_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 265">[p. 265]</button> prisoners, who, they hope, will be sufficient hostages for the English ashore, should any trouble arise. Would be glad to have speedy intelligence of the state of affairs.
