Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Robert Hutchinson and Justinian Offley, aboard the <em>Blessing</em>, to the President and Council [at Surat?], November</span> 19, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 450).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They are busy packing up the money, but need for this purpose some boards to make chests, gunny, cords, &amp;c. (<em>Copy. ¾ p. Received November 20.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Consultation held at Surat by President Rastell and Messrs. James, Heynes, Young, Hopkinson, and Hoare, November</span> 19, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. i. p. 77).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The President states that some months past he received letters from the King to whom the port of ‘Batacala’ [Bhatkal] belongs, inviting and soliciting the English to visit that port, ‘with promise not onlie off future faire comerce but assuringe at present to furnish with 2,000 candees of pepper then in readinesse.’ It is thought, however, that although it would be to the advantage of the Company to open up such a trade, yet the nearness of the port to Goa would make it too dangerous for a single ship to go there; and as not more than one could be spared for such a purpose, it is decided to postpone the attempt until a fitter opportunity. The people of Surat having now concluded the desired agreement, it is resolved that the junks be released accordingly. As the present stock of ready money is largely in excess of their requirements in India, the Council decide to send the surplus to other settlements. The question being raised whether a ship should be dispatched at once with money only or whether it should be kept back till March in order to send part in goods, it is decided that the former would be the better plan. It is therefore resolved to dispatch the <em>Reformation</em>, with 80,000 rials and such of the Mokha remains as are suitable, to Achin and Bantam by the first of December. As it is likely that they will be able to lade two ships for England next March, and also for better defence against the Portuguese, the Council determine <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-363" data-page="363" data-folio="324" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n362_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 324">[p. 324]</button> to send both the <em>William</em> and <em>Blessing</em> to Persia, to sail not later than December 1. Heynes and Lancaster are to go in these ships as merchants, as it is believed that the factors have already departed overland for Europe, in accordance with the permission given to them by the Company. ‘In respecte of the manye greate motyves to determine a residence at courte, as well to justifie and gyve reasonn of our past proceedings with these people as alsoe to obtayne the Kings firmaen to ratifie and confirme the contracts concluded on with Cosrooe the younge Prince<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-111" href="#sec-05-fn-111" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-111" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, the Kings deputies, Caun Assum and Sief Chan, with other cheifes of this place, and likewise to make sale of sundrye goods and jewells which that place onlie will vend to most benyfitt’, it is resolved to dispatch Robert Young, Justinian Offley, and John Goodwin thither. In order to set on foot the Ahmadābād investment for relading the ships for England, Robert Hutchinson, John Leachland, William Hill, Thomas Vincent, and John Towerson are ordered for that place, with twelve chests of rials and a guard of fourteen sailors under Richard Morton. Should any investment in Cambay be necessary, that business is to be entrusted to Leachland. As Mr. James’s period of service is nearly up, he is permitted to close his books at the end of September and hand over the accounts to Joseph Hopkinson, who will then give up the charge of the warehouse to William Hoare. Willoughby is appointed to assist Mr. Martin at Broach in lieu of Mr. Offley, and Tottle is to succeed Goodwin as steward of the English house. It is decided to restore the goods taken out of a Cannanore junk at sea and then supposed to belong to subjects of the Zamorin, the owners having now satisfied the Council that this is not so; also to do the same with the goods found in ‘the juncke of Sovegee’ belonging to other than ‘Dabulleers’. (<em>Copy.</em> 3¾ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">JOHN LEACHLAND AT RĀNDER TO PRESIDENT RASTELL AT SURAT, NOVEMBER 20, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 451).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Delivered his letter and was promised leave to dispatch the goods; but at present the Dutch have monopolized the carts. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-364" data-page="364" data-folio="325" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n363_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 325">[p. 325]</button> Reminds him of various matters in connexion with his intended journey. <em>P.S.</em>—Has delivered the velvet, &amp;c., to Mr. Hayward. Apologizes for his writing, which is done ‘with Ind[u]stany wepons’. Begs some gunpowder. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received November 21.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Richard Allnatt [Master of the</span> <em>Reformation</em>] <span class="sc">at Swally to President Rastell at Surat, November 20, 1623</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 451).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Was about to sail for the Bar of Surat when he received his countermand. Is now busy filling the water-casks. Sends a note of stores wanted. <em>P.S.</em> (21<em>st</em>)—Has just received a verbal order from Mr. Young to go down to the Bar, and is about to sail accordingly. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Edward Heynes, aboard the</span> <em>Dolphin</em>, <span class="sc">to President Rastell at Surat, November 21, 1623</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 452).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The bearer, Mr. Brewerton, desires to know how he is to be employed. Commends him for ‘a pretty seaman’. Last night the <em>Blessing</em> and <em>William</em> were ordered up to Swally, and the <em>Reformation</em> is now going down to the junks. No news of the chests from Surat. To-morrow he will send two chests of rials by Messrs. Hutchinson and ‘Billadge’ [Billings?]. <em>P.S.</em>—The packers have not arrived. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received November 22.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Matthew Wills, aboard the</span> <em>Dolphin</em>, <span class="sc">to President Rastell at Surat, November 21, 1623</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 453).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">‘Yours received the 20th of present by Mr. Hopton, thereby perceavinge your courtious entertainment att the handes of the Gosseratts, as also a grant for the despeed of goods for the lading of the <em>Dolphin.</em>’ Will be ready to receive them at the ‘Marrine’. The money and convoy for Ahmadābād shall also be provided. Robert Broadstreet has taken over the post of purser from Thomas Pory. The junk with the Dābhol goods has been brought into the Road; he would be glad to know to whom he is to deliver her cargo. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received November 22.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-365"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="365" data-folio="326" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n364_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 326">[p. 326]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Edward Heynes, aboard the</span> <em>DOLPHIN</em>, <span class="sc">to President Rastell at Surat, November</span> 22, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 453).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has now landed the twelve chests of rials for Ahmadābād. Richard Morton is captain of the convoy, and Mr. Lancaster is to accompany them to Variao, where Hutchinson will take charge. Has cleared with ‘Court Mamoot’, and has delivered to ‘the Mallabars’ their goods and money; it will, however, be necessary, ‘his cabemall<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-112" href="#sec-05-fn-112" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-112" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> beinge dead, to take ther accknowledgment before the Cassie, firmed by his chopp, that they have received from you all whatsoever was taken from them at sea by us.’ Lancaster is to take charge at the Marine, with West to assist him; and a court of guard has been established there. The natives are dilatory over their business. Mats, gunny, &amp;c., wanted. <em>P.S.</em>—Has just received the orders for the delivery of rials and gold to the ‘Vicle’ [<em>see</em> p. 11] of Is-hāq Beg. Has delivered him ‘one suppetty [Hind. <em>sampatī</em>, a casket] with royalls’, and will see to the gold to-morrow. His letter about Brewerton received. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p. Received November</em> 23.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same, November</span> 22, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 454).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has delivered to the messengers the two ‘suppettas of plate’. They had not brought the keys to unlock them, and so he does not know their contents; but he thinks there could have been no rials and not much gold in them. A receipt has been taken. <em>P.S.</em>—He sends Mr. Martin’s recantation, which the Council has accepted. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same, November</span> 23, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 455).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Explains why certain gold has not been delivered to the bearer, as directed. ‘Robert Younge hath some wormes in his pate that gives him littele content in this place.’ He has gone up without leave, and Heynes begs that he may be sent down again to perform the duties assigned him. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p. Received November</em> 24.)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-366"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="366" data-folio="327" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n365_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 327">[p. 327]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Richard Lancaster at Variao to President Rastell at Surat, November</span> 23, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 452).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The convoy with twelve chests of money has arrived, and the coaches are now returning. Requests money to pay for them. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">John Leachland at Rānder to President Rastell at Surat, November</span> 23, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 455).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wrote yesterday by a ‘masure’ [Hind. <em>mazūr</em>, a porter] but had no answer returned. He is promised carts this morning, and no time shall be lost in sending down the goods. <em>P.S.</em>—Mr. Gray desires to go home in the first ship, as his wife is dead and his estate left out of order. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Hoare and Edward Heynes, aboard the</span> <em>Dolphin</em>, <span class="sc">to President Rastell at Surat, November</span> 24, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, pp. 456, 459).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Silk, opium, and benzoin transferred to the <em>Dolphin</em>. The native merchants should be stirred up to finish their business, as time is short. As yet the <em>Shahī</em> has not yielded more than about 20,000 rials, although most of her chests have now been opened. Suggest that the lead on board Captain Hall’s ship be left there until his return. Delivery of money and gold. Mr. Wills entreats the dispatch of the pepper. <em>P.S.</em>—The decision as to the priority of the two admirals has greatly incensed Captain Hall’s company (though he himself shows no discontent). They take it much to heart that after so long service they should ‘be forced to conceale their flag in the cookroome (their owne word) for any whosoever in India, yourselſe only excepted, when aboard any other shipp.’ (<em>Copy. 1 p. Received November</em> 25.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Richard Allnatt, aboard the</span> <em>Reformation</em> <span class="sc">at the Bar of Surat, to President Rastell, November</span> 24, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 459).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The ‘blacks’ have now discharged all their goods and are preparing to take their junks over the bar. Inquires therefore whether he may leave them and come up. (<em>Copy. ¼ p. Received November</em> 25.)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-367"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="367" data-folio="328" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n366_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 328">[p. 328]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same, November</span> 24, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 460).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has delivered Mr. Wedmore’s goods to Mr. Hodges. Asks Mr. Rastell to write on his (Allnatt’s) behalf to the President at Batavia to allow him to return to England by the first conveyance after his arrival. (<em>Copy. ½ p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Captain John Hall at Swally to President Rastell at Surat, November</span> 25, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 456).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">He has had a dispute with Captain Brown ‘concerning our flaggs wearinge’. The Company made him the commander of a fleet and he intends to maintain his rights. It is true that he is at the President’s disposal, but he trusts the latter will not suffer him ‘to be an open disgrace to all men’. Requests his decision on the point. <em>P.S.</em>—Complains of the little respect given to him when he comes ashore. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Edward Heynes, aboard the <em>Blessing</em></span>, <span class="sc">to President Rastell at Surat, November</span> 26, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 457).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Explains why he has not delivered to ‘Mucksud Ally’ [Maqsūd Alī] his ‘suppetaes of money’ pending further instructions. Packing of quicksilver. The merchants should be sent down to weigh their money. Destination of certain bales of goods. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received November</em> 27.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">John Leachland at Ahmadābād to President Rastell at Surat, November</span> 29, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 471).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Arrived here yesterday. Went this morning to deliver to Khān-i-Azam the letters from Saif Khān and the Hakīm, but could not speak with him, as he was closeted with the Prince. He and Gurdās then inquired secretly for indigo, and heard of about 3,000 fardles, part here and part in Cambay. The broker, however, is ‘a sutelle knave’, and demands ’50 rupes per fardle of 4 mands and 7 seares’; he told them, too, of the approach of the Dutch and of the intended dispatch of rials from Variao by the English for this place. There is a large quantity of loose indigo at Sarkhej, to be <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-368" data-page="368" data-folio="329" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n367_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 329">[p. 329]</button> had at Rs. 8½ and 8¾ per maund, but he fears that his time is too short to permit of its purchase; and, moreover, the price is likely to rise, as the news of the conclusion of peace between the English and the natives has brought many buyers into the market. ‘This day arived a pattamar from Goa, which [brought?] newes of two English [<em>sic</em>] shipps and seven gallioons arived at Goa and since departed for Ormus for the restablishing of them there, and verrified that Rufrra hath taken both townes [<em>sic</em>] and castle allreadie; likewise of pease made betweene us, and free licence for our shipps for trade in any there ports or wintring, if wee so bee pleased.’ ‘The Prince and Caun Azum is com within the cittie; and a great deale of grace is given to Sife Caun by the King, with a new nam [<em>see</em> p. 305] and a chopp of the Kings sending, wherein is written a pleasinge phrase. Mer Mosa [Mīr Mūsā] hath gained the juger [<em>jāghīr</em>] of Cambayett, and is on the way to take possession of it; who alwayes hath bin a freind to marchantes and a man that delights much in tophie [<em>see</em> p. 89], but as hard as flinte in his bargaine, but a good payemaster.’ <em>P.S.</em>—Sends Mr. Gibbs’ receipt, to be given to Mr. Offley. (<em>Copy. 1½ pp. Received December 8.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">EDWARD HEYNES, ABOARD THE <em>WILLIAM</em>, TO PRESIDENT RASTELL AT SURAT, NOVEMBER 29, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 457).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">He has allowed Mr. Offley, at his urgent entreaty, to go to Surat, and has appointed Mr. Willoughby to take his place. They are waiting for the merchants to come down and count their money. Would be glad of John Glanvill’s assistance, if he can be spared. ‘Suffgee’, the nākhudā of the junk now discharged, is so untruthful that Heynes must leave Rastell to deal with him. The ‘Mallabars’ promised to visit the President; if they have not done so, they shall be sent up. Packing of quicksilver and delivery of goods. Captain Hall promises to obey the President’s orders regarding the flag, but considers that ‘hee shall loose his reputation and creditt by furlinge up his flagg in presence of a man soe inferior to himselfe in the eye of the world’. His crew are much aggrieved and may show their resentment at sea. Hears that the Dutch are sending a ship to Bantam (some say a month hence), but they are working very slowly. They seem to shun the English. (<em>Copy. 1¼ pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-369"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="369" data-folio="330" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n368_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 330">[p. 330]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3">RICHARD LANCASTER AND NATHANIEL WEST [AT SWALLY] TO PRESIDENT RASTELL AT SURAT, [NOVEMBER 30?] 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 460).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Goods received and dispatched. Request an early supply of pepper ‘to shute with the indico’. <em>P.S.</em>—Mr. Glanvill should be sent back to finish his work. (<em>Copy. ½ p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">RICHARD LANCASTER AT SWALLY TO PRESIDENT RASTELL AT SURAT, DECEMBER 1, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 461).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has sent up a piece of branched camlet, as ordered. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">MATTHEW WILLS, ABOARD THE <em>DOLPHIN</em>, TO PRESIDENT RASTELL AT SURAT, DECEMBER 1, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 461).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">More cotton required. Two hundred pepper bags sent by the bearer. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">ROBERT HUTCHINSON AND WILLIAM HILL AT BARODA TO PRESIDENT RASTELL AT SURAT, DECEMBER 2, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 461).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have got thus far without payment of tolls. Presented Nāhir Khān’s letters at Broach, but they were ‘little respected, for that I went to deliver them emptie handed’; also that to Bahādur Khān at Baroda. From Broach they had only one horseman to guard them, and he did not appear till they were half-way to Baroda. At this place they are promised horse and foot, but how many and how far they will go is not yet known. The brokers say they have news of the arrival of Mr. Leachland and Gurdās at Ahmadābād. <em>P.S.</em>—Mr. Hopkinson’s boy Peter is here with Bahādur Khān and has ‘turned Moore’. His surrender was demanded but refused. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received December 5.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">JOHN LEACHLAND AT AHMADĀBĀD TO PRESIDENT RASTELL AT SURAT, DECEMBER 2, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 469).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wrote on November 29 to announce his arrival. Has now agreed for nearly 1,200 fardles [of indigo] and is in treaty for <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-370" data-page="370" data-folio="331" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n369_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 331">[p. 331]</button> 2,500 more. He was promised samples from Sarkhej but they have not arrived, and he is afraid to go thither, ‘for that none knowes otherwise butt that my busines is to Caun Azum, whoe (in regard of the Princes not being halfe currantt) hath not sett forth since my arivall.’ The Dutch are at ‘Nerriad’ [Nariād], and have written to engage house room in ‘Durgesera’; consequently prices are now rising. Hopes to get the indigo down by about the end of January, in time for the ships for England; if desired, that already bought may be sent in advance. <em>P.S.</em>—Gurdās sends his respects. (<em>Copy.</em> ¾ <em>p. Received December</em> 8.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">CONSULTATION HELD IN SURAT BY PRESIDENT RASTELL AND MESSRS. JAMES, BANGHAM, HEYNES, HOPKINSON, AND YOUNG, DECEMBER 3, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. i. p. 72).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Nicholas Bangham being in bad health and desirous of going home in the <em>Dolphin</em>, it is decided to permit him to do so and to allow him the accustomed sum of 200 mahmūdīs towards his provisions for sea. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">EDWARD HEYNES, ABOARD THE <em>WILLIAM</em>, TO PRESIDENT RASTELL AT SURAT, DECEMBER 6, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 462).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They are still handing over the money; but there appears to be much less than the owners reported, and perhaps it would be well to suspend further payments until they have ascertained the exact amount available. Though Saif Khān may threaten, he will not break with them as long as the ships are here. The captains complain of the pestering of their ships with passengers’ goods, and beg that no more licences be granted. Ropes wanted for packing; also chests for the money. (<em>Copy.</em> ¾ <em>p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">JOHN LEACHLAND AT AHMADĀBĀD TO PRESIDENT RASTELL AT SURAT, DECEMBER 7, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 473).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Will follow his directions. Has now struck a bargain for 1,600 fardles more at Rs. 42¾ per fardle, this being the lowest price he could obtain. Mr. Hutchinson and his company arrived on the 5th. Neither he nor Leachland has yet been able to see the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-371" data-page="371" data-folio="332" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n370_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 332">[p. 332]</button> Nawāb. Is glad the indigo at Cambay has been bought. The Dutch here offered Rs. 43½ for it without success; and they have returned to-day from Sarkhej without doing anything. The price has risen to Rs. 10 and 10½ per maund. Intends to visit Cambay shortly. Has now got together nearly 3,000 fardles, delivery of which is expected to-morrow. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p. Received December</em> 12.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Edward Heynes aboard the</span> <em>WILLIAM</em> <span class="sc">to President Rastell at Surat, December</span> 8, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 469).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has not yet received his letter about Saif Khān’s money, but supposes it will arrive shortly. He is very glad to hear that the Hakīm shows himself so friendly disposed, as his influence is great; and his advancing 7,000 rials of his own to satisfy Saif Khān is a true testimony of his affection. Reiterates his advice to stop further payments until all the money has been counted. Mr. Willoughby is employed in packing goods aboard; but ‘his peevish humor’ makes him unfit for any service. A firm attitude should be maintained as regards the prize goods belonging to Malik Ambar, though some consideration might be shown in the case of private persons. ‘By an unfortunate accident my old acquaintance Nicholas Hayward last night at Swaly was slane.’ Detains Lancaster to help in his place. Would be glad to have West instead of Willoughby. Amber beads and other goods sent up to Surat. ‘The seaven chests [of] rials by the convoy is returned last night, whose joyfull and safe arivall cost Nicholas Haywards life; whoe sitting on the chowtree<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-113" href="#sec-05-fn-113" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-113" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> of Swalley with the convoye, resting a time from theire dayes travell, a volley of shott being given, on unadvisedlye discharged his peece laden with a bullett, and tooke that poore soule from us to rest in heavne.’ The chests will be laden in the <em>Reformation</em>. Orders have been given to send aspers<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-114" href="#sec-05-fn-114" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-114" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>, bullion, and rials to Surat for Saif Khān to the amount of thirty or forty thousand rupees. The gold intended for Ahmadābād is being got ready. <em>P.S.</em>—Willoughby is now more tractable. Paper wanted for use in the voyage. (<em>Copy.</em> 1¾ <em>pp. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-372"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="372" data-folio="333" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n371_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 333">[p. 333]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Captain Christopher Brown aboard the</span> <em>William</em> <span class="sc">to President Rastell, December</span> 8, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 473).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Requests that four runaways may be intercepted. Cannot spare any men to the <em>Reformation</em>, as he has only 160 (including the runaways) and many of these are sick. (<em>Copy.</em> ¼ <em>p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Robert Hutchinson at Ahmadābād to President Rastell at Surat, December</span> 8, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 474).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Arrived safely on the 5th. Nothing paid on the way for ‘either customes or radare’ [<em>see</em> p. 310]. He would have sent back the convoy at once, but no man is allowed to leave the city without permission. To-day he visited Khān-i-Azam, by whom he was courteously entertained, and after presentation of some knives and amber beads obtained a parwāna for the free coming and going of the English, with leave to buy some gunpowder ‘for theire use downewards’. The soldiers will therefore be sent off to-night. Requests detailed instructions for his guidance. Mr. Leachland’s letter will show what has been done as regards the purchase of indigo. <em>P.S.</em>—Particulars of the indigo bought. (<em>Copy.</em> 1¼ <em>pp. Received December</em> 13.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Robert Hutchinson, John Leachland, and William Hill at Ahmadābād to President Rastell at Surat, December</span> 9, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 478).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">These few lines sent by the returning convoy. A hundred rupees given to them for expenses on the way. Will write soon at length. (<em>Copy. ¼ p. Received December</em> 17.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same, December</span> 11, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 476).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They note the bargain he has made with Saif Khān for indigo at Cambay. Mr. Leachland is about to go thither to receive it. The Dutch have done next to nothing, except ‘to hire our houses at Serques out of our hands at eight rupes per mounth’. The English brokers have already reached Cambay. During Mr. Leachland’s absence, Mr. Hill will take charge of the cash and accounts. Have gone through the fardles of indigo, and found only twenty bad, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-373" data-page="373" data-folio="334" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n372_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 334">[p. 334]</button> ‘which wee leave for the Dutch.’ Trust that shortly they will procure enough to fill the <em>Blessing</em> and the <em>William</em>. Suggest that it should be sent down in two ‘caphiloes’ [<em>see</em> p. 4], owing to the difficulty of procuring carts. <em>P.S.</em>—Send particulars of the indigo already provided. <em>P.P.S.</em>—Advise an offer for all the quicksilver at Surat, and request a speedy decision; also one for 2,000 maunds of lead at Rs. 3¾ per maund. Gurdās sends his ‘sallam’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-115" href="#sec-05-fn-115" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-115" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>. (<em>Copy.</em> 1¾ <em>pp. Received December</em> 16.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">John Leachland at Ahmadābād to President Rastell at Surat, December</span> 11, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 476).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Thinks it will be best to send down the indigo in two caravans. To-morrow he starts for Cambay. The Dutch are likely to return as light as they came, for they have been several times to Sarkhej without getting any indigo, and are now at a loss what to do. Two of their merchants have gone to Cambay, and the rest are ‘a hireing of houses and repairing of warehouses, and at last fill them with wine, for there noses are slitt for this yeares doinge any thinge that good cann bee’. (<em>Copy.</em> ¾ <em>p. Received December</em> 16.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Edward Heynes in Swally Road to President Rastell at Surat, December</span> 12, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 473).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Delivery of goods. The sugar they cannot take, for the ships are already so pestered that ‘it wilbe fearfull to goe to sea in them, espetially our danger of enemies being to bee expected’. <em>P.S.</em>— More gunny wanted. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">President Brockedon and Council at Batavia to the Company, December</span> 14, 1623 (<em>O.C.</em> 1130).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-116" href="#sec-05-fn-116" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-116" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The <em>Coaster</em> arrived on October 2. About 500 leagues short of the Cape she met a Portuguese vessel of about 150 tons, which had left Lisbon in company with three carracks, three galleons, and one ship of her own burden, ‘sett forth principallie for the regayning of Ormuz, having store of people but small cavidale’ [<em>see</em> <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-374" data-page="374" data-folio="335" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n373_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 335">[p. 335]</button> p. 32]. After a fight lasting twelve hours, the Portuguese vessel was captured and carried to the Cape, where she was released after some victuals had been taken out of her. . . . The sending of a ship to the Red Sea from Masulipatam (as suggested by the Company) cannot be effected as speedily as is supposed; for even if that place affords a sufficient quantity of suitable goods (which they doubt) it would require six or eight months to procure them. The ship must set out in February, and therefore no arrangements can be made this year; but they have written to the factors at Masulipatam on the subject, with a view to future action should an occasion present itself. . . . They note the Company’s remarks concerning ‘the diamonds of the Coast’ sent home in the <em>James</em> and <em>Charles</em>, ‘wherein yow saie yow finde no cause to discourage your factors from proceeding in that commoditie,’ but seem to hint that there has been some unfaithful dealing in the buying of them; with regard to this, they assure the Company that the diamonds were sent just as they were received from Masulipatam. Those who bought them have now returned and can satisfy the Company in the matter; but certainly the diamonds referred to were better than some which have since come from the same place. A copy of the Company’s remarks and instructions for the purchase of greater quantities has now been forwarded to the Masulipatam factors. . . . They blame the Dutch for the ill-success of the late voyage to the Malabar Coast, and have recorded a formal protest against Dedel for separating his fleet. ‘For those just reasons therein contayned, uppon their late demand that wee should furnish shippes with them to send uppon a second exploite for Goa, wee have utterlie refused to joyne with them herein, or in anie other like exploits of those remote defensive, or rather offensive, warres in places whereas wee have no trade nor residence, untill first your losses received by the Admirall Dedells proceedings maie be repaired, and secondlie our freedomes restored according to the articles of agreement or untill further prescriptions maie be brought from you our emploiers.’ A similar answer was given to a second demand. The complaints against Dedel have been referred to Europe. . . . ‘If the Danish shipp which arrived att Plymouth had gone from this place wee should have advized you of it. By copies of letters received from Pallicatt which wee sent yow it <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-375" data-page="375" data-folio="336" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n374_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 336">[p. 336]</button> appeared shee went from that coast, and was but half laden. For that pepper shee had they left divers of their people and thirtie-six peeces of ordnance in pawne untill they maie receive meanes from Denmark to paie for it’.... They will make further inquiry concerning the gum-lac from Masulipatam sent home in the <em>Charles</em>.... ‘The great quantities of long callicos sent home yeerlie by the Dutch from Musulpatam gave us incouragement to provide good quantities of the like, which wee have sent home per the <em>Palsgrave</em>; and also of a finer sort of cloth made there. But finding its vent not answearable to our expectations, wee will forbeare anie further investments in those commodities untill further incouragement from yow or that wee find overplus of cavidall that maie be well spared from more needfull emploiements. Those of Musulpatam wilbe hardlie brought to make closer cloth then their accustomed.... There are no dutties made at the Coast of Choromandell’; those sent home in the <em>Charles</em> had come from Surat. Mr. Mills was mistaken in his advice of the goodness and quantities of gunpowder procurable at Pulicat. The Dutch make use of some for current needs, but they rely upon Holland powder for store, ‘because the other will not keepe, for being ill-corned it growes all into clodds.’ Moreover, if they could get good powder at Pulicat, they would not lade home such quantities of saltpetre as they yearly do from thence.... Orders have been given for procuring a good parcel of diamonds from the Coast, which will no doubt be effected if the mine be open; ‘last yeare, when the mine was shutt, they were at four times the price of those formerlie sent yow, and few or noone good to be procured at anie rate....’ They note that the charges of the fort at Pulicat began June 13, 1620, but in the absence of particulars they cannot say whether any of the items has been paid already; send therefore a statement of their payments on this account for comparison with the Dutch particulars. Refer to other documents for ‘the reasons moving us to depart from Pallicatt’. They consider that the Dutch have much wronged the Company in making them pay their share of the expenses at Pulicat before the arrival of the English factors there.... ‘The Goa fleett arrived in this road, the <em>Roiall Ann</em> the 28th Maie, the <em>Exchange</em> and <em>Diamond</em> the 10th June, having in a manner made a lost voiage through the perfidious dealing of the Dutch Admirall Dedell, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-376" data-page="376" data-folio="337" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n375_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 337">[p. 337]</button> cheeflie by sending awaie two of his best shippes for the Red Sea.’ But for this the whole Portuguese squadron would have been captured. The value taken from the sunk carrack and certain frigates was 36,591 rials. Both in this and the Manilla voyage there has been much pilfering by the commanders and sailors. Captain Michael Green came short in account 8,453 rials, besides having been exceedingly lavish in provisions, &amp;c. As he could give no satisfactory reasons for this, they have decided to send him home by the first ship for examination and punishment. Others will be questioned at the earliest opportunity.... Account of the estate of the late President Fursland. He brought a good stock out of England, and invested it in goods at Surat which more than doubled it at Achin; afterwards he speculated in diamonds at the Coast, and thus increased his estate to its present dimensions....</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">‘The Danes have laden this yeere at the Coast about 400 tonnes of pepper, which with some callicos make upp the cargazon of one of their shippes for Denmark. The prince of Tajour [Tanjūr] or Naick of those parts hath often invited us to live in his countrie, promising us great priviledges and favours; and now by the <em>Ruby</em><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-117" href="#sec-05-fn-117" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-117" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> there is come an Englishman, named Johnson, who came out master of one of the first Danish shippes, who hath lien about 18 months at the Naicks court as a pawne for the pepper first sent home by the Danes; who reports the Naick, having heard the English to be a peaceable nation that seek not to incroach on other mens territories, was earnest with him to moove unto us the favourable opinion hee had of our nation and great desire that wee should trade in his dominions; wherefore, understanding that better cloth of all sorts is made there then at Pallicatt or Musulpatam, and pepper likewise to be gotten in reasonable quantities, wee have resolved to send a shipp thither within these three months to make triall what good maie be done in those parts. The Portugalls have lived long in his countrie, but without fortification, hee taking their protection uppon him, and offers the like to us or to fortifie as the Danes have done. The Dutch have been earnest suitors to the Naick to fortifie in his countrie, and had begunn a fort at Tinegapatan [Tegnepatam]; but the Naick refuseth to have them live in <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-377" data-page="377" data-folio="338" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n376_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 338">[p. 338]</button> his countrie and hath demolished what they had begunn, saying hee hath heard how they incroched uppon other princes dominions and countries, and therfore should not live in his.’ As regards sending a ship to the Red Sea from Masulipatam, they wrote both to that place and to Surat for advice. The factors at the former sent merely a list of goods suitable for the purpose, with an intimation that they could not be provided under six months. They propose to do nothing until an answer comes from Surat; but as they are to send a ship to Tanjūr, they will arrange that, in the event of non-success there, she shall go on to Masulipatam and be ready to proceed to the Red Sea. It is, however, the general report that the goods obtained in the Mogul’s dominions are much more suitable for that quarter than any to be had in Masulipatam; and, were it otherwise, the Moors of the latter place would not be so backward in sending thither as they now are. Gum-lac is most uncertain in price at Masulipatam, rising or falling in accordance with the quantities brought from Pegu and ‘Tanassary’; at present there is plenty available at a reduced price. About twenty chests of rough coral may be sold yearly at the same place; the polished is in no demand. Lead also will sell, and about ten stammet broadcloths. ‘The Dutch have a great advantage of us in the Coast trade, by reason of the great quantities of cloves, nuts, and mace by them sold there at verie great proffitt, and brings them in gold, which is there much preferred to rialls or other silver.’ Sent a quantity of Japan silver last year to Masulipatam, but a difficulty was found in putting it off, and consequently the returns of cloth, diamonds, &amp;c., came short of expectations. The factors hope, however, to get rid of the silver after the Dutch have made all their investments. . . . Matthew Duke, employed at Masulipatam, has handed in his accounts duly balanced, and has been granted permission to return to England. . . . Pulicat was abandoned chiefly because ‘the trade therof depends wholie uppon the sales of Molluccos, Amboyna, and Banda’, the English factories in which have now been withdrawn. Even if those factories should at some future date be re-established, it would not be advisable to resume operations at Pulicat, for better cotton cloth can be obtained at other places on the Coast without contributing to the charges of a costly garrison. ‘The goods made in the Naicks countrie <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-378" data-page="378" data-folio="339" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n377_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 339">[p. 339]</button> formerlie mentioned farr exceeds Pallicatt in coulours, goodnesse, and cheapnesse, as is reported.’ . . . William Methwold stands debtor in their books for the 200 pagodas lost at the mines. . . . (<em>About</em> 4½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Consultation held aboard the</span> <em>Blessing</em> <span class="sc">by President Rastell and Messrs. James, Bangham, Heynes, and Hoare</span>, <span class="sc">December</span> 15, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. i. p. 76).</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">As it is feared that the natives may violate their agreement, and it is therefore inadvisable to land more money than is absolutely necessary, it is determined to put 32,000 rials of eight on board the <em>Blessing</em> and <em>William</em>, now bound for Persia, with orders that the money should be brought back to Surat intact. The <em>Dolphin</em> being too weak to be left in port alone, it is decided to keep back the <em>Reformation</em> until she be laden for England. In recognition of Captain Hall’s great care and diligence in the business of the seizure of the junks, it is ordered that on his return from Persia a chain of gold to the value of one hundred marks be presented to him on behalf of the Company. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Captain John Hall, aboard the</span> <em>Blessing</em> [<span class="sc">at Swally</span>], <span class="sc">to the Company, December</span> 16, 1623 (<em>O.C.</em> 1134).</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Set sail for ‘Combron’ [Gombroon] on December 6 [1622] with the <em>Blessing</em>, <em>Whale</em>, <em>Dolphin</em>, and <em>Reformation</em>, and arrived at that port on the 29th. Dispatched the <em>Whale</em> and <em>Dolphin</em> for Surat on January 8, and by the 18th the remaining ships had got on board all the goods available, viz., 800 bales of silk. When these had been embarked, ‘the Persian solicited us with many complements and faire words, and sente his cheefe noble hee had to see wheather they could worke with mee to goe for Muskatt; wheruppon I gave him faire words and tould them that I had noe such order from the Kinge of England, my lord and master.’ Sailed from Ormus on January 26. The day before he sold the small ship to the Khān, who was very desirous to have her. She had proved ‘a verey bauble’, and was nearly abandoned on the way out as unseaworthy. The price was ‘400 demaunds’ [i.e. <em>tūmāns: see</em> p. 35], equivalent to 1,330<em>l.</em> sterling, which is 1,000<em>l.</em> more than she was worth. He reached Swally on February 11, and there received orders to take <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-379" data-page="379" data-folio="340" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n378_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 340">[p. 340]</button> in provisions and water as secretly as possible and then repair to Dābhol in the <em>Blessing</em>, to be followed by the <em>Reformation</em>, which was to visit Chaul on the way. He sailed accordingly on the 23rd, and four days later anchored in Dābhol Road. Having driven off three Malabar pirate boats, ‘the people did like marvelous well of our nation, and sente us beefe and other refreshinge what wee could desyer;’ and Robert Hutchinson, ‘our merchante,’ went ashore every day for five or six days together. The <em>Reformation</em> not appearing, a consultation was called, at which it was decided to attempt the seizure of two frigates lying in front of the customhouse, which were supposed to be bound for Mokha and to be ‘verey rich’. At this moment the <em>Reformation</em> came in sight; whereupon Hall went out to her, and told her officers what had been determined. Remaining on board, he took her straight into the port and anchored ‘close abord the towne’. A boat was sent from shore to learn the intentions of the English. ‘I made answere that yt was nott unknowne unto them wherfore wee came in, for they knewe well in whate manner they had robbed us and tooke our goods from us, and that our comming was for satisfaction.’ The messenger promised to acquaint the Governor with Hall’s demands; ‘butt as soone as hee came ashore they wente to gettinge out of the frigatts the goods, and wee in the shipp to gett them of from shore; and so I caused the boatswaine to lay mee a hawser uppon the biggest of them and to cutt all there faest<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-118" href="#sec-05-fn-118" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-118" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> upon the shore and to clapp a rope upon thother (which laye both by one another); so that in lesse tyme then halfe an ouer we had them both of. Butt in the intreme they lett fly there smale shott and arrowes so thicke as wee could hardly walke upon the decke. . . . I made the gunner to give fyer to doe them all the spoyle wee could possible, and after wee had begunn there was no lookinge for nothinge butt blowes. All this while the tyde would nott serve to have the <em>Blessinge</em> come in to sucker us; butt soe soone as shee came in wee lett fly into the towne, and att the castell, almost 1,000 greatt shott, all that night and all the next day battringe the towne. And I myselfe and Richard Weodmore wente ashore; and like a company of bad-minded men, as soone as they sawe a man or to <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-380" data-page="380" data-folio="341" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n379_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 341">[p. 341]</button> [two] hurte they all runn into the watter to take boate.’ Wedmore fled with them, but as he is now dead, Hall will speak no evil of him; and indeed ‘hee was as proper a younge man as I would desyer to have in company’. ‘Wee lost ashore in that buisnes, drowned and slaine, betweene both shipps 20 men, which greeved mee to the harte.’ On March 5 the <em>Reformation</em> went aground, but after being lightened she was got off and came safely out of the harbour with the two frigates. Swally was reached on March 23, and on the last day of that month Hall sailed for Mokha. Arriving there on May 24, he found the <em>Dolphin</em> in the road. On June 12 the <em>Blessing</em> went over to Asab for wood and water. Sailed for Surat on August 7, leaving the <em>Dolphin</em> still at Mokha. In obedience to his commission he seized all the Indian junks. The first belonged to Chaul and had in her about 40,000 rials in money, besides goods; next the <em>Shāhī</em>, then the <em>Ganjāwar</em>, and then one belonging to Gōghā. On October 1 he met at night off Damān two Dutch ships and fired at them, thinking them to be Portuguese. Reached Swally on the 5th, with eight junks in all, and found there the <em>William</em>. ‘Shee had tooke of the Presidente and Counsell before, and att my cominge in over the barr they all came abord of mee; and then wee gave them to understand of our demands, which to most of them wee brought them to. The merchants which had the corrall would have lost by yt, yf our merchants would take yt, 1,500<em>l.</em> sterlinge. So the 17th of November wee had made all thinges perfitt and landed the President and Counsell againe. Our merchants are now better used then ever they were, but how longe yt will last I know not.’ The <em>Blessing</em> and <em>William</em> are bound for Gombroon, and the <em>Reformation</em> for the southwards. The two former on their return will lade for England, but if they cannot get cleared before the end of March it will be necessary to winter somewhere on this coast. (4 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Emanuel Butta, Master of the <em>Blessing</em>, at Swally to the Company, December</span> 16, 1623 (<em>O.C.</em> 1135).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Account of his voyage from England, in company with the <em>Discovery</em> and <em>Reformation</em>. Lost sight of the Lizard on April 3, 1622. Reached Table Bay June 27, and rode there till July 8. On <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-381" data-page="381" data-folio="342" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n380_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 342">[p. 342]</button> August 20 anchored at ‘Malale’ [Mohilla, one of the Comoro Islands]; there obtained water and provisions, and sailed again on the 27th. Reached Swally Road on October 3, 1622, where they found the <em>London</em>, <em>Jonas</em>, <em>Whale</em>, <em>Dolphin</em>, <em>Lion</em>, and <em>Rose</em>, besides four Dutch ships. On the 24th arrived the <em>Exchange</em>, <em>Anne</em>, and <em>Diamond</em>; the two former sailed again for Goa, with the four Dutch vessels, on November 22. Started on December 6 for Persia, in company with the <em>Whale</em>, <em>Dolphin</em>, <em>Reformation</em>, and <em>Primrose</em>, leaving behind the <em>London</em>, <em>Jonas</em>, and <em>Lion</em>, bound for England, the <em>Discovery</em>, bound for Batavia, the <em>Diamond</em> and <em>Rose</em> intended for the Fleet of Defence, and two Flemings that had arrived the day before from Batavia. Gombroon was reached on December 30, where four merchants came aboard, viz. Messrs. Darrell (who died during the ships’ stay), Barker, Benthall, and Thompson, while Mr. Rawson stayed on shore. On January 9, 1623, the <em>Whale</em> and <em>Dolphin</em> were sent away for Surat (the coast being clear of the Portuguese), the former with 150 and the latter with 100 bales of silk. The <em>Blessing</em> then took in 420 bales and the <em>Reformation</em> 150. The <em>Primrose</em> was sold to ‘the Cawne’ for 400 ‘tamaunes’, value 3<em>l.</em> 6<em>s.</em> 4<em>d.</em> each. Sailed on January 25 and arrived on February 11 at Swally, where they found the <em>Whale</em>, <em>Dolphin</em>, and <em>Rose</em>. Eight days later the <em>Rose</em> sailed to join the Fleet of Defence. The silk on board the <em>Blessing</em> transferred to the <em>Whale</em> and the <em>Dolphin</em>. On February 23 the <em>Blessing</em> and the <em>Reformation</em> started for Dābhol to capture the junks there bound for the Red Sea. The next day the <em>Reformation</em> went into Chaul, and on the 28th the <em>Blessing</em> anchored off Dābhol. The barge went ashore and returned with provisions and news that the two great junks reported at Surat were really two frigates. Five Malabar junks were taken, but subsequently released, four of them having passes from the Fleet of Defence and the fifth one from the English factors at Mokha. On March 3 a consultation was held at which it was agreed to take the ship into the port to fetch out the frigates. This was about to be attempted when the <em>Reformation</em> hove in sight, whereupon Captain Hall boarded her and took her into the harbour, followed by the <em>Blessing</em>. By means of their boats the English fetched the frigates from the customhouse. ‘Then the <em>Reformation</em> shott at the towne and the towne at them againe; <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-382" data-page="382" data-folio="343" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n381_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 343">[p. 343]</button> then we shott at them and they at us; so we continued most part of the night. And the 4th day in the morninge we beate downe there barracathoes [Port. <em>barricada</em>, a barricade] and dismounted them four peeces of ordnanc that plaide on us all night and all the rest that did offende us. And then our captaine came from the <em>Reformation</em> and woulde needes goe ashore with 90 men from both shipps to take the town, nothwithstandinge all the perswasions I coulde doo and other men to the contrary; so he went with 80 or 90 men on lande, but was gladd to runne to the boates againe so fast as they coulde, loosing 19 men outright, some slaine and some drowned for hast to gett into the boates.’ That night they weighed, with the two frigates, to get out of shot of the town. The <em>Reformation</em> ran aground, but was got off; and on the 6th they sailed for Surat. On the 12th the <em>Reformation</em> took the goods out of one of the frigates and burnt her as unserviceable; whereupon the crew of the <em>Blessing</em> refused to weigh anchor until the cargo of the other frigate was transferred to their ship. This mutiny was quelled by the captain, and on the 15th the <em>Blessing</em> went on ahead, leaving the frigate with the <em>Reformation</em>. On the 19th they met a junk, built by Portuguese but manned by Gujarātīs, on which account they dismissed her. Next day they encountered the <em>Dolphin</em> and heard of the loss of the <em>Whale</em>. They anchored near the wreck and recovered two topmasts and two great yards; and on the 24th they reached Swally. Sailed for Mokha. On March 31 met the <em>Reformation</em>, which had burnt the second frigate. Reached Socotra on April 25,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-119" href="#sec-05-fn-119" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-119" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> and Mokha on May 24. The <em>Dolphin</em> had arrived there on the 9th. From June 13 to July 1 the <em>Blessing</em> was absent, watering at Asab. On August 7 she sailed from Mokha, and got out of the Straits two days later. Chased a junk on the 12th, but lost her. A week later they captured one which was found to be ‘Court Mamotes of Chaule’; her goods and moneys were taken out and she was dismissed on September 5. On the 15th near Diu Head they saw the <em>William</em> from England and a Fleming from Holland. Took a small junk of Surat on the 16th, and next day the <em>William</em> and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-383" data-page="383" data-folio="344" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n382_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 344">[p. 344]</button> the Fleming went on to Swally. Some days later captured a Goghā junk and a Portuguese ship of Diu. The <em>Dolphin</em> joined on the 23rd, and then both vessels sailed for Damān. On the 30th took the <em>Ganjāwar</em> of Surat. Met the <em>Reformation</em> and the Portuguese prize on October 3, and heard of Mr. Wedmore’s death in Persia. Next day anchored off Swally, and captured the <em>Shāhī</em> from Jiddah. ‘The 12th of November our President and the Governoure firmed to the artickles of pease betweene us, and the 17th day they went all ashore.’ Now bound for Persia. (4½ <em>pp. Endorsed as received July 31, 1624.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Arthur Suffield, Purser of the</span> <em>BLESSING</em>, <span class="sc">at Surat Bar to the Company, December 16</span> [1623] (<em>O.C.</em> 1171).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wrote last on November 29, 1622 [<em>not extant</em>]. Seven days later the fleet sailed for Persia. The <em>Primrose</em> almost lost on the way. Arrived at ‘Combroone’ on December 30, and took in a quantity of silk. The Khān of Shīrāz tried to induce Captain Hall to join in attacking Muskat. He also bought the <em>Primrose</em> for 400 ‘tomanes’; she was valued, with her ordnance and fittings, at 700<em>l.</em> The fleet returned to Swally on February 12. The <em>Blessing</em> and <em>Reformation</em> sailed on February 23 for Dābhol. The <em>Reformation</em> went into Chaul to capture a junk, but could not, as the latter was under the guns of the castle. She then came on to Dābhol, arriving just as Captain Hall had decided to take the <em>Blessing</em> over the bar. The two now entered the port and surprised two frigates, though the natives had managed to take great part of the goods out of them. Next morning Hall and Wedmore landed with 70 or 80 men, but in the eighth part of an hour were forced to fly for their lives, losing thirteen men from the <em>Blessing</em> and seven from the <em>Reformation</em>, including Thomas Thompson, a merchant from Persia. The ships sailed with their prizes on March 7. One of the frigates, being leaky, was burnt; the other was left to be towed by the <em>Reformation</em>, while the <em>Blessing</em>, fearing to lose the monsoon for Mokha, went on ahead to Swally. Soon after, the second frigate had to be abandoned. The <em>Blessing</em> reached Swally on March 24, took in stores, &amp;c., and sailed for Mokha on the 30th, carrying the provisions, &amp;c., intended for the <em>Reformation</em>, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-384" data-page="384" data-folio="345" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n383_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 345">[p. 345]</button> which had not yet arrived. She was met five or six leagues from the Road and the stores transferred; but, being in want of fresh water, was forced after all to go into Swally. The <em>Blessing</em> arrived at Mokha on May 24, and found the <em>Dolphin</em>, which had been there about a fortnight. Kept on their guard, remembering the cruel treatment of the crew of the <em>Rose</em> by the Turks. Sailed on August 7, and lay in wait for the junks. Captured one on the 19th, and found her to be a frigate bound for Chaul. Her treasure, &amp;c., was taken out and she released on September 5. Diu Head was reached on the 16th, and the <em>William</em> and a Dutch ship were met with. Three junks were there captured; also a Portuguese vessel from Mokha, ‘fraighted with baneanes’, who fled ashore. Two of the junks belonged to Gujarāt; the third was a Malabar, laden with coco-nuts, and sank the same night owing to a leak. The <em>Dolphin</em>, with a Dābhol prize, was met on the 25th. Three days later they stood over to ‘St. Johns’. Captured the junks they were waiting for, and on October 3 anchored at Surat Bar with seven prizes. From one of these 600<em>l.</em> was stolen by some of the men. The merchants will relate the subsequent occurrences. Sends copies of the wills, &amp;c., of 32 dead men: the inventory of the <em>Primrose</em>: records of five consultations: a list of the crew: an account of the goods in the two Dābhol frigates, amounting to 30,060 mahmūdīs, and of the treasure in the Chaul frigate, 38,444½ rials of eight. The ship is now ready to sail for Persia, being partly freighted with Moors’ goods. (3 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">John Beamont</span>, <span class="sc">aboard the</span> <em>ROYAL EXCHANGE</em><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-120" href="#sec-05-fn-120" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-120" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, <span class="sc">to Henry Sill at Amboyna, December</span> 17, 1623 (<em>O.C.</em> 1136).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">. . . ‘Wee have ill tidings that [the] <em>Whalle</em>, a great ship laden with a rich cargazon from Pe[r]cia and Suratt, beinge sett saylle from the Roade of Suratt, soncke right downe in the sea, 10 leages of the Roade, 36 men drowned and nothinge saved of hir goods, by reason of a plancke that spronge in hir. As yet noe ships com from Suratt, but this newes came overland to Meslepotan.’ . . . (¾ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-385"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="385" data-folio="346" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n384_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 346">[p. 346]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Robert Hutchinson at Ahmadābād to President Rastell at Surat, December</span> 19, 1623 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 480).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">‘Our friend Leon Tinto’ desires him to look into John George’s estate, with a view to his being satisfied for a venture of about Rs. 50 entrusted to the deceased. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received December 23.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same, December</span> 19, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 480)</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Mr. Leachland left on the 12th for Cambay. Requests an early answer to their previous letter. All the indigo is in their hands; and they would be glad of a copy of the Company’s instructions for packing it. They have prevented the Dutch from getting the English house at Sarkhej (‘yett not seene in the busines ourselves’) and will do their best to disappoint them in other matters. Rastell’s letter, ordering 300 churls of indigo to be bought at Cambay for the <em>Dolphin</em>, missed Mr. Leachland but has now been forwarded to him. (<em>Copy. 1 p. Received December 23.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Robert Young, Justinian Offley, and John Goodwin at Broach to the President and Council at Surat, December</span> 20, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 479).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Arrived here safely with their goods, and visited ‘Nahore Caun’. He demanded the payment of customs, but this they refused, ‘standing upon our articles and agreement with Sife Caun;’ whereupon the question was deferred until the arrival of the latter. He crossed the river last night, and this morning they visited him and the Hakīm. Saif Khān, on hearing of the dispute, wrote an order, of which they enclose a copy; ‘the contents are that wee are not to pay custome for any goods which passeth from Surratt, nether these goods which come thether from Amadavaz, Cambayett, Brodera, or any bought in this cittie, except we shipp them from hence.’ The Hakīm has bought a small vessel at Gandevī, and, fearing the Malabars, wishes her to be escorted to Surat River by two English boats. He has written to the President, but has also asked them to second his request, which they gladly do. Request the invoices of their goods, with full instructions, and any <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-386" data-page="386" data-folio="347" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n385_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 347">[p. 347]</button> documents belonging to the court and Agra. The ‘barratts’ given by Bahādur Khān, if not already confirmed by Saif Khān, should also be sent, as the former accompanies Saif Khān to Baroda. The ‘lesker’ starts to-morrow. <em>P.S.</em>—‘Sife Caun remembers his 7,000 rials.’ (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p. Received December</em> 21.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Justinian Offley and John Goodwin at Baroda to President Rastell at Surat, December</span> 25, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 481).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Arrived here yesterday, and leave again to-morrow. It is said that Bahādur Khān has been called up by Khān-i-Azam, who is very angry with him for ‘his lavish expenses among his souldiers’. The ‘barratts’, if not confirmed by Saif Khān, should be forwarded to them to get them allowed by Khān-i-Azam. Repeat their request for invoices, &amp;c. Hope to be at Ahmadābād in four or five days, as Saif Khān has been ordered by the Prince to come with all speed. Baftas are plentiful here at low prices. <em>P.S. (by Robert Young)</em>—Has just received a letter from Mr. Leachland about the bargain for Saif Khān’s indigo at Cambay. Has seen the latter and adjusted the matter satisfactorily. Saif Khān is always reminding him about the non-payment of his money aboard the ships, saying that others have been satisfied but he has been forgotten. (<em>Copy.</em> 1¼ <em>pp. Received December</em> 28.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Hoare, aboard the</span> <em>DOLPHIN</em>, <span class="sc">to President Rastell at Surat, December</span> 29, 1623 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 482).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Search will be made for the missing bales of broadcloth. The money will be packed and sent on board the <em>Reformation</em>, as desired. The ‘Cambaya caphilae’ is now departed, but the Portuguese is still on board; advises his release. Mr. Martin’s invoice of gold will be sent to-morrow. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><section class="apparatus-zone" aria-label="Footnotes"><h2 class="apparatus-heading">Critical Apparatus &amp; Notes</h2><ol class="apparatus-list"><li id="sec-05-fn-001" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-001" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This is an overstatement. The Court had declared on Jan. 23, 1622, that ‘they have no meaning wholly to relinquish the trade, but to draw better conditions to their marchants’ (<em>Court Minutes</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-002" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-002" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly the letter noted on p. 633 (no. 639) of Mr. Sainsbury’s <em>Calendar for 1630–4</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-003" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-003" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Van Uffelen wrote to Batavia on January 15 (N. S.) that the English at Masulipatam had agreed to share the cost of presents and to pay forty rials for the house at Pulicat (<em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. vi. no. 194).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-004" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-004" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A letter from the Dutch factors at Agra, dated February 20, 1623 (N.S.) (<em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. vi. no. 197) records the arrival, six days previously, of an Englishman who had fled from the royal court. This was, no doubt, Willoughby. Probably he sent his letter by the same conveyance as the Dutch.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-005" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-005" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Formerly the English broker.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-006" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-006" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably the Port. <em>Musulmana</em>, ‘Muhammadan’ (writing), i. e. Persian or Hindūstānī.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-007" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-007" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For this attack on Agra, see Elliot and Dowson’s <em>History</em>, vol. vi. p. 385, and <em>Della Valle</em>, vol. i. p. 121. The Dutch letter mentioned on p. 195 says that the rebel forces, under Vikramājīt, Rustam Khān and others, attempted to surprise the castle but were repulsed, whereupon they plundered the city. The date is given as February 16 (N. S.).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-008" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-008" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly an error for Lālā Bīr Singh (see the letter of May 27, 1623). The story of Āsaf Khān’s imprisonment is mentioned by Della Valle (vol. i. p. 121). See also Willoughby’s letter of April 15, <em>infra</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-009" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-009" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">On the complaint of Āghā Sālih, Governor of Cambay, of the depredations committed by the <em>Weesp</em>, Āsaf Khān imprisoned Hendrick Adriaenszoon and Maerten Frederickszoon, but released them on their undertaking to pay the amount claimed, which was about Rs. 13,000. The money was accordingly remitted by the Dutch factors at Surat. The ‘Captaine’ referred to is Wouter Heuten. (<em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. vi. nos. 197, 198.)</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-010" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-010" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Cf. Della Valle</em> (Hakl. Soc.), vol. i. p. 130.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-011" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-011" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mīrzā Muhammad Yār, brother of Himmat Khān, the Governor of Broach.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-012" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-012" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably Antonie Claeszoon Vischer, who appears later among the Dutch factors at Surat.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-013" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-013" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">i. e. Pietro Della Valle, who had come from Persia in the <em>Whale</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-014" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-014" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘This Sunday night, att nine of the clocke.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-015" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-015" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">According to Faria y Sousa (<em>Asia Portuguesa</em>, vol. iii. p. 357) the <em>St. John</em>, an Indiabuilt vessel, while proceeding to Portugal met three Dutch ships, who pursued her for twenty days. Being disabled, she offered to surrender, but while treating for terms a storm sprang up and parted the vessels. The <em>St. John</em> stranded in Delagoa Bay; the crew got ashore, and after much suffering reached Mozambique.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-016" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-016" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">An error for 350 (<em>see</em> p. 225).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-017" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-017" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably Dāndi (pur), a village on the coast just below the mouth of the Purna river. From the letter it appears that Tottle and a native broker had been sent to search for goods coming ashore from the wrecked <em>Whale</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-018" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-018" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly Medhar, on the north side of the Ambika estuary.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-019" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-019" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>patel</em>, the head man of a village.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-020" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-020" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">In the original the date is given as ‘March 3d’, but this is evidently a mistake. According to Butta’s letter (Dec. 16) the two ships met on March 31.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-021" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-021" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The portion of Masulipatam actually lying on the coast is called Machli-bandar, or simply Bandar (the port).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-022" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-022" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Valentyn mentions Adolf Thomassoon as being in charge of the Dutch factories on the Coromandel Coast from 1617 to 1619.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-023" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-023" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Persian <em>pīr</em>, a saint or holy man.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-024" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-024" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably Āsaf Khān is meant.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-025" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-025" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Wouter Heuten, the Dutch Agent at Agra.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-026" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-026" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably the Dāndi(pur) mentioned on p. 211.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-027" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-027" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This rumour was untrue.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-028" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-028" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">According to the <em>Tūzak-i-Jahāngīrī</em> this was a eunuch named Vafādar.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-029" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-029" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably the younger brother of Rājā Vikramājīt, whom the latter had left in charge of Ahmadābād (see p. 162).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-030" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-030" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A Danes’?</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-031" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-031" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">On the western side of Borneo.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-032" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-032" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">A letter from Van Uffelen, dated January 15, 1623 (N.S.), states that in future the mines would not be leased but worked for the benefit of the king (<em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. vi. no. 194).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-033" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-033" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Port. <em>páo da China</em>, China-root, the tuber of various species of <em>Smilax</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-034" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-034" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Port. <em>mal Francez</em>, ‘French disease,’ i. e. syphilis, for which China-root was a usual remedy.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-035" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-035" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This looks like an error of the contemporary copyist for ‘as chouckees [<em>chaukīs</em>, or customs-guards] att Wassett’ [i. e. Vāsad, on the Mahī: see the previous volume, p. 307].</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-036" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-036" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably a mistake for ‘Futtybeague’, i. e.
