Fateh Beg, who is mentioned by Herbert as being among the Prince’s partisans. ‘Bobcaun’ may be Bābū Khān (<em>see</em> p. 240).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-037" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-037" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly Karod, on the right bank of the Narbadā, half-way between Broach and Shuklatīrth.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-038" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-038" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Some place about half-way between Baroda and Broach.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-039" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-039" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly an error for Bahādur Khān, as this name is frequently abbreviated in the manuscript.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-040" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-040" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This may be Lālā Bīr Singh, i. e. Bīr Singh Deo Bundelā of Orchhā, the murderer of Abul Fazl. Hawkins calls him ‘Lalla Bersingdia’, and Finch makes the name ‘Lala Bersing’. Lāl is still a Rājpūt title.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-041" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-041" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A slip for ‘Suffee Caun’?</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-042" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-042" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Khān-i-Azam’s own son, Mīrzā Shādmān, is meant (see p. 245).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-043" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-043" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently a slip for ‘Sadala’, as on p. 245. ‘Mīrzā Sadullah’ is probably meant. <span class="folio-turn">[p. 245]</span> good wishes (Hind. <em>duā</em>, ‘a prayer,’ ‘thanksgiving,’ &amp;c.). p. 257 as son of Khān Jahān.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-044" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-044" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Eight cash made one fanam, and sixteen fanams one pagoda.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-045" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-045" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">More probably the fifth (cf. p. 225 and above).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-046" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-046" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>kharāj</em>, strictly ‘revenue’, but used here and elsewhere in the wider sense of ‘government business’, which in India was largely concerned with revenue matters. In Turkey the term was specially applied to the poll-tax levied on Christians (<em>see Oxf. Eng. Dict.</em>, s. v. ‘Caratch’).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-047" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-047" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly ‘the headman’ (Guj. <em>kala</em>, ‘the head’).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-048" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-048" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>pahile lāthī, pīchhe bolte’</em>, i.e. ‘First the stick, and then they speak’.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-049" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-049" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>krorī</em>, ‘a rent-collector,’ properly one who collects a <em>kror</em> of dams (Rs. 250,000). This official had the general superintendence of the lands in his district, and the charge of the police.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-050" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-050" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sana; see note on p. 34 of the previous volume. In the letter referred to on p. 61 Becker narrates his second voyage to the Red Sea in January, 1623, this time in the yacht <em>Heusden</em>. They reached Mokha on March 12 (N.S.). On the seventeenth of the following <span class="folio-turn">[p. 252]</span> month the ‘Foddeli Basha’ [probably Fazlī Pāshā] arrived, and twelve days later he hanged the Governor, Alī Āghā, at the door of the custom-house, for releasing the Dutch the previous year without authority, and threatened to do the same to the Dutch factor, Willem de Milde. Finding that they could do no good, Becker and his companions sailed again on the 26th, and reached Aden on May 15, whence they departed to Gombroon and landed Hubert Vissnich on a mission to Shāh Abbās.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-051" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-051" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The pinnace <em>Richard</em> had been at Mokha the year before and had plundered a small ship belonging to Aden (see p. 61 and <em>O. C.</em> 1169), and had thus brought about the seizure of part of the crew of the <em>Rose</em>. Her master was Robert Davis.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-052" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-052" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>gharī</em>, an interval of time (about twenty-four minutes); see the previous volume, p. 147 <em>n</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-053" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-053" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See the <em>Calendar of State Papers, E. Indies, 1622–24</em>, no. 67, &amp;c.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-054" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-054" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Cf. Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. vi. no. 217.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-055" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-055" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Christopher Brown, commanding the <em>William</em>, which had just arrived from England. Evidently he had sent a present of wine to the factors at Surat.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-056" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-056" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Called later ‘Sovegee Banyan’, i.e. Sivajī Vāniya.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-057" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-057" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Dumas, a village at the mouth of the Tāptī, on the southern bank.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-058" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-058" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The modern Arajān, a village just below Surat, on the other side of the river. Valentyn calls it ‘Radie’.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-059" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-059" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably the Portuguese <em>escrivão</em>, ‘a scrivener.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-060" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-060" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly <em>kiwār</em>, a gate or entrance.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-061" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-061" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sebald Wouderaer.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-062" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-062" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mr. Framji Jamaspji Thanawala has kindly suggested (through the Rev. G. P. Taylor) that this is the Gujarātī <em>dodarū</em>, ‘weak’ or ‘yielding,’ employed here as a substantive for ‘threatening’ or ‘browbeating’ (in order to make one yield).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-063" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-063" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The modern Borbhātha, on the southern bank of the Narbadā, opposite Broach.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-064" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-064" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Prayers (from <em>but</em>, ‘an idol,’ and <em>duā</em>, ‘a prayer’).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-065" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-065" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">This physician (<em>hakīm</em>) was evidently the person of chief importance amongst those captured in the junks, and he subsequently played a prominent part in the negotiations for their release. The mention in a later document of his title of <em>Masīh-al-Zamān</em> (‘Messiah of the Age’) enables us to identify him with a Persian named Sadrā, who came to India in the reign of Akbar, and under Jahāngīr became one of the leading court physicians. It was he who restored Prince Khusrū’s sight after he had been partially blinded by his father’s orders (Elliot and Dowson’s <em>History</em>, vol. vi. p. 448). He had recently, however, fallen into disfavour, owing to his refusal to treat the Emperor during a severe illness, and had judged it expedient, therefore, to make the pilgrimage to Mecca (<em>Tūzak-i-Jahāngīrī</em>). It was in returning from this expedition that he fell into the hands of the English.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-066" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-066" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Theirs in the end?</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-067" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-067" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">i.e. asked leave to depart, in accordance with native etiquette.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-068" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-068" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably an error for <em>mubārak</em> (‘prosperous’)—a word generally employed for respectful felicitations.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-069" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-069" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See p. 272.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-070" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-070" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently Rastell had ordered the detention of the native passengers who had come from Gombroon in the <em>Reformation</em>. They were of course quite innocent of the wrongs complained of by the English, and Heynes takes their side with much warmth.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-071" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-071" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">An Anglicized form of <em>bār-band</em>, a man who ties up bales.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-072" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-072" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly Bhatha, on the right bank of the Tāptī, about three miles below Surat.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-073" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-073" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably the copyist’s error for ‘muzzraw’, i.e. Persian <em>masarrat</em>, ‘a cause of joy.’ The meaning here is ‘credit’ or ‘praise’.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-074" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-074" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">To be left ‘in the suds’ was a proverbial expression for ‘in distress’.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-075" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-075" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">At Agra.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-076" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-076" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Francisco Pelsart, who later on did go to Agra as Agent, and wrote an interesting account of the city, its trade, &amp;c., which is printed in Thevenot’s <em>Relations de divers Voyages Curieux</em>, 1696, vol. i. part 2.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-077" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-077" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Damka, a village about three miles north-east of Swally.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-078" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-078" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘From this side of Battoch River’ [apparently the Kim].</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-079" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-079" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Kosamba, a village on the road from Surat to Broach, three miles north of the Kim River.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-080" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-080" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Arabic <em>mushaf</em>, ‘a book’; especially <em>the</em> book, i.e. the Qurān.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-081" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-081" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>juma-rāt</em>, ‘Friday-eve,’ i. e. Thursday.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-082" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-082" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably the ‘two strangers’ of p. 278. ‘Bastiano’ is no doubt the Venetian merchant, Sebastiano Fiorino, mentioned in the previous volume (pp. 223, 291).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-083" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-083" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ichhapur, about half-way between Surat and Swally.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-084" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-084" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pers. <em>pīsh-khāna</em>, ‘advance-service,’ i. e. tents sent on for erection at the next stoppingplace.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-085" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-085" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Gondwānā seems to be intended; but the whole story was evidently a fabrication.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-086" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-086" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Padshāh salāmat</em> (‘Health to the Emperor’), the salutation always addressed to the reigning monarch (see <em>The Embassy of Sir T. Roe</em>, p. 321).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-087" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-087" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">i. e. the subjects of the allied Deccan kings.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-088" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-088" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pers. <em>kānūngū</em>, ‘an expounder of the law.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-089" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-089" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>Allāh kī panāh</em>, ‘God is our refuge’—an expression of resignation to the inevitable.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-090" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-090" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">These are in slightly different order from the items as given on p. 300. It will therefore be found more convenient to compare them with the particulars given on p. 283.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-091" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-091" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">As they did on October 27 (<em>see Hague Transcripts</em>, series i, vol. vi. no. 200).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-092" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-092" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This was Safī Khān’s new title—Saif Khān Jahāngīr Shāhī—bestowed upon him by the Emperor in recognition of his recent services (<em>Tūzak-i-Jahāngīrī</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-093" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-093" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Cf. <em>Della Valle</em>, pp. 214, 255, 278, &amp;c.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-094" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-094" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Mārasfand</em> is the Persian name of the twenty-ninth day of the month.  By ‘Abaun Elahee’ is meant the month Ābān in the Divine (<em>ilāhī</em>) Era instituted by Akbar.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-095" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-095" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">This should be ‘18th’, according to Jahāngīr’s reckoning in the <em>Tūzak</em> (see Elliot’s <em>History</em>, vol. vi. p. 290 <em>note</em>), but ‘19th’ is right according to the English method.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-096" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-096" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Corresponding to November 10, 1623.  Van Den Broeck gives the date of the agreement as the 13th; while Butta (<em>vide infra</em>) says that it was signed on the 12th.  See also Leachland’s and Hopkinson’s letters on pp. 317, 318, 320.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-097" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-097" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A rough summary of the English demands is given in <em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. vi. no. 200.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-098" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-098" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Rāhdārī</em>, charge for guarding a road.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-099" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-099" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably Viāra, on the Surat-Burhānpur road.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-100" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-100" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">The ‘Curka’ of Finch, ‘Corka’ of Jourdain, and ‘Kerkoa’ of Tavernier, who places it fifteen <em>kos</em> west of Navāpur. It is not found on modern maps.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-101" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-101" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Growing rice; a very early example of the word in its present form.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-102" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-102" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Coco and toddy palms.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-103" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-103" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Malay <em>sambah</em>, a salute, homage.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-104" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-104" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Customhouse. For a discussion of the derivation see <em>Hobson-Jobson</em>, 2nd ed., p. 61.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-105" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-105" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See a letter written a little later by Jan Libenaer at Masulipatam to Batavia (<em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. vi. no. 216). The writer’s ship, the <em>Wapen van Rotterdam</em>, reached Masulipatam on October 17 (N.S.), having some days before driven ashore and burnt a Portuguese vessel, after throwing overboard those of the crew who had not already escaped to land. This action was no doubt the cause of the subsequent troubles, though Libenaer angrily accuses Mills of having egged on the natives. Soon after the ship’s arrival, Harmen Prins was sent by the Dutch to Golconda with presents for the King. Halfway he met Sultān Khurram with his army of 14,000 horse, 1,500 elephants, 2,500 camels, and 36,000 (<em>sic</em>) foot. The news of the Prince’s approach caused many of the principal inhabitants to flee, and the Dutch hurried their goods on board ship. On November 8 (N.S.) the Dutch Governor (Abraham Van Uffelen) was summoned to the customhouse, but sent Libenaer in his place. The native authorities demanded to know whether the Dutch would assist in defending the town against the Prince; whereupon the Governor went himself to the customhouse, and there, in company with Mills, conferred with them on the subject. On his leaving the building, Van Uffelen was informed that the King had summoned him to Golconda, and that he must start at once. He made a show of resistance, but was beaten and pursued to a little village outside the town, where he was captured and put in irons. Libenaer, at the head of the Dutch and Danes, twenty in number, attempted to rescue him but were forced to withdraw and secure themselves in the Dutch factory. Meanwhile, the native merchants who were in the habit of dealing with them were seized and tortured, to force them to pay a ransom; while ten Dutch sailors were seized and put in irons. On the approach of the Prince the officials fled, carrying with them their prisoner, the Governor. When ‘His Majesty’ (as he is termed) arrived, the Dutch complained of the outrage to which they had been subjected and begged his assistance in recovering their chief; but the natives pleaded that the latter was already halfway to Golconda, and the Prince thereupon said that no doubt he would soon be back. Libenaer was treated with every courtesy by Khurram, and he gleefully contrasts his reception with that of Mills, who, he says, coming one day in his company to the Prince’s tent was knocked down and told to wait till he was summoned. The Prince also made overtures to the Dutch to accompany him to Bengal, offering among other things to hand over to them the castle of Surat; but Libenaer pleaded inability to do anything in the absence of his chief. On Khurram’s departure the native Governor recommenced the persecution of the Dutch and took three of them prisoners, at the same time blockading the factory once more; whereupon a Moorish ship which had just reached the port from Tenasserim was seized and held as a hostage, and it was decided to take possession of any others that might arrive. At the time of writing the Dutch Governor was still a prisoner.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-106" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-106" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">It seems to have been all false.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-107" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-107" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Dāk chaukī.</em> Cf. Hamilton’s <em>New Account</em> (vol. i. p. 149): ‘The Post in the Mogul’s Dominions goes very swift, for at every Caravanseray, which are built on the High-roads, about ten miles distant from one another, Men, very swift of Foot, are kept ready . . . And these Curriers are called <em>Dog Chouckies</em>.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-108" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-108" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Rev. G. P. Taylor suggests that this may be meant for <em>Kitāb-sī moāf rakkiye</em>, ‘Excuse (us) from the book.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-109" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-109" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>qarārī</em>, ‘firm,’ ‘stable.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-110" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-110" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly the <em>Dāroghā</em>, a term applicable to various officials, such as the head of the custom-house.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-111" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-111" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Prince Dāwar Bakhsh, son of Khusrū, is intended.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-112" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-112" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Perhaps <em>qābū-wālā</em>, ‘possessing authority,’ i.e. an authorized agent.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-113" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-113" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A <em>choultry</em> or covered platform used as a resting-place for travellers.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-114" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-114" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">A small silver Turkish coin.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-115" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-115" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Arabic <em>salām</em> (‘peace’), the well-known Muhammadan form of greeting.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-116" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-116" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Only the portions relating to Indian topics are here abstracted. There is a copy of this letter among the <em>O.C. Duplicates</em>, and an incomplete one among the <em>Triplicates</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-117" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-117" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">She reached Batavia from the Coast on November 23 (<em>Marine Records</em>, vol. xxxvi. p. 25).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-118" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-118" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A ‘fest’ or ‘fast’ is in nautical parlance a rope by which a ship is fastened to a wharf.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-119" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-119" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">It is probably to this visit that we owe the letter from the King of Socotra to King James I calendared by Mr. Sainsbury at p. 118 of his 1622–4 volume. It is dated 6 Rajab, A. H. 1032, corresponding to April 26, 1623.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-120" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-120" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Homeward bound from Batavia, having left that port two days before.</span></li></ol></section>
